Fudan lectures on 2d conformal field theory
Satoshi Nawata, Runkai Tao, Daisuke Yokoyama

Abstract
These are lecture notes on 2d conformal field theory at Fudan University.



1
Introduction

Conformal field theories play a central role in the study of quantum field theory due to their profound relevance in multiple areas of modern theoretical physics: quantum field theories (QFTs) at an infrared fixed point, two-dimensional critical phenomena, and string theory. Moreover, two-dimensional conformal field theories (2d CFTs) are grounded on a mathematically rigorous foundation, facilitating new developments in mathematics. Remarkably, these trends continue to advance, underscoring the lasting impact of CFTs on both physics and mathematics.

In this course, we will focus on conformal field theories of
two dimensions
. However, the subject is so rich that we can only cover the basics. To begin with, let us begin by introducing the concept of conformal field theories in the context of physics.

1.1
Renormalization group flow and Wilson-Fisher fixed points

In quantum field theory with a Lagrangian description, a partition function is conceptually defined by using Feynman path integral. The basic integration variables are the Fourier modes
\phi_{k}
of a field
\phi
. With a cutoff
\Lambda
, we can schematically express it as

\mathcal{Z}=\prod_{|k|<\Lambda}\int\mathcal{D}\phi_{k}\exp\left[-\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}[\phi_{k}]\right]~.

To relate the coupling constants in a theory with an energy cutoff
\Lambda
to those in a theory with a lower energy cutoff
\Lambda^{\prime}<\Lambda
, we change the variable
\phi\rightarrow\phi+\phi^{\prime}
, where
\phi^{\prime}
only has non-trivial Fourier components in
\Lambda^{\prime}<|k|<\Lambda
and
\phi
only has non-trivial Fourier components in
|k|<\Lambda^{\prime}
. After integrating out the field
\phi^{\prime}
, we obtain a new action in terms of
\phi

\exp\left(-\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}[\phi]\right)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\ \int_{\Lambda^{\prime}\leq|k|\leq\Lambda}\mathcal{D}\phi\exp\left[-\mathcal{S}_{\Lambda}[\phi]\right].

Integrating out the high-energy quantum modes results in changes to the coupling constants in the action, leading to a new, effective action for our low-energy theory. Repeatedly integrating out thin shells in momentum space will lead to a continuous trajectory through the space of couplings: this is
renormalization group flow
.

By employing naive dimensional analysis, we find that the possible parameter space becomes finite-dimensional. Although one can consider infinitely many interaction terms:

\mathcal{S}_{int}=\int d^{d}x\sum g_{i}\mathcal{O}_{i}(\phi)~,

the terms
\mathcal{O}_{i}
with
d<\Delta^{i}
are suppressed at low energy, which are called
irrelevant
. Therefore, we just need to consider finitely many terms
\mathcal{O}_{i}
with
d\geq\Delta^{i}
at low energy. To understand the behavior of a theory for these terms, we analyze the beta function
\beta(g)
, which describes how the coupling parameter
g
changes with respect to the energy scale
\Lambda

\beta(g)=\frac{\partial g}{\partial\log(\Lambda)}=\Lambda\frac{\partial g}{\partial\Lambda}.

(1.1)

The non-trivial beta function will result in
the breaking of conformal invariance quantum mechanically. For example, the one-loop
\beta
function for the
\phi^{4}
theory in
d=4
dimensions is

\beta(\lambda)=\frac{3}{16\pi^{2}}\lambda^{2}.

Since it is positive, the coupling increases with the energy scale. One the other hand, the one-loop
\beta
-function of QCD is

\beta(g)=-\frac{9g^{3}}{16\pi^{2}}.

This beta-function shows the coupling constant decreases as we increase the energy. Such theory is
asymptotic free
. The above two examples show how the theory depends on the
length scale through quantum effects. However, if
\beta=0
, the theory is scale-invariant,
i.e.
the couplings are fixed under RG flow. The coupling constant
g^{*}
with
\beta(g^{*})=0
is called
Wilson-Fisher fixed point
, and the theory at the fixed point is believed to have conformal symmetry. Therefore, conformal field theories appear at special points in the parameter spaces of quantum field theories, and they play an important role in understanding families of quantum field theories.

Figure 1:
Conformal field theory at infra-red fixed point
\beta(g^{*})=0
and illustration of spectrum of states in quantum field theory

As we will see below, conformal field theories have more enhanced symmetries than general quantum field theories. Moreover, 2d CFTs are special because conformal symmetry becomes infinite-dimensional. In this lecture, we will study 2d CFTs by making full use of an infinite-dimensional symmetry. Nonetheless, even in higher dimensions, conformal symmetries are incredibly powerful, offering profound insights into QFTs. Furthermore, if a CFT is endowed with sufficient supersymmetry, it becomes tractable, so recent studies have revealed aspects of QFTs lacking a Lagrangian description.

1.2
Critical phenomena

Let us recall the basics of statistical mechanics. The partition function is defined by

\mathcal{Z}=e^{-F/T}=\sum_{i}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathcal{E}_{i}}{T}\right)~,

where
F
is called the free energy, and we set the Boltzmann constant
k_{B}=1
. Then, the probability
P_{i}
that the system is in a state with energy
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is given by the Boltzmann distribution

P_{i}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\exp(-\frac{\cE_i}{T})~.

Therefore, the expectation value of an operator
\mathcal{O}
is given by

\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\sum_{i}\mathcal{O}_{i}~e^{-\mathcal{E}_{i}/T}

For instance, the average energy is

E=\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle=-T^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial T}\left(\frac{F}{T}\right)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\sum_{i}\mathcal{E}_{i}~e^{-\mathcal{E}_{i}/T}

Now we consider the
Ising model
of a
d
-dimensional lattice
\Lambda
with
N
lattice sites. For each lattice site
k\in\Lambda
, there is a discrete variable
\sigma_{k}\in\{+1,-1\}
, representing the site’s spin configuration.
The Hamiltonian of the Ising model is given by

\mathcal{H}=-J\sum_{\left\langle i,i^{\prime}\right\rangle}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i^{\prime}}-B\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}

(1.2)

where
B
is the external magnetic field. If
J>0
, the alignment of spins is energetically favored and the configuration is called
ferromagnetic
. Conversely, for
J<0
, the system is in an
antiferromagnetic
phase. In the following discussion, we focus on the case
J>0
.

At low temperatures (
T
), the system settles into one of two ground states, leading to a jump discontinuity in the magnetization
M
across the line of
first-order
transitions at
B=0
. A first-order phase transition is characterized by a discontinuity in some first derivative of the free energy
F
. In this example, the average energy
E
is discontinuous.

At high temperatures, thermal fluctuations dominate, and the spins are randomly oriented, resulting in a
paramagnetic
state. A phase transition occurs at the critical temperature
T=T_{c}
, known as the
Curie temperature
. The magnetization, which serves as the
order parameter
of the Ising model, is discontinuous across this phase transition.

To describe this critical point, we need to introduce a notion of correlation functions. A two-point correlation function is defined as

G(r)=\left\langle\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\right\rangle-\left\langle\sigma_{i}\right\rangle\left\langle\sigma_{j}\right\rangle\approx r^{-\tau}\mathrm{e}^{-r/\xi}

where
r=|i-j|
is the distance between the sites
i
and
j
. In the 2d Ising model, one has
\tau=1/2
for
T>T_{c}
and
\tau=2
for
T<T_{c}
.
For
r<\xi
, the spins are correlated, indicating a high probability of having the same value.
On the other hand, for sufficiently large
r\gg\xi
, the probability decreases exponentially with
r
. Hence, the mean cluster size of correlated spins is
\xi
, which is called the
correlation length
. As a temperature
T
approaches
T_{c}
, the correlation length
\xi
diverges, and such a critical point is called
second-order
. At the critical point, fluctuations on all length scales become important, resulting in a fractal-like spin configuration (Figure
3
)
1
1

1

Thanks to Yuji Tachikawa for the permission of figure use.
. Consequently, the corresponding mass scale
1/\xi
disappears, and physics is described by conformal field theory at the critical point
[
Car84a
, ISZ88-No.2]
. (See Youtube video
[
Thu12
]
.)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\includegraphics[width]{picture/hi}&\includegraphics[width]{picture/critical}&\includegraphics[width]{picture/low}\\
\text{High temperature}&\text{Curie temperature}&\text{Low temperature}\end{array}

Figure 2:
Temperature dependence of Ising model.

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\includegraphics[width]{picture/spin1024framed}&{\raisebox{56.9055pt}{$\to$}}&\includegraphics[width]{picture/spin512framed}&{\raisebox{56.9055pt}{$\to$}}&\includegraphics[width]{picture/spin256}\end{array}

Figure 3:
At the critical temperature, zooming into the red squares, the pattern does not change so much. Figures are taken from
[
Tac11
]
.

Near the critical point, the correlation lengths and the thermodynamic observables obey power laws, and the behavior is characterized by the values of the
critical exponents
.
Introducing the reduced temperature
t
and the reduced magnetic field
h

t:=\frac{T-T_{c}}{T_{c}}\quad\text{and}\quad h:=\frac{B}{T_{c}}~,

(1.3)

the conventional critical exponents
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta,\nu,\eta
are now defined by

\begin{matrix}C\sim t^{-\alpha}&(h=0)&\textrm{specific heat}\\
M\sim(-t)^{\beta}&(t<0,h=0)&\textrm{spontaneous magnetization}\\
\chi\sim t^{-\gamma}&(h=0)&\textrm{zero field susceptibility}\\
M=h^{1/\delta}&(t=0)&\textrm{magnetization}\\
\xi\sim t^{-\nu}&(h=0)&\textrm{correlation length}\\
G(r)\sim|r|^{2-d-\eta}&(t=0,h=0)&\textrm{2pt-function}\end{matrix}

(1.4)

where

C:=-\frac{T}{N}\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial T^{2}},\quad M:=-\frac{1}{N}\frac{\partial F}{\partial B}\quad,\quad\chi:=\frac{\partial M}{\partial B}~.

In fact, there are relationships among these critical exponents, leaving only two independent exponents,
\nu
and
\eta
. For the 2d Ising model, the exponents are

\alpha=0~,\quad\beta=\frac{1}{8}~,\quad\gamma=\frac{7}{4}~,\quad\delta=15~,\quad\nu=1~,\quad\eta=\frac{1}{4}

Remarkably, these critical exponents remain unchanged even if the lattice shape is altered or next-nearest-neighbor interactions are included. This independence from microscopic details is known as
universality
.

At the critical point, the two-point function can be written as

\langle\sigma(x)\sigma(0)\rangle=\frac{C}{|x|^{2\Delta_{\sigma}}}

where
\Delta_{\sigma}
is called the
scaling dimension
of
\sigma
.
One can similarly define a local energy density operator

\varepsilon_{i}=\sum_{i^{\prime}}J\left(i,i^{\prime}\right)\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i^{\prime}}~.

Then, in the 2d Ising model, their scaling dimensions are

\Delta_{\sigma}=\frac{1}{8}~,\qquad\Delta_{\epsilon}=1~,

(1.5)

which we will see in the subsequent lectures.

1.3
String theory

Conformal field theories (CFTs) play a central role in string theory, serving as the mathematical foundation that underpins the formulation and analysis of string dynamics
[
GSW87
,
Pol98
]
. In string theory, the propagation of strings on a manifold
M
is described by a nonlinear sigma model
\Sigma\to M
, where
\Sigma
is a two-dimensional Riemann surface representing the string worldsheet, and
M
is the target space manifold. For instance, in bosonic string theory, the action is expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}x\left(\sqrt{h}h^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}(X)+i\varepsilon^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X^{\nu}B_{\mu\nu}(X)+\alpha^{\prime}\sqrt{h}R^{(2)}\Phi(X)\right)\ .

where
h
is the metric on the worldsheet
\Sigma
,
G_{\mu\nu}(X)
is the metric on the target space
M
,
B_{\mu\nu}(X)
is an antisymmetric B-field, and
\Phi(X)
is the dilaton field. To describe a consistent string theory, the model must satisfy conformal invariance at the quantum level. Specifically, the dimensionality of
M
is constrained to be 26 for bosonic string theory and 10 for superstring theories with supersymmetry. Furthermore, maintaining conformal invariance at the quantum level requires the vanishing of the one-loop renormalization group beta function, which implies that the target manifold
M
must be Ricci-flat.

In particular, Calabi-Yau sigma models are described by
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal field theories. It was discovered that
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal field theories associated with pairs of Calabi-Yau manifolds are equivalent, a phenomenon known in mathematics as
mirror symmetry
. This equivalence indicates that two Calabi-Yau manifolds, although topologically distinct, yield the same physical theory, leading to significant advances in both mathematics and theoretical physics.

Moreover, the relationship between string theory on anti-de Sitter (AdS) spaces and conformal field theories led to the proposal of the
AdS/CFT correspondence
. This duality posits that a string theory defined on an AdS space is equivalent to a conformal field theory on the boundary of that space. For instance, the correspondence suggests that the non-perturbative formulation of type IIB superstring theory on AdS
{}_{5}\times S^{5}
is captured by the four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=4
superconformal field theory. The AdS/CFT correspondence has been extensively studied over the past decades, significantly impacting various fields of physics, including quantum gravity, particle physics, and condensed matter theory.

References

There is a vast array of literature on 2d conformal field theories (CFTs), with each resource focusing on specific aspects of this expansive subject. The classical lecture notes include
[
Car88
,
Gin88a
,
ZZ89
]
. Additionally, the well-known ”yellow book”
[
FMS97
]
serves as an invaluable reference, effectively functioning as a comprehensive dictionary for CFT topics. This lecture note is primarily based on these foundational works, with Sections
2
and
3
serving as concise summaries of the corresponding parts in
[
FMS97
]
.

In compiling this note, we have also included several standard references relevant to various related fields. For statistical mechanics, see
[
Car96
]
and
[
Hen13
]
. String theory is covered in
[
BP09
]
,
[
Sch96
]
, and
[
Ket95
]
. For insights into integrable systems, consider
[
GRAS05
]
and
[
Mus10
]
.

In the realm of mathematics, the representation-theoretic aspects of 2d CFTs have been extensively explored, notably by Kac
[
Kac94
]
. The mathematical foundation for 2d CFTs was established in
[
Seg88
,
TUY89
]
(also included in
[
JMT89
]
), leading to significant insights into various modern mathematical fields. These areas include vertex operator algebras, moduli spaces, low-dimensional topology, and geometric representation theory. Key references in these domains include
[
FLM89
,
Koh02
,
FBZ04
,
BD04
]
.

For those interested in conformal field theories in higher dimensions, the following references provide comprehensive insights:
[
Nak15
,
Qua15
,
Ryc16
,
SD17
]
.

Upon completion of this course, it is highly recommended that readers engage with some of the seminal papers collected in
[
ISZ88
]
. These papers are frequently referenced throughout this lecture note. For related yet more mathematically oriented papers, see the collections in
[
JMT88
]
and
[
JMT89
]
.

Even in this fast-moving community, the importance of reading classical works cannot be overemphasized, as they provide the foundational insights and methodologies that continue to influence contemporary research.

Nevertheless, we would be delighted if this note becomes a concise introduction to the huge topic for students.

2
Conformal invariance

This section studies the concept of conformal symmetry in detail. For additional information and in-depth explanations, we recommend consulting
[
FMS97
, §4]
,
[
Gin88a
, §1, §2]
, and
[
BP09
, §2]
.

2.1
Conformal transformation

Roughly speaking, a conformal transformation is a mapping between two spaces that preserves angles but not necessarily lengths. Formally, consider a differentiable map
\phi:U\rightarrow V
, where
U
and
V
are open subsets of manifolds
M
and
M^{\prime}
respectively. Let
g
and
g^{\prime}
denote the metric tensors of
M
and
M^{\prime}
. In differential geometry, we can pull back the metric
g^{\prime}
using the map
\phi
. A transformation is said to be conformal if this pullback metric
\phi^{*}g^{\prime}
satisfies

\phi^{*}g^{\prime}=\Lambda g\,.

(2.1)

If we denote
x^{\prime}=\phi(x)
for
x\in U
, this condition can be expressed in a covariant form as

g^{\prime}_{\rho\sigma}(x^{\prime})\partialderivative{x'^{\rho}}{x^\mu}\partialderivative{x'^\sigma}{x^\nu}=\Lambda(x)g_{\mu\nu}(x)\,,

(2.2)

where
\Lambda(x)
ensures that angles between vectors are preserved under the transformation. Notably, in one dimension, every smooth transformation is inherently conformal due to the nature of scalar metrics.

Additionally, from (
2.2
), we can derive that the inner product of two arbitrary vectors
V^{\prime\mu}_{1}(x^{\prime})
and
V^{\prime\mu}_{2}(x^{\prime})
in
M^{\prime}
scales under a conformal map:

g_{\mu\nu}(x)V_{1}^{\mu}(x)V_{2}^{\nu}(x)=g^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}(x^{\prime})V^{\prime\mu}_{1}(x^{\prime})V^{\prime\nu}_{2}(x^{\prime})\Lambda\,,

(2.3)

where
V^{\mu}_{1}(x)
and
V^{\mu}_{2}(x)
are the pullback vectors. This equation demonstrates that the angle between two vectors in
M^{\prime}
remains unchanged when mapped to
M
via a conformal transformation.

In this lecture, we focus on the case where
M^{\prime}=M
, implying
g^{\prime}=g
. We will consider
M
as a flat space with a constant metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}=\text{diag}(-1,+1,\dots,+1)
. Thus, the condition for a conformal transformation simplifies to

\eta_{\rho\sigma}\partialderivative{x'^\rho}{x^\mu}\partialderivative{x'^\sigma}{x^\nu}=\Lambda(x)\eta_{\mu\nu}\,.

(2.4)

Under a conformal transformation, the line element transforms as follows

\differential s^{2}=\eta_{\mu\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}\rightarrow\eta_{\rho\sigma}\differential x^{\prime\rho}\differential x^{\prime\sigma}=\eta_{\rho\sigma}\partialderivative{x'^\rho}{x^\mu}\partialderivative{x'^\sigma}{x^\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}=\Lambda(x)\differential s^{2}\,.

(2.5)

Two remarks should be stated here. The composition of two conformal transformations is also conformal, suggesting that conformal transformations form a group. Secondly, when
\Lambda(x)=1
, the conformal transformations reduce to the Poincaré group transformations, which include translations, rotations, and Lorentz boosts, preserving both angles and lengths.

2.2
Conditions for conformal invariance

In this part, we need to figure out some basic conformal transformations in flat space. To begin with, we study the infinitesimal transformations

x^{\prime\rho}=x^{\rho}+\epsilon^{\rho}(x)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\,,

(2.6)

with an infinitesimal variable

\epsilon(x)\ll 1
.
Plugging into the conformal condition
(
2.4
),
up to first order in
\epsilon
, the infinitesimal form of conformal transformation may be derived as:

\partial_{\mu}\epsilon_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\epsilon_{\mu}=K(x)\eta_{\mu\nu}\,,

(2.7)

with
K(x)=\Lambda(x)-1
, some infinitesimal function. However, since we want to find out the explicit form for
\epsilon(x)
,
K(x)
needs to be cancelled in the constraint formula. By tracing both sides of (
2.7
), we have

K(x)=\frac{2\partial^{\mu}\epsilon_{\mu}}{d}\,.

(2.8)

Plugging back to (
2.7
), we find the restriction depending on
\epsilon(x)
to make the transformation conformal:

\partial_{\mu}\epsilon_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\epsilon_{\mu}=\frac{2}{d}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)\eta_{\mu\nu}\,.

(2.9)

Let us derive two useful equations for later purposes. First of all, by taking
\partial^{\nu}
to both sides of (
2.9
), we can obtain

\partial_{\mu}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)+\square\epsilon_{\mu}=\frac{2}{d}\partial_{\mu}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)\,.

(2.10)

Therefore, only when
d=2
, we will have

\square\epsilon_{\mu}=0~,

(2.11)

which makes
d=2
conformal transformations special.
We then take
\partial_{\nu}
to both sides:

\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)+\square\partial_{\nu}\epsilon_{\mu}=\frac{2}{d}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)\,.

(2.12)

Interchanging
\mu\leftrightarrow\nu
, adding back to (
2.12
) and using (
2.9
), we get

(\eta_{\mu\nu}\square+(d-2)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu})(\partial\cdot\epsilon)=0\,.

(2.13)

Finally, tracing spacetime indices gives

(d-1)\square(\partial\cdot\epsilon)=0\,.

(2.14)

The second useful expression is obtained by taking
\partial_{\rho}
of (
2.9
) and permuting indices:

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\mu}\epsilon_{\nu}+\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\nu}\epsilon_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{d}\eta_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\epsilon_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\epsilon_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{d}\eta_{\rho\mu}\partial_{\nu}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\epsilon_{\rho}+\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\mu}\epsilon_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{d}\eta_{\nu\rho}\partial_{\mu}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)\,.

Subtracting the first line from the sum of the last two leads to

2\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\epsilon_{\rho}=\frac{2}{d}(-\eta_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}+\eta_{\rho\mu}\partial_{\nu}+\eta_{\nu\rho}\partial_{\mu})(\partial\cdot\epsilon)\,.

(2.15)

2.3
Conformal transformation in
d\geq 3

For the case
d=1
, the relation (
2.14
) imposes no constraint to
\epsilon(x)
, which accords with what we have claimed, that any smooth transformation in one dimension is conformal. The case
d=2
will be studied later in detail. In this subsection, let us focus on the case
d\geq 3
.

According to (
2.14
) for
d\geq 3
, we have
\square(\partial\cdot\epsilon)=0
. Substituting back to condition (
2.13
) gives
\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)=0
,implying that
(\partial\cdot\epsilon)
is at most linear in
x^{\mu}
, i.e.
(\partial\cdot\epsilon)=A+B_{\mu}x^{\mu}
.When plugging this linear expression back to (
2.15
), we immediately obtain that
\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\epsilon_{\rho}
equals to a constant. This follows that
\epsilon_{\mu}
is at most quadratic in
x^{\nu}
so that we can make the ansatz:

\epsilon_{\mu}=a_{\mu}+b_{\mu\nu}x^{\nu}+c_{\mu\nu\rho}x^{\nu}x^{\rho}\,,

(2.16)

where
c_{\mu\nu\rho}=c_{\mu\rho\nu}
. For the infinitesimal nature of
\epsilon_{\mu}
, all the constants:
a_{\mu},b_{\mu\nu},c_{\mu\rho\nu}\ll 1
.

Noticing that all the terms in (
2.9
) have only one derivative and that the conformal constraints for these constants should be independent of
x^{\mu}
, we can study the various terms in (
2.16
) separately. The constant term
a_{\mu}
in (
2.16
) is not constrained by (
2.9
). It describes infinitesimal translation
x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+a^{\mu}
, for which the generator is the momentum operator
P_{\mu}=-i\partial_{\mu}

2
2

2

All the generators of this form can be calculated by definition:

iG_{a}\Phi=\frac{\delta x^{\mu}}{\delta\omega_{a}}\partial_{\mu}\Phi\,,

(2.17)

if we suppose that the fields are not affected by the transformation with
\omega_{a}
the corresponding parameter.
. Inserting the linear term into (
2.9
) , we find

b_{\mu\nu}+b_{\nu\mu}=\frac{2}{d}(\eta^{\rho\sigma}b_{\sigma\rho})\eta_{\mu\nu}\,.

(2.18)

We then split
b_{\mu\nu}
into symmetric and antisymmetric parts, i.e.
b_{\mu\nu}=n_{\mu\nu}+m_{\mu\nu}
, with
n_{\mu\nu}=n_{\nu\mu}
and
m_{\mu\nu}=-m_{\nu\mu}
. The antisymmetric part
m_{\mu\nu}
satisfies (
2.18
) automatically, while the symmetric part gives constraint:
n_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{d}(\eta^{\rho\sigma}n_{\sigma\rho})\eta_{\mu\nu}
, telling that the symmetric part should be diagonal. Therefore, we can split
b_{\mu\nu}
as

b_{\mu\nu}=\alpha\eta_{\mu\nu}+m_{\mu\nu}\,.

(2.19)

The symmetric term
\alpha\eta_{\mu\nu}
describes infinitesimal scale transformation
x^{\prime\mu}=(1+\alpha)x^{\mu}
with generator
D=-ix^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
. The antisymmetric part
m_{\mu\nu}
corresponds to infinitesimal rotations
x^{\prime\mu}=(\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}+{m^{\mu}}_{\nu})x^{\nu}
with generator being the angular momentum operator
L_{\mu\nu}=i(x_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}-x_{\nu}\partial_{\mu})
.

Now let us consider the quadratic term. By inserting the quadratic term into (
2.15
), after straight-forward calculation, we find that

c_{\mu\nu\rho}=\eta_{\rho\mu}f_{\nu}+\eta_{\mu\nu}f_{\rho}-\eta_{\nu\rho}f_{\mu}\,,

(2.20)

with
f_{\mu}=\frac{1}{d}{c^{\rho}}_{\rho\mu}
. The resulting transformations are

x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+2(x\cdot f)x^{\mu}-(x\cdot x)f^{\mu}\,,

(2.21)

which are called
special conformal transformations
(SCT) and the corresponding generator is
K_{\mu}=-i(2x_{\mu}x^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}-(x\cdot x)\partial_{\mu})
.

2.4
Finite conformal transformation

So far, we have identified all basic infinitesimal conformal transformations and their generators. However, in order to determine the conformal group, we also need the corresponding finite transformations. All the basic finite conformal transformations are shown in Table
1
.

Transformations

Generators

translation

x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+a^{\mu}

P_{\mu}=-i\partial_{\mu}

dilatation

x^{\prime\mu}=\alpha x^{\mu}

D=-ix^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}

rotation

x^{\prime\mu}={M^{\mu}}_{\nu}x^{\nu}

L_{\mu\nu}=i(x_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}-x_{\nu}\partial_{\mu})

SCT

x^{\prime\mu}=\frac{x^{\mu}-(x\cdot x)f^{\mu}}{1-2(f\cdot x)+(f\cdot f)(x\cdot x)}

K_{\mu}=-i(2x_{\mu}x^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}-(x\cdot x)\partial_{\mu})

Table 1:
Finite conformal transformations and corresponding generators

The finite transformations for translation and rotation are Poincare transformations we are familiar with. The finite transformation for dilatation can be derived from its infinitesimal form easily. Thus, what we focus on is the finite transformation for SCT. In fact, we can guess this finite form of transformation if we realize that an inversion
x^{\prime\mu}=\frac{x^{\mu}}{x\cdot x}
with respect to the unit sphere is also a conformal map. However, it has neither infinitesimal form nor parameters. Using the transitive property for a conformal map, we can thus first make an inversion of
x^{\mu}
, then translate by
f^{\mu}
, and inverse the result again to construct a new conformal transformation. An illustration in two dimensions is shown in Figure
4
.

Figure 4:
Illustration of a finite Special Conformal Transformation

x^{\prime\mu}=\frac{\frac{x^{\mu}}{x\cdot x}-f^{\mu}}{(\frac{x^{\mu}}{x\cdot x}-f^{\mu})(\frac{x^{\mu}}{x\cdot x}-f^{\mu})}=\frac{x^{\mu}-(x\cdot x)f^{\mu}}{1-2(f\cdot x)+(f\cdot f)(x\cdot x)}\,.

(2.22)

It is easy to verify that when
f^{\mu}\rightarrow 0
, (
2.22
) reduces to the infinitesimal form of SCT (
2.21
). Furthermore, by using (
2.4
), the conformal factor for SCT can be computed as

\Lambda(x)=(1-2(f\cdot x)+(f\cdot f)(x\cdot x))^{-2}

(2.23)

Finally, we observe from (
2.22
) that a point
x^{\mu}=\frac{1}{f\cdot f}f^{\mu}
is mapped to infinity. Therefore, in order to define the arbitrary conformal transformation, the point at infinity should be included.

2.5
Conformal groups and algebras

The conformal transformations form a group, called the conformal group of dimension
d\geq 3
. Actually, the algebra generated by infinitesimal conformal transformations is the Lie algebra of the conformal group, called the conformal algebra.

Let us identify the conformal algebra of dimension
d\geq 3
. The dimension of the algebra is
N=d+1+\frac{d(d-1)}{2}+d=\frac{(d+2)(d+1)}{2}
by counting the generators in Table
1
. The commutation rules with no central charge can be obtained by direct calculations, and non-trivial commutation relations are

\displaystyle\commutator{D}{P_\mu}

\displaystyle=iP_{\mu}~,

(2.24)

\displaystyle\commutator{D}{K_\mu}

\displaystyle=-iK_{\mu}~,

\displaystyle\commutator{K_\mu}{P_\nu}

\displaystyle=2i(\eta_{\mu\nu}D-L_{\mu\nu})~,

\displaystyle\commutator{K_\rho}{L_{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=i(\eta_{\rho\mu}K_{\nu}-\eta_{\rho\nu}K_{\mu})\,,

\displaystyle\commutator{P_\rho}{L_{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=i(\eta_{\rho\mu}P_{\nu}-\eta_{\rho\nu}P_{\mu})~,

\displaystyle\commutator{L_{\mu\nu}}{L_{\rho\sigma}}

\displaystyle=i(\eta_{\rho\nu}L_{\mu\sigma}+\eta_{\mu\sigma}L_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\rho\mu}L_{\nu\sigma}-\eta_{\nu\sigma}L_{\mu\rho})\,.

The others commute. To put the above commutation relations into a simpler form, we define the following generators.

\displaystyle J_{\ \mu,\nu}=L_{\mu,\nu}\,,

\displaystyle J_{-1,0}=D\,,

\displaystyle J_{-1,\mu}=\frac{1}{2}(P_{\mu}-K_{\mu})\,,

\displaystyle J_{\ 0,\mu}=\frac{1}{2}(P_{\mu}+K_{\mu})\,.

By using (
2.24
), we can verify that
J_{m,n}
with
m,n=-1,0,1,\dots d
, satisfy the following commutation relations:

\commutator{J_{mn}}{J_{rs}}=i(\eta_{ms}J_{nr}+\eta_{nr}J_{ms}-\eta_{mr}J_{ns}-\eta_{ns}J_{mr})\,.

(2.25)

For Euclidean
d
-dimensional space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we see that the metric
\eta_{mn}
here is
\eta_{mn}=

\text{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)
so that we identify (
2.25
) with the commutation relations of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(1,d+1)
. We can generalize the result to the space
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
, with
d=p+q\geq 3
. The corresponding conformal group is
SO(p+1,q+1)
.

2.6
2d conformal transformation

In this section, we will focus on the special case of two dimensions with the Euclidean metric. From (
2.9
), the infinitesimal condition for conformal transformation in two dimensions reads as follows:

\partial_{1}\epsilon_{1}=\partial_{2}\epsilon_{2}\,,\qquad\partial_{1}\epsilon_{2}=-\partial_{2}\epsilon_{1}\,.

(2.26)

These are indeed the Cauchy-Riemann equations in complex analysis. Thus, if we introduce complex coordinates

\displaystyle z=x^{1}+ix^{2}\,,\qquad\epsilon=\epsilon^{1}+i\epsilon^{2}\,,

(2.27)

\displaystyle\overline{z}=x^{1}-ix^{2}\,,\qquad\overline{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{1}-i\epsilon^{2}\,,

(
2.26
) tells us that
\epsilon(z)
is a holomorphic function (in some open set). Equivalently any infinitesimal holomorphic transformation
z^{\prime}=z+\epsilon(z)
gives rise to a two-dimensional conformal transformation.
This can be seen from (
2.11
) because the Laplacian is
\partial\overline{\partial}\epsilon_{\mu}=0
in the complex coordinates.

In fact, any holomorphic function on the complex plane is conformal (i.e, preserves angles). To prove this, we should rewrite the definition of a conformal transformation (
2.4
) with complex variables. Let us first, rewrite the line element:

ds^{2}=(\differential x^{1})^{2}+(\differential x^{2})^{2}=\differential z\differential\overline{z}\,.

(2.28)

Therefore, in terms of complex coordinates, the metric can be written as

g_{\alpha\beta}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & \frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2} & 0 \endlx@physics@matrix)\,,\qquad g^{\alpha\beta}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & 2 \\
2 & 0 \endlx@physics@matrix)\,.

(2.29)

The condition (
2.4
) now becomes:

g_{\alpha\beta}\partialderivative{z'^\alpha}{z^\gamma}\partialderivative{z'^\beta}{z^\delta}=\Lambda(z,\overline{z})g_{\gamma\delta}\,,

(2.30)

which is satisfied, if
z^{\prime}=f(z)
is a holomorphic function. The conformal factor is then
\Lambda(z,\overline{z})=\absolutevalue{\pdv{f(z)}{z}}^{2}
. Figure
5
shows some examples on conformal transformations. We can see that an angle at any point is preserved when
z^{\prime}=f(z)
is holomorphic.

(a)

z

(b)

z^{\prime}=z^{2}

(c)

z^{\prime}=1/z

(d)

z^{\prime}=z\absolutevalue{z}

Figure 5:
Coordinate transformation: The transformation from square lattice (a), onto the lattice in (b) and (c) is conformal, while the transformation onto the lattice in (d) is not.

Witt algebra

As we have seen above, for an infinitesimal conformal transformation in two dimensions,
\epsilon(z)
has to be holomorphic in some open set. We can perform a Laurent expansion of
\epsilon(z)
locally around
z=0
. A general infinitesimal conformal transformation thus can be written as

\displaystyle z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=z+\epsilon(z)=z+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\epsilon_{n}(-z^{n+1})\,,

\displaystyle\overline{z}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=z+\overline{\epsilon}(\overline{z})=\overline{z}+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\overline{\epsilon}_{n}(-\overline{z}^{n+1})\,,

where
\epsilon_{n}
and
\overline{\epsilon}_{n}
are infinitesimal parameters. Supposing a scalar and dimensionless field
\phi(z,\overline{z})
living on the plane, the effect of such a mapping would be:

\phi(z,\overline{z})\rightarrow\phi^{\prime}(z^{\prime},\overline{z}^{\prime})=\phi(z,\overline{z})\,,

(2.31)

or

\displaystyle\delta{\phi}

\displaystyle\equiv\phi^{\prime}(z,\overline{z})-\phi(z,\overline{z})=\phi^{\prime}(z^{\prime}-\epsilon(z),\overline{z}^{\prime}-\bar{\epsilon}(\overline{z}))-\phi(z,\overline{z})

(2.32)

\displaystyle=-\epsilon(z)\partial\phi-\bar{\epsilon}(\overline{z})\overline{\partial}\phi+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2},\bar{\epsilon}^{2})=-\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\epsilon_{n}l_{n}\phi(z,\overline{z})+\bar{\epsilon}_{n}\overline{l}_{n}\phi(z,\overline{z}))\,,

where we have introduced the generators

l_{n}=-z^{n+1}\partial\,,\qquad\overline{l}_{n}=-\overline{z}^{n+1}\bar{\partial}\,.

(2.33)

We find that the number of independent infinitesimal conformal transformations is infinite. The commutators for these local generators can be calculated straightforwardly:

\displaystyle\commutator{l_n}{l_m}

\displaystyle=(n-m)l_{n+m}\,,

(2.34)

\displaystyle\commutator{\overline{l}_n}{\overline{l}_m}

\displaystyle=(n-m)\overline{l}_{n+m}\,,

\displaystyle\commutator{l_n}{\overline{l}_m}

\displaystyle=0\,.

The first commutation relations define one copy of the so-called Witt algebra. We can observe that the local conformal algebra (
2.34
) is the direct sum of two Witt algebras.

Möbius transformation

A global conformal transformation in two dimensions is defined to be invertible and well-defined on the Riemann sphere
S^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
. Consider the Laurent expansion:

l_{n}=-z^{n+1}\partial_{z}

where
z
is a complex coordinate. The operator
l_{n}
is nonsingular at
z=0
only for
n\geq-1
. To analyze behavior at infinity, we perform a change of variable
z=-{1}/{w}
. Under this transformation, the generator
l_{n}
becomes:

l_{n}=\left(-\frac{1}{w}\right)^{n-1}\partial_{w}

which is nonsingular at
w=0
only for
n\leq 1
.
Thus, the globally defined conformal transformations on the Riemann sphere are generated by
l_{-1}
,
l_{0}
, and
l_{1}
. These three generators form a closed subalgebra of the Witt algebra (
2.34
), defined globally on the Riemann sphere. The discussion of the antiholomorphic part is analogous.

Let us examine the global conformal transformation of the sphere. Using the Laurent expansion
l_{n}=-z^{n+1}\partial_{z}
, we observe the following:

•

Translation:
The operator
l_{-1}=-\partial_{z}
generates the infinitesimal transformation
z^{\prime}=z-\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is an infinitesimal constant. This corresponds to a translation
z\mapsto z+b
, with
b
being an arbitrary complex constant.

•

Dilatation and Rotation:
For
n=0
, the operator
l_{0}=-z\partial_{z}
generates the linear transformation
z^{\prime}=az
, where
a
is an arbitrary complex number. The modulus of
a
corresponds to a dilatation, while the argument (phase) of
a
corresponds to a rotation. To gain geometric intuition, we perform a change of variables
z=re^{i\theta}
, leading to:

l_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\theta}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{l}_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}-\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\theta}

(2.35)

Consequently, the linear combinations

l_{0}+\bar{l}_{0}=-r\partial_{r}\quad\text{and}\quad i(l_{0}-\bar{l}_{0})=-\partial_{\theta}

(2.36)

reveal that
l_{0}+\bar{l}_{0}
generates dilatations, and
i(l_{0}-\bar{l}_{0})
generates rotations.

•

Special Conformal Transformation:
The operator
l_{1}
generates the infinitesimal quadratic transformation. By changing the variable
w=-\frac{1}{z}
, we find
l_{1}=-\partial_{w}
, corresponding to a translation in
w
:
w\mapsto w-c
. Thus,
l_{1}
generates a special conformal transformation given by
z\mapsto\frac{z}{cz+1}
.

The complete set of mappings discussed above is of the form

z\mapsto\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\,,

(2.37)

with
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{C}
. For this transformation to be invertible, we have to require that
ad-bc\neq 0
. If this is the case, we can scale the constants
a,b,c,d
, such that
ad-bc=1
. Furthermore, the expression is unaffected by taking all of
a,b,c,d
to minus themselves. These mappings are called projective transformations. We see each global conformal transformation in two dimensions can be associated with a matrix
\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\
c & d\endlx@physics@matrix)
. For instance:

\displaystyle\text{translation}:\qquad z\mapsto z+b\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & b \\
0 & 1\endlx@physics@matrix)\,,

\displaystyle\text{dilatation}:\quad z\mapsto az\,,a\in\mathbb{R}\,\Leftrightarrow\,\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\sqrt{a} & 0\\
0 & 1/\sqrt{a} \endlx@physics@matrix)\,,

\displaystyle\text{rotation}:\qquad z\mapsto e^{i\theta}z\,,\theta\in\mathbb{R}\,\Leftrightarrow\,\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix e^{i\theta/2} & 0\\
0 & e^{-i\theta/2} \endlx@physics@matrix)\,,

\displaystyle\text{SCT}:\qquad\qquad z\mapsto\frac{z}{cz+1}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & 0 \\
c & 1\endlx@physics@matrix)\,.

We can infer that the conformal group of the Riemann sphere
S^{2}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
is the Möbius group
SL(2,\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

3
Basics in conformal field theory

Conformal field theories are quantum field theories invariant under conformal transformations. This fundamental property leads to powerful constraints on the dynamics and structure of the theory, especially in two dimensions where the conformal symmetry is infinite-dimensional. In this section, we explore how these constraints manifest and significantly enhance our understanding of 2d systems. For a comprehensive treatment, refer to
[
FMS97
, §5]
,
[
Gin88a
, §3]
, and
[
BP09
, §2]
.

3.1
Noether theorem and Ward-Takahashi identity

In this subsection, we review the importance of symmetry in quantum field theory, emphasizing the implications of continuous symmetries as articulated by Noether’s theorem and the Ward-Takahashi identity.

Continuous symmetry transformation

We begin by examining the transformation properties of fields under coordinate changes. In earlier sections, we discussed how scalar fields transform and identified the corresponding symmetry generators. However, fields can have more complex transformation properties than those of simple scalars. For instance, Figure
6
illustrates the rotation transformation of a vector field, demonstrating how the field
\Phi
transforms under coordinate changes.

x\rightarrow x^{\prime}\,,\qquad\Phi(x)\rightarrow\Phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x))\,.

(3.1)

Functional change
\mathcal{F}
can be obtained by studying the representation theory of the coordinate transformation group. We will not illustrate more on this, but give some examples for later use.

Figure 6:
Rotation transformation of a vector field.

Let us start with a rather trivial one, a translation:

x^{\prime}=x+a\,,\qquad\Phi^{\prime}(x+a)=\Phi(x)\,,

(3.2)

which implies that functional change
\mathcal{F}
is trivial here. Next, we consider a Lorentz transformation (rigid rotation). In general, it takes the following form:

x^{\prime\mu}={\Lambda^{\mu}}_{\nu}x^{\nu}\,,\qquad\Phi^{\prime}(\Lambda x)=U(\Lambda)\Phi(x)\,,

(3.3)

where
U(\Lambda)
is a transformation matrix acting on
\Phi
.
3
3

3

In quantum field theory,
\Phi
is more likely to be treated as an operator with transformation rule:

\Phi^{\prime}(\Lambda x)=U^{-1}(\Lambda)\Phi(x)U(\Lambda)\,.

The infinitesimal form of
U(\Lambda)
is

U(\Lambda)=1-\frac{1}{2}i\omega_{\rho\nu}S^{\rho\nu}\,,

(3.4)

where
\omega_{\rho\nu}
are infinitesimal parameters for Lorentz transformation and
S^{\rho\nu}
is a representation of the Lorentz algebra. Finally, under a scaling transformation, we have

x^{\prime}=\lambda x\,,\qquad\Phi^{\prime}(\lambda x)=\lambda^{-\Delta}\Phi(x)\,,

(3.5)

where
\Delta
is the scaling dimension of the field.

Generator

In a field theory, the generator of a symmetry depends on transformations of fields as well as coordinates. The infinitesimal form of (
3.1
) can be written as:

\displaystyle x^{\prime\mu}

\displaystyle=x^{\mu}+\omega_{a}\frac{\delta x^{\mu}}{\delta\omega_{a}}\,,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Phi(x)+\omega_{a}\frac{\delta\mathcal{F}}{\delta\omega_{a}}(x)\,.

Here
\{\omega_{a}\}
is a set of infinitesimal parameters. Now it is customary to define the generator
G_{a}
of a symmetry transformation by the following expression:

\delta_{\omega}\Phi(x)\equiv\Phi^{\prime}(x)-\Phi(x)\equiv-i\omega_{a}G_{a}\Phi(x)\,,

(3.7)

which represents the infinitesimal transformation of the field at the same point. Together with (
3.6
), to first order, we obtain the explicit expression:

iG_{a}\Phi=\frac{\delta x^{\mu}}{\delta\omega_{a}}\partial_{\mu}\Phi-\frac{\delta\mathcal{F}}{\delta\omega_{a}}\,.

(3.8)

For a field satisfying
\Phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\Phi(x)
, the expression reduces to (
2.17
). Using this definition, and combining it with the infinitesimal transformations in the cases of translation, rotation, and scaling, we can write the corresponding generators in field space as follows

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\text{translation}:\qquad P_{\nu}=-i\partial_{\nu}\,,

\displaystyle\text{rotation}:\qquad L^{\rho\nu}=i(x^{\rho}\partial^{\nu}-x^{\nu}\partial^{\rho})+S^{\rho\nu}\,,

\displaystyle\text{dilatation}:\qquad D=-ix^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}-i\Delta\,.

We observe that when the field transformation
\mathcal{F}(\Phi)
is trivial, three expressions reduce to the first three generators in Table
1
.

Noether’s Theorem

Noether’s theorem is a fundamental result in classical field theory, establishing a deep connection between symmetries and conserved quantities. We will now derive Noether’s theorem, which asserts that for every continuous symmetry of the action, there is a corresponding conserved current at the classical level. Consider an action in
d
dimensions

\mathcal{S}=\int\differential^{d}x\mathcal{L}(\Phi,\partial_{\mu}\Phi)

(3.9)

which is endowed with symmetry generated by
\omega_{a}G_{a}
. Under the continuous transformation (
3.1
), the action becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int\differential^{d}x^{\prime}\mathcal{L}(\Phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),\partial_{\mu}^{\prime}\Phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime}))

(3.10)

\displaystyle=\int\differential^{d}x\absolutevalue{\pdv{x'}{x}}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x)),(\partialderivative{x^\nu}{x'^\mu})\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x)))\,.

Using the infinitesimal form for transformation (
3.6
), we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\int\differential^{d}x\bigg(1+\partial_{\mu}(\omega_{a}\frac{\delta x^{\mu}}{\delta\omega_{a}})\bigg)\mathcal{L}\Bigg(\Phi+\omega_{a}\frac{\delta\mathcal{F}}{\delta\omega_{a}},\bigg(\delta^{\nu}_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}(\omega_{a}\frac{\delta x^{\nu}}{\delta\omega_{a}})\bigg)\bigg(\partial_{\nu}\Phi+\partial_{\nu}(\omega_{a}\frac{\delta\mathcal{F}}{\delta\omega_{a}})\bigg)\Bigg)\,.

(3.11)

Since
\omega_{a}G_{a}
is symmetry of the action,
\delta\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}-\mathcal{S}
vanishes if
\omega_{a}
is a constant. Thus, the contribution from all terms with no derivatives of
\omega_{a}
in the variation must be zero. Therefore, to first order, the variation involves only the first derivatives of
\omega_{a}
, obtained by expanding the Lagrangian so that we can write

\delta\mathcal{S}=-\int\differential^{d}x~j^{\mu}_{a}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{a}\,,

(3.12)

where
j^{\mu}_{a}
is called the current associated with the infinitesimal transformation:

j^{\mu}_{a}=\bigg(\partialderivative{\mathcal{L}}{ (\partial_\mu\Phi)}\partial_{\nu}\Phi-\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}\mathcal{L}\bigg)\frac{\delta x^{\nu}}{\delta\omega_{a}}-\partialderivative{\mathcal{L}}{(\partial_\mu\Phi)}\frac{\delta\mathcal{F}}{\delta\omega_{a}}\,.

(3.13)

The integral by parts yields

\delta\mathcal{S}=\int\differential^{d}x\,\omega_{a}\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{a}\,.

(3.14)

If the field configurations satisfy the classical equations of motion, the action is invariant under any variation of the fields. Thus
\delta\mathcal{S}
should vanish for any parameters
\omega_{a}(x)
. This implies the conservation law:

\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{a}=0\,.

(3.15)

The associated charge

Q_{a}=\int\differential^{d-1}x\,j^{0}_{a}\,,

(3.16)

is conserved because its time derivative vanishes

\frac{d}{dt}{Q}_{a}=\int\differential^{d-1}x~\partial_{0}j_{a}^{0}=-\int\differential^{d-1}x~\partial_{i}j^{i}=0\,.

(3.17)

One remark to be mentioned here is that we may freely add to the expression (
3.13
) the divergence of an antisymmetric tensor without affecting the conservation law:

j^{\mu}_{a}\rightarrow j^{\mu}_{a}+\partial_{\nu}B^{\nu\mu}_{a}\,,\qquad B^{\nu\mu}_{a}=-B^{\mu\nu}_{a}\,.

(3.18)

In a field theory, the energy-momentum tensor
T^{\mu v}
is defined from the change of the action under an arbitrary coordinate transformation
x^{{}^{\prime}\mu}=x^{\mu}+\omega^{\mu}(x)
as

\delta\mathcal{S}=\int d^{d}xT^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}~.

(3.19)

In particular, under the translation
\omega^{\mu}=\textrm{const}
, we obtain the form of the canonical energy-momentum tensor by using (
3.13
):

T^{\mu\nu}_{\text{c}}=-\eta^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{L}+\partialderivative{\mathcal{L}}{(\partial_\mu\Phi)}\partial^{\nu}\Phi\,,

(3.20)

Note that the energy-momentum tensor defined in this way (
3.19
) is generally not symmetric under
\mu
and
\nu
. Nevertheless, we have freedom to modify this tensor, as illustrated in (
3.18
), to make it symmetric.

T^{\mu\nu}_{\text{B}}=T^{\mu\nu}+\partial_{\rho}B^{\rho\mu\nu}\,,\quad B^{\rho\mu\nu}=-B^{\mu\rho\nu}\,.

(3.21)

Additionally, we often need to further adjust the form of the energy-momentum tensor due to the last term in (
3.13
). For more detail, the reader can refer to
[
FMS97
, §2.5,§4.2.2]

Now, let us consider the property of the energy-momentum tensor in conformal field theory. Under a conformal transformation
x^{{}^{\prime}\mu}=x^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu}(x)
, we have a conserved current

j_{\mu}=T_{\mu\nu}\epsilon^{v}~.

(3.22)

Since a translation is a conformal transformation, the current conservation implies

0=\partial^{\mu}j_{\mu}=\partial^{\mu}\left(T_{\mu\nu}\epsilon^{\nu}\right)=\left(\partial^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}\right)\epsilon^{\nu}

(3.23)

yielding
\partial^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}=0
. For more general conformal transformations
x^{{}^{\prime}\mu}=x^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu}(x)
, the current conservation imposes

\displaystyle 0=\partial^{\mu}j_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\left(\partial^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}\right)\epsilon^{\nu}+T_{\mu\nu}\left(\partial^{\mu}\epsilon^{\nu}\right)

(3.24)

\displaystyle=0+\frac{1}{2}T_{\mu\nu}\left(\partial^{\mu}\epsilon^{\nu}+\partial^{\nu}\epsilon^{\mu}\right)

(3.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}T_{\mu}{}^{\mu}(\partial\cdot\epsilon)

(3.26)

where we use (
2.9
) in the last line. Thus, the energy-momentum tensor is traceless
T_{\mu}{}^{\mu}=0
in conformal field theory.

Correlation functions

In the previous sections, we focused on the effect of a continuous symmetry classically. At the quantum level, the primary objects of study are correlation functions. A general correlation function is defined as

\expectationvalue{\Phi(x_1)\cdots\Phi(x_n)}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi]\Phi(x_{1})\cdots\Phi(x_{n})\exp{-\cS[\Phi]}\,,

(3.27)

where
\mathcal{Z}
is the vacuum functional:

\mathcal{Z}=\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi]\exp(-\cS[\Phi])\,.

(3.28)

The path integral notation
[\mathcal{D}\Phi]
indicates integration over all possible field configurations of
\Phi
. The operators in the correlation function
\Phi(x_{1})\cdots\Phi(x_{n})
are automatically time-ordered when using the path integral method, i.e.

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{T}(\Phi(x_1) \cdots\Phi(x_n))}=\expectationvalue{\Phi(x_1)\cdots\Phi(x_n)}\,,

(3.29)

where
\mathcal{T}
is the time ordering operator which makes the field operator in time order:

\mathcal{T}(\Phi(x_{1})\cdots\Phi(x_{n}))=\Phi(x_{1})\cdots\Phi(x_{n})\,,\qquad\text{if}\quad t_{1}>t_{2}>\cdots>t_{n}\,.

(3.30)

In Euclidean space, time direction can be arbitrary. We will specify the time direction in the two-dimensional case later.

A continuous symmetry leads to constraints on the correlation function:

\expectationvalue{\Phi(x_1')\cdots\Phi(x_2')}=\expectationvalue{\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x_1))\cdots\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x_n)) }\,.

(3.31)

This identity is a consequence of the invariance of the action and the path integral measure under the transformation:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\Phi(x_1')\cdots\Phi(x_n')}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi]\Phi(x_{1}^{\prime})\cdots\Phi(x_{n}^{\prime})\exp(-\cS[\Phi])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi^{\prime}]\Phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime}_{1})\cdots\Phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime}_{n})\exp(-S[\Phi'])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi]\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x_{1}))\cdots\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x_{n}))\exp(-\cS[\Phi])

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue{\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x_1))\cdots\mathcal{F}(\Phi(x_n))}\,,

where in the second line, we rename the integration variable
\Phi\rightarrow\Phi^{\prime}
. On the third line, the symmetry assumptions for the action and the functional integral measure are applied. (
3.31
) immediately implies that the correlation function is invariant under translation.

Ward-Takahashi identity

The quantum version of Noether’s theorem is known as the Ward-Takahashi identity. We first change the functional field variable in (
3.27
) by an infinitesimal transformation (
3.7
)

\Phi^{\prime}(x)=\Phi(x)-i\omega_{a}G_{a}\Phi(x)\,,

(3.32)

The correlation function is invariant under the symmetry so that we can write

\expectationvalue{X}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi^{\prime}](X+\delta X)\exp{-\cS[\Phi]-\int\dd^d x\omega_a (x) \partial_\mu j^\mu_a}\,,

(3.33)

where
X
denotes the product
\Phi(x_{1})\cdots\Phi(x_{n})
of fields in (
3.27
). Expanding this to first order in
\omega_{a}(x)
yields

\expectationvalue{\delta X}=\int\differential^{d}x\partial_{\mu}\expectationvalue{j^\mu_a (x)X}\omega_{a}(x)\,.

(3.34)

The variation
\delta X
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\delta X

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Phi(x_{1})\cdots G_{a}\Phi(x_{i})\cdots\Phi(x_{n}))\omega_{a}(x_{i})

(3.35)

\displaystyle=-i\int\differential^{d}x\,\omega_{a}(x)\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Phi(x_{1})\cdots G_{a}\Phi(x_{i})\cdots\Phi(x_{n}))\delta(x-x_{i})

Since the above two expressions hold for any infinitesimal function
\omega^{a}(x)
, we can express the following local relation:

\displaystyle\partialderivative{x^\mu}

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{ j^\mu_a(x)\Phi(x_1)\cdots\Phi(x_n) }

(3.36)

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta(x-x_{i})\expectationvalue{ \Phi(x_1)\cdots G_a\Phi(x_i) \cdots\Phi(x_n) }\,.

This is the Ward-Takahashi identity for the current
j^{\mu}_{a}
.

The Ward-Takahashi identity allows us to identify the conserved charge
Q_{a}
defined in (
3.16
) as the generator of the symmetry transformation in the Hilbert space of quantum states. Let
Y=\Phi(x_{2})\cdots\Phi(x_{n})
and suppose that the time
t=x^{0}_{1}
is larger than all the times in
Y
. We integrate the Ward-Takahashi identity in a very thin box bounded by
t_{-}<t
,
t_{+}>t
, and by spatial infinity, which excludes all other points
x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
. This gives

\expectationvalue{Q_a(t_+)\Phi(x_1)Y}-\expectationvalue{Q_a(t_-)\Phi(x_1)Y}=-i\expectationvalue{G_a \Phi(x_1)Y}\,.

(3.37)

In the limit
t_{-}\rightarrow t_{+}
, for an arbitrary set of fields
Y
, we obtain

\commutator{Q_a}{\Phi}=-iG_{a}\Phi\,.

(3.38)

This shows that the conserved charge
Q_{a}
is the generator in the operator formalism.

3.2
Primary fields

In the previous section, we reviewed some concepts of field theory in
d
dimensions and derived the current expressions for three important currents in conformal symmetry. Now, we will focus specifically on two dimensions. First, we need to define how fields transform under a conformal transformation. From (
2.34
), we see that
l_{0}
and
\bar{l}_{0}
are the Cartan subalgebra of the conformal algebra. Therefore, we can assume that physical states are eigenstates of
l_{0}
and
\bar{l}_{0}
with eigenvalues
\{h,\bar{h}\}
, known as the
conformal dimensions
. Under a scaling transformation
z\to\lambda z
,
\bar{z}\to\lambda\bar{z}
, the field operator transforms as

\phi^{\prime}(\lambda z,\overline{\lambda}\overline{z})=(\lambda)^{-h}(\overline{\lambda})^{-\overline{h}}\phi(z,\overline{z})\,.

(3.39)

If a field transforms under an arbitrary global conformal map
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
,
z\rightarrow w(z),\overline{z}\rightarrow\overline{w}(\overline{z})
,

\phi^{\prime}(w,\overline{w})=\bigg(\frac{\differential w}{\differential z}\bigg)^{-h}\bigg(\frac{\differential\overline{w}}{\differential\overline{z}}\bigg)^{-\overline{h}}\phi(z,\overline{z})\,,

(3.40)

then we call it a
quasi-primary field
. Under infinitesimal transformation
z\rightarrow z^{\prime}=z+\epsilon(z)
, we obtain the infinitesimal transformation of quasi-primary field:

\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\phi\equiv\phi^{\prime}(z^{\prime},\overline{z}^{\prime})-\phi(z,\overline{z})=-(h\phi\partial\epsilon+\epsilon\partial\phi)-(\overline{h}\phi\overline{\partial}\overline{\epsilon}+\overline{\epsilon}\overline{\partial}\phi)\,.

(3.41)

A field whose transformation under any conformal transformation given by (
3.40
) or equivalently (
3.41
) is called a
primary field
. Therefore all primary fields are also quasi-primary, but the reverse is not true. If an operator is not primary, it is generally called secondary.

The definition (
3.40
) imposes strong constraints on their correlation functions. For the two-point function of chiral quasi-primary fields, translation invariance implies that the two-point function is of the form

\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z)\phi_2(w)}=g(z-w)\,.

(3.42)

Then under conformal transformation
z\rightarrow\lambda z
, we have

\lambda^{-(h_{1}+h_{2})}g(z-w)=g(\lambda(z-w))\,,

(3.43)

where
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are the conformal dimensions of
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
, respectively. This leads to a constraint for the two-point function. Since the relation above satisfies for arbitrary
\lambda
, we obtain that
g(z-w)
is of the form

g(z-w)=\frac{C_{12}}{(z-w)^{h_{1}+h_{2}}}\,,

(3.44)

where
C_{12}
is a constant. Additionally, the two-point function should be consistent with (
3.40
) when we apply the conformal transformation
z\rightarrow-1/z
. This constraint requires that conformal dimensions of the two fields must be equal, i.e.

\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z)\phi_2(w)}=\frac{C_{12}}{(z-w)^{2h}}\,,\quad\text{if}\quad h_{1}=h_{2}=h\,.

(3.45)

The same treatment can be used to obtain the form of the three-point function for quasi-primary operators. For the holomorphic part, we have

\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z_1)\phi_2(z_2)\phi_3(z_3)}=\frac{C_{123}}{z_{12}^{h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}}z_{23}^{h_{2}+h_{3}-h_{1}}z_{13}^{h_{3}+h_{1}-h_{2}}}\,,

(3.46)

where
z_{ij}=z_{i}-z_{j}
. (Exercise) Although these results are obtained by Polyakov in
[
Pol70
]
, determining the four-point function and beyond requires the use of an infinite-dimensional symmetry
[
BPZ84
, ISZ88-No.1]
, which will be discussed in §
5.6
.

3.3
Conformal Ward-Takahashi identity

Now, let us apply Ward-Takahashi identities to general conformal invariance, known as
conformal Ward-Takahashi identity
. Conformal invariance gives rise to the current (
3.22
). Substituting it into (
3.34
), we obtain the conformal Ward-Takahashi identity

\delta_{\epsilon}\expectationvalue{X}=\int_{D}\differential^{2}x~\partial_{\mu}\expectationvalue{T^{\mu\nu}(x)\e_\nu(x)X}\,.

(3.47)

Here, the integral is taken over the domain
D
containing the positions of all the fields in the
X
. In two dimensions, it is convenient to use the (anti-)holomorphic coordinate
(z,\overline{z})
as introduced in (
2.27
).
Writing each component explicitly, we have

\displaystyle T_{zz}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(T_{00}-2iT_{10}-T_{11})\,,

(3.48)

\displaystyle T_{\overline{z}\overline{z}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(T_{00}+2iT_{10}-T_{11})\,,

\displaystyle T_{z\overline{z}}=

\displaystyle T_{\overline{z}z}=\frac{1}{4}(T_{00}+T_{11})

The traceless property of the energy-momentum tensor is
T_{z\overline{z}}=T_{\overline{z}z}=0
. From the conservation law
\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0
, we see that
T_{zz}
and
T_{\overline{z}\overline{z}}
are holomorphic and antiholomorphic respectively. Furthermore we introduce the notation

T=-2\pi T_{zz}\,,\quad\overline{T}=-2\pi T_{\overline{z}\overline{z}}\,.

(3.49)

Using Gauss’s theorem and in two dimensions, we find

\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\expectationvalue{X}=\frac{i}{2}\int_{C}\{-\differential z\expectationvalue{T^{\overline{z}\overline{z}}\e_{\overline{z}}X}+\differential\overline{z}\expectationvalue{T^{zz}\e_z X}\}\,,

(3.50)

where we have defined
\epsilon(z)=\epsilon^{z}
and
\overline{\epsilon}(\overline{z})=\epsilon^{\overline{z}}
, respectively.
Using (
3.49
), we get

\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\expectationvalue{X}=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C}\differential z\,\epsilon(z)\expectationvalue{T(z)X}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C}\differential\overline{z}\,\overline{\epsilon}(\overline{z})\expectationvalue{\overline{T}(\overline{z})X}\,,

(3.51)

where the contour
C=\partial D
should contain all the positions of fields in
X
. Notice that the validity of (
3.51
) extends beyond primary fields, and thus it may be taken as a definition of the effect of conformal transformations on an arbitrary local field within a correlation function. Suppose that
X
is a product of
n
primary fields
\phi_{i}(w_{i})
,

X(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}):=\phi_{1}(w_{1})\cdots\phi_{n}(w_{n})~.

Using simple complex analysis, we write the variation (
3.41
) of a primary field

\displaystyle(\partial_{w_{i}}\epsilon({w_{i}}))\phi_{i}({w_{i}},\overline{w}_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C}dz~\frac{\epsilon(z)\phi_{i}({w_{i}},\overline{w}_{i})}{(z-{w_{i}})^{2}}

(3.52)

\displaystyle\epsilon({w_{i}})(\partial_{w_{i}}\phi_{i}({w_{i}},\overline{w}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C}dz~\frac{\epsilon(z)\partial_{w_{i}}\phi_{i}({w_{i}},\overline{w}_{i})}{z-{w_{i}}}~,

Therefore, the conformal Ward identity (
3.51
) can be written as

\expectationvalue{T(z)X}=\sum_{i}\bigg\{\frac{1}{z-w_{i}}\partial_{w_{i}}\expectationvalue{X}+\frac{h_{i}}{(z-w_{i})^{2}}\expectationvalue{X}\bigg\}+\text{reg.}\,,

(3.53)

where ”reg.” refers to a holomorphic function of
z
that is regular at
z=w_{i}
. The anti-holomorphic counterpart is similar. This expression provides the singular behavior of the correlator of the energy-momentum tensor
T(z)
with a set of primary fields
X
as
z
approaches the point
w_{i}
. This implies the operator product expansion (OPE) of the energy-momentum tensor with primary fields by removing the bracket
\langle\cdots\rangle
:

T(z)X\sim\sum_{i}\bigg\{\frac{1}{z-w_{i}}\partial_{w_{i}}X+\frac{h_{i}}{(z-w_{i})^{2}}X\bigg\}\,.

(3.54)

The symbol
\sim
indicates that regular terms are ignored. The OPE also contains infinitely many regular terms which, for the energy-momentum tensor, cannot be derived from the conformal Ward identity. The
operator product expansion
(OPE) represents a product of operators and describes the behavior as two operators approach each other. In general, we would write the OPE of two operators
A(z)
and
B(w)
as

A(z)B(w)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\{AB\}_{n}(w)}{(z-w)^{n}}\,,

(3.55)

where the composite operators
\{AB\}_{n}(w)
are nonsingular at
w=z
. In particular for a single primary field
\phi
with conformal dimensions
h
and
\overline{h}
, we have:

\displaystyle T(z)\phi(w,\overline{w})

\displaystyle\sim\frac{h}{(z-w)^{2}}\phi(w,\overline{w})+\frac{1}{z-w}\partial\phi(w,\overline{w})\,,

(3.56)

\displaystyle\overline{T}(\overline{z})\phi(w,\overline{w})

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\overline{h}}{(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{2}}\phi(w,\overline{w})+\frac{1}{\overline{z}-\overline{w}}\overline{\partial}\phi(w,\overline{w})\,.

(3.57)

The expression above can be treated as an alternative definition for a primary field. For a secondary field, but with conformal dimension
h
and
\overline{h}
, its OPE with energy-momentum tensor has more singular terms, i.e.

\displaystyle T(z)\phi(w,\overline{w})

\displaystyle\sim\cdots+\frac{h}{(z-w)^{2}}\phi(w,\overline{w})+\frac{1}{z-w}\partial\phi(w,\overline{w})\,,

(3.58)

\displaystyle\overline{T}(\overline{z})\phi(w,\overline{w})

\displaystyle\sim\cdots+\frac{\overline{h}}{(\overline{z}-\overline{w})^{2}}\phi(w,\overline{w})+\frac{1}{\overline{z}-\overline{w}}\overline{\partial}\phi(w,\overline{w})\,.

(3.59)

3.4
Example: Free boson

Now we will study the free boson field as an example. We first review some basic concepts in field theory.

Calculation of two-point function

A two-point function is a correlation function of two operators. Therefore, from the definition of correlation function (
3.27
), we can easily write the definition of a two-point function for the bosonic field as

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{T}(\phi(x_1)\phi(x_2))}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi]\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2})\exp{-\cS[\Phi]}\,.

(3.60)

The time-ordering operator
\mathcal{T}
is automatically satisfied in a path integral. Then, we can define a generating functional for the theory:

\mathcal{Z}[J]=\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi]\exp{-(S-\int\dd^d x \phi(x) J(x)}\,,

(3.61)

where
J(x)
is an auxiliary ”current”. Then the two-point function can be generated by the functional derivative of
J
:

\expectationvalue{\phi(x_1)\phi(x_2)}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}[0]}\frac{\delta}{\delta J(x_{1})}\frac{\delta}{\delta J(x_{2})}\mathcal{Z}[J]\Bigg|_{J=0}\,.

(3.62)

To calculate the two-point function more generally, we first assume that the action
S
takes the following form:

\mathcal{S}=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d}x\differential^{d}y\,\phi(x)A(x,y)\phi(y)\,.

(3.63)

Let us introduce a variable transformation
\phi\rightarrow\phi+\phi_{0}
without changing the path integral measure and result as we have illustrated before. The term in the exponential of the path integral becomes:

\displaystyle-\mathcal{S}+\int\differential^{d}x\phi(x)J(x)\rightarrow

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d}x\differential^{d}y\phi(x)A(x,y)\phi(y)-\int\differential^{d}x\differential^{d}y\phi(x)A(x,y)\phi_{0}(y)-

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d}x\differential^{d}y\phi_{0}(x)A(x,y)\phi_{0}(y)+\int\differential^{d}x\phi_{0}(x)J(x)+\int\differential^{d}x\phi(x)J(x)\,,

Now we choose
\phi_{0}
to satisfy the equation:

\int\differential^{d}yA(x,y)\phi_{0}(y)=J(x)\,\rightarrow\phi_{0}(x)=\int\differential^{d}yA^{-1}(x,y)J(y)\,.

(3.64)

Then, the generating functional can be
written as

\mathcal{Z}[J]=N\exp{\frac{1}{2}
\int\dd^d x \dd^d y \,
J(x) A^{-1}(x,y) J(y)
}\,,

(3.65)

where the factor
N
is independent of
J

N=\int[\mathcal{D}\Phi]\exp{-\frac{1}{2} \int\dd^d x \dd^d y \,
\phi(x) A(x,y) \phi(y)}\,.

(3.66)

Therefore, by using
(
3.62
),
we have

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{T}(\phi(x)\phi(y))}=A^{-1}(x,y)

(3.67)

We will apply the formula above to calculate the two-point function of specific theories.

Two-Point function in Free boson

In two dimensions, the massless free boson has the following Euclidean action:

\mathcal{S}=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int\differential^{2}x~\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial^{\mu}\varphi\,,

(3.68)

which is conformal. Comparing with (
3.63
), we have

A(x,y)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\delta^{(2)}(x-y)\square\,,

(3.69)

From (
3.67
), we can calculate the two-point function
K(x,y)\equiv\expectationvalue{\varphi(x_1)\varphi(x_2)}
by solving the following equation:

-\frac{1}{4\pi}\square K(x,y)=\delta^{(2)}(x-y)\,,

(3.70)

From (
3.31
) and the trivial nature of
\mathcal{F}
for a scalar field,
K(x,y)
is invariant under translation and rotation. Thus, we can write
K(x,y)\equiv K(\rho)
with
\rho=\absolutevalue{x-y}
, and integrate over
x
within a disk of radius
\rho
around y. We find

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int^{r}_{0}\differential\rho\rho\bigg(-\frac{1}{\rho}\partialderivative{\rho}(\rho K'(\rho))\bigg)

(3.71)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(-rK^{\prime}(r))\,.

The solution of the two-point function for massless free boson can be obtained up to an additive constant,

\expectationvalue{\varphi(x)\varphi(y)}=-\ln{\abs{x-y}}^{2}\,.

(3.72)

Wick’s Theorem

In the last part, we introduced the concept of the two-point function for a theory. Now, we will briefly review Wick’s Theorem, which highlights the fundamental role of the two-point function in quantum field theory.

In quantum field theory, a free (elementary) field operator can be decomposed into two parts:

\phi(x)=\phi^{+}(x)+\phi^{-}(x)\,,

(3.73)

where
\phi^{-}(x)
contains only annihilation operators, and
\phi^{+}(x)
contains only creation operators. The normal ordering
:\ :
is defined to place all annihilation operators to the right of the creation operators. For instance,

:\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2}):=\phi(x_{1})^{+}\phi(x_{2})^{+}+\phi(x_{1})^{-}\phi(x_{2})^{-}+\phi(x_{1})^{+}\phi(x_{2})^{-}+\phi(x_{2})^{+}\phi(x_{1})^{-}\,.

The time-ordered product for
x^{0}_{1}>x^{0}_{2}
gives

\mathcal{T}(\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2}))=\phi(x_{1})^{+}\phi(x_{2})^{+}+\phi(x_{1})^{-}\phi(x_{2})^{-}+\phi(x_{1})^{+}\phi(x_{2})^{-}+\phi(x_{1})^{-}\phi(x_{2})^{+}\,.

Thus, we obtain the relation between the time-ordered product and the normal-ordered product for
x^{0}_{1}>x^{0}_{2}
as

\mathcal{T}(\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2}))=:\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2}):+\commutator{\phi(x_1)^-}{\phi(x_2)^+}\,.

(3.74)

The commutator on the right side is a constant. By taking the expectation value of both sides, we find that this constant is simply the two-point function

\mathcal{T}(\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2}))=:\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2}):+\expectationvalue{\phi(x_1)\phi(x_2)}\,.

(3.75)

Note that the same expression holds for
x^{0}_{1}<x^{0}_{2}
. It is convenient to introduce
Wick contraction
for the computation of two-point functions and OPE

\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=28.42368pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=28.42368pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.35558pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=23.39876pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.8611pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.0556pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=21.38911pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 3.47221pt}}\phi_{1}(x_{1})\phi_{2}(x_{2})\equiv\expectationvalue{\phi_1(x_1)\phi_2(x_2)}\,.

(3.76)

Wick’s Theorem states that the time-ordered product of operators at different points in space-time is equal to the normal-ordered product, plus all possible ways of contracting pairs of fields within it. For instance,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{4})=

\displaystyle:\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{4}:+:\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=10.44447pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=10.44447pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.35558pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=8.71117pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.8611pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.0556pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=8.1112pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 3.47221pt}}\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{4}:+:\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=20.88895pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=20.88895pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.35558pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=17.42233pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.8611pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.0556pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=16.22241pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 3.47221pt}}\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{3}\phi_{4}:+:\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=31.33342pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=31.33342pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.35558pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=26.1335pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.8611pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.0556pt\hbox{\vrule The normal-ordering can be applied to the product of two operators at the same point in space-time. In this case, we can apply Wick’s theorem in the following way

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(:\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{2}(x)::\phi_{3}(y)\phi_{4}(y):)

width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=58.2598pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.8611pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.0556pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=52.33269pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 3.47221pt}}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 23.93755pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=8.61108pt\vrule width=23.93755pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=8.61108pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 23.93755pt\kern 5.22223pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=8.61108pt\vrule width=23.93755pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=8.61108pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 19.49593pt\kern 4.35558pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=8.61108pt\vrule width=19.49593pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=8.61108pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.8611pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 17.48628pt\kern 4.0556pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=8.61108pt\vrule width=17.48628pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=8.61108pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 3.47221pt}}\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{2}(x)\phi_{3}(y)\phi_{4}(y):\,.

For fermions, Wick’s Theorem is almost identical, except that exchanging two fermions introduces a minus sign.

OPEs in Free boson

Now we are ready to calculate OPE for the massless free boson. The equation of motion for the action (
3.68
) is

\square\varphi=0\,.

(3.77)

In complex coordinates, we have

\overline{\partial}\partial\varphi(z,\overline{z})=0\,,

(3.78)

implying that
\partial\varphi
and
\overline{\partial}\varphi
are holomorphic and antiholomorphic respectively. The canonical energy-momentum tensor defined by (
3.20
) is

T_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\big[\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\varphi\partial^{\rho}\varphi\big]\,.

(3.79)

It is automatically symmetric and traceless, and its quantum version in complex coordinates is

T(z)=-\frac{1}{2}:\partial\varphi\partial\varphi:\,.

(3.80)

In order for its vacuum expectation value to vanish, the energy-momentum tensor has to be normal ordered. The antiholomorphic part is similar. We can write two-point function (
3.72
) in terms of the complex coordinates, this is

\expectationvalue{\varphi(z,\overline{z})\varphi(w,\overline{w})}=-\{\ln(z-w)+\ln(\overline z-\overline w)\}+\text{const}\,.

(3.81)

We shall concentrate more on the holomorphic field
\partial_{z}\varphi
. By taking derivatives
\partial_{z}
and
\partial_{w}
from above, we have

\expectationvalue{\partial\varphi(z,\overline z) \partial\varphi(w, \overline w) }=-\frac{1}{(z-w)^{2}}\,.

(3.82)

Hence, the OPE of
\partial\varphi
with itself is

\partial\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(w)\sim-\frac{1}{(z-w)^{2}}\,.

(3.83)

The OPE of
T(z)
with
\partial\varphi
may be calculated from Wick’s theorem:

\displaystyle T(z)\partial\varphi(w)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}:\partial\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(z):\partial\varphi(w)

(3.84)

\displaystyle\sim-:\partial\varphi(z)\partial\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.27083pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=33.05202pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.30554pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.27083pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=33.05202pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.30554pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.62987pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=27.70726pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 3.01389pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.29515pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=24.94966pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 2.15277pt}}\varphi(z):\partial\varphi(w)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\partial\varphi(z)}{(z-w)^{2}}\,.

By expanding
\partial\varphi(z)
around
w
, we arrive at the OPE

T(z)\partial\varphi(w)\sim\frac{\partial\varphi(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi(w)}{z-w}\,.

(3.85)

Comparing with (
3.56
), we see
\partial\varphi(z)
is a primary field with conformal dimension h=1. Wick’s theorem also allows us to calculate the OPE of the
energy-momentum tensor with itself.

T(z)T(w)\sim\frac{1/2}{(z-w)^{4}}+\frac{2T(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial T(w)}{(z-w)}\,.

(3.86)

Transformation of the energy-momentum tensor

It is easy to see that the energy-momentum tensor is not a primary field because of the term

\frac{1/2}{(z-w)^{4}}~.

From (
3.58
), we see that the energy-momentum tensor for free massless boson is a secondary field, with conformal dimension
h=2
. For a general 2d conformal field theory,
TT
OPE takes the form

T(z)T(w)\sim\frac{c/2}{(z-w)^{4}}+\frac{2T(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial T(w)}{(z-w)}\,,

(3.87)

with
c
a constant called the
central charge
. The central charge depends on a 2d CFT, and it is one of the most important information of a 2d CFT. For the free boson, it is equal to one.

By using conformal Ward-Takahashi identity
(
3.51
), we get an infinitesimal variation of
T
under a local conformal
transformation.

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}T(w)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C}\differential z\,\epsilon(z)T(z)T(w)

(3.88)

\displaystyle=-\frac{c}{12}\partial^{3}\epsilon(w)-2T(w)\partial\epsilon(w)-\epsilon(w)\partial T(w)\,.

Its finite transformation can be written as
z\rightarrow w(z)
is

\widetilde{T}(w)=\bigg(\derivative{w}{z}\bigg)^{-2}\big[T(z)-\frac{c}{12}\{w;z\}\big]\,,

(3.89)

where
\{w;z\}
is the additional term called the
Schwarzian derivative
:

\{w;z\}=\frac{w^{\prime\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}-\frac{3}{2}\left(\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w^{\prime}}\right)^{2}~.

(3.90)

Figure 7:
Mapping from the cylinder to the complex plane.

3.5
Virasoro algebra

Radial quantization

In the previous discussion, we observed that the operator formalism distinguishes between time and space directions. However, in a 2D CFT, it is most convenient to work with complex coordinates in the Euclidean signature. In fact, there is a natural choice in the context of string theory by using radial quantization. We may first define our theory on an infinite space-time cylinder, with time
t
going from
-\infty
to
+\infty
along the “flat” direction of the cylinder, and space being compactified with a coordinate
x
going from 0 to
\ell
, where the points
(0,t)
and
(\ell,t)
are identified. Here,
t
is still the Euclidean time, and the cylinder can be described by a complex coordinate
x=t+ix
. We then map the cylinder onto a complex plane by a conformal transformation

z=e^{2\pi\xi/\ell}\,

(3.91)

as illustrated in Figure
7

[
Car84b
, ISZ88-No.18]
. The remote past
(t\to-\infty)
is mapped to the origin
z=0
, while the remote future
(t\to+\infty)
corresponds to infinity. Now, the theory lives on the complex plane where the radial ray and the circle around the origin correspond to the
t
-direction and the cylinder’s circle, respectively. From quantum mechanics, we know that the generator of time translations is the Hamiltonian, which in this case corresponds to the dilatation operator. Similarly, the generator for space translations is the momentum operator, corresponding to rotations. Recalling (
2.36
), we can write

H=L_{0}+\overline{L}_{0}\,,\qquad P=i(L_{0}-\overline{L}_{0})\,

(3.92)

where
L_{0}
and
\overline{L}_{0}
are the zero modes of the energy-momentum tensors, as we will see below.

A time-ordering product now becomes the radial ordering product:

\mathcal{R}(\phi_{1}(z)\phi_{2}(w))=\begin{cases}\phi_{1}(z)\phi_{2}(w)&\absolutevalue{z}>\absolutevalue{w}\,,\\
\phi_{2}(w)\phi_{1}(z)&\absolutevalue{z}<\absolutevalue{w}\,.\end{cases}

(3.93)

As usual, we will always omit the radial-ordered operator in the correlation function as well as in the OPE expansion. One consequence after specifying time direction is that we can relate OPE to commutation relations. For this, let consider the contour integral around
w
for two holomorphic field
a(z)
and
b(w)

\oint_{w}\differential z\,a(z)b(w)=\oint_{C_{1}}\differential z\,a(z)b(w)-\oint_{C_{2}}\differential z\,b(w)a(z)=\commutator{A}{b(w)}\,,

(3.94)

where the operator
A
is the contour integral of
a(z)
at a fixed time

A=\oint\differential z\,a(z)\,.

(3.95)

Here we take the contours
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
at fixed-radius
\absolutevalue{w}+\epsilon
and
\absolutevalue{w}-\epsilon
with a small positive number
\epsilon
as illustrated in Figure
8
. Then, with
B=\oint\differential z\,b(z)
