, we can generalize the relation (
3.94
) to

\commutator{A}{B}=\oint_{0}\differential w\commutator{A}{b(w)}=\oint_{0}\differential w\oint_{w}\differential za(z)b(w)\,.

(3.96)

Figure 8:
Subtraction of contours

State/Operator correspondence

After the radial quantization, states living on the circle can be generated by inserting operators. The vacuum state
\ket{0}
corresponds to inserting nothing. The initial state
\ket{\phi_{\text{in}}}
corresponds to inserting an operator at the origin since the infinite past on the cylinder is mapped to
z=\overline{z}=0
,

\ket{\phi_{\text{in}}}=\lim_{z,\overline{z}\rightarrow 0}\phi(z,\overline{z})\ket{0}\,.

(3.97)

As we have seen, a 2D CFT has scaling transformation as a symmetry. The state defined on a circle with a fixed radius can be scaled down to the origin by the scaling transformation as in (
3.97
), which results in an operator. Conversely, an operator at the origin can be scaled up to a state on a circle around the origin. Therefore, there is a one-to-one correspondence between a state and an operator in 2d CFT. This is called the
state-operator correspondence
.

In this Hilbert space, we must also define a bilinear product, by defining an asymptotic out state. In Minkowski space, Hermitian conjugation does not affect the space-time coordinates. However, since the Euclidean time
t_{\text{E}}=it_{\text{M}}
, the Euclidean time must be reversed upon Hermitian conjugation. In radial quantization, this corresponds to the mapping
z\rightarrow 1/\overline{z}
. Therefore, the definition of Hermitian conjugation is

[\phi(z,\overline{z})]^{\dagger}=\overline{z}^{-2h}z^{-2\overline{h}}\phi(1/\overline{z},1/z)\,,

(3.98)

where
h
and
\overline{h}
are conformal dimensions of quasi-primary field
\phi
. The prefactors on the r.h.s. can be justified by demanding that the hermitian conjugation exchanges the conformal dimensions for holomorphic and antiholomorphic parts. We then define the asymptotic out state:

\bra{\phi_\text{out}}=\ket{\phi_\text{in}}^{\dagger}\,.

(3.99)

Then, the inner product is well defined:

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\phi_{\text{out}}}{\phi_{\text{in}}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{z,\overline{z},w,\overline{w}\rightarrow 0}\overline{z}^{-2h}z^{-2\overline{h}}\bra{0}\phi(1/\overline{z},1/z)\phi(w,\overline{w})\ket{0}

(3.100)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\xi,\overline{\xi}\rightarrow\infty}\overline{\xi}^{2h}\xi^{2\overline{h}}\bra{0}\phi(\overline{\xi},\xi)\phi(0,0)\ket{0}\,.

According to the expression
(
3.45
)
, the last expression is independent of
\xi
, which also justifies the prefactors appearing in (
3.98
).

The mode expansion of a field
\phi(z,\overline{z})
with conformal dimension
(h,\overline{h})
is conventionally given by

\phi(z,\overline{z})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-m-h}\overline{z}^{-n-\overline{h}}\phi_{m,n}\,.

(3.101)

A straightforward Hermitian conjugation yields

\phi(z,\overline{z})^{\dagger}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\overline{z}^{-m-h}z^{-n-\overline{h}}\phi_{m,n}^{\dagger}\,,

(3.102)

while the definition (
3.98
) gives

\phi(z,\overline{z})^{\dagger}=\overline{z}^{-2h}z^{-2\overline{h}}\phi(1/\overline{z},1/z)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{-m,-n}\overline{z}^{-m-h}z^{-n-\overline{h}}\,.

(3.103)

Comparing the above two expressions for mode expansions, we get

\phi^{\dagger}_{m,n}=\phi_{-m,-n}

(3.104)

This is the usual expression for the Hermitian conjugate of modes, not only for primary fields.

Conformal generators and Virasoro algebra

In §
2.6
, we have derived the conformal generators
l_{n}
and
\overline{l}_{n}
in coordinate space, which form two copies of the Witt algebra (
2.34
). Now, we shall derive the conformal generators in Hilbert space and its corresponding algebra. We have already seen in (
3.38
) that the conserved charge defined by (
3.16
) acts as a generator of the corresponding symmetry in Hilbert space. Since the current for conformal symmetry has the form of
T^{\mu\nu}\epsilon_{\nu}
, writing in complex coordinate and dropping the antiholomorphic part for convenience, we arrive at the conserved charge by performing the contour integral of equal time as

Q_{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{C}\differential z\,\epsilon(z)T(z)\,.

(3.105)

Then, the conformal Ward-Takahashi identities (
3.51
) becomes the same as (
3.38
)

\delta_{\epsilon}\Phi(z)=-\commutator{Q_\e}{\Phi(z)}\,,

(3.106)

which verifies that the conserved charge in (
3.105
) acts like a conformal generator as well. Now we expand the holomorphic part of the energy-momentum as follows

T(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-2}L_{n}\,,

(3.107)

with
L_{n}
equals to

L_{n}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\differential z\,z^{n+1}T(z)\,.

(3.108)

If we expand
\epsilon(z)
as

\epsilon(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{n+1}\epsilon_{n}\,,

(3.109)

we find the conserved charge

Q_{\epsilon}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\epsilon_{n}L_{n}\,.

(3.110)

Therefore,
L_{n}
and its antiholomorphic part
\overline{L}_{n}
become a set of conformal generators in Hilbert space. The commutator of
L_{n}
can be obtained by using (
3.96
)

\commutator{L_n}{L_m}=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\oint_{0}\differential w\,w^{m+1}\int_{w}\differential z\,z^{n+1}T(z)T(w)\,.

(3.111)

Plugging
TT
OPE (
3.87
), we finally obtain the
Virasoro algebra

\commutator{L_n}{L_m}=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,0}\,.

(3.112)

The antiholomorphic part is similar. When central charge
c
vanishes, the Virasoro algebra reduces to the Witt algebra.

3.6
Verma module

This subsection will briefly introduce the Hilbert space of a 2d CFT constructed by primary field operators and generators in Virasoro algebra. We have previously mentioned that the vacuum state
\ket{0}
corresponds to a state with no operator inserted. Since
T(z)\ket{0}
and
\overline{T}(\overline{z})\ket{0}
is supposed to be well-defined as
z
,
\overline{z}
goes to zero, a vacuum state should satisfy

L_{n}\ket{0}=0\,\qquad\overline{L}_{n}\ket{0}=0\,.\qquad(n\geq-1)

(3.113)

We know that asymptotic states can be obtained by acting an operator at the origin on the vacuum state. By inserting a primary field, the obtained state becomes the eigenstate of
L_{0}
.

\ket{h,\overline{h}}\equiv\phi(0,0)\ket{0}\,.

(3.114)

A simple demonstration follows from (
3.96
) and OPE for primary field (
3.56
):

\displaystyle\commutator{L_n}{\phi(w,\overline{w})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{w}\differential z\,z^{n+1}T(z)\phi(w,\overline{w})

(3.115)

\displaystyle=h(n+1)w^{n}\phi(w,\overline{w})+w^{n+1}\partial\phi(w,\overline{w})\,.

With
w\rightarrow 0
above, we immediately reach the conclusion that
\ket{h,\overline{h}}
is an eigenstate of
L_{0}
.

L_{0}\ket{h,\overline{h}}=h\ket{h,\overline{h}}\,,\qquad\overline{L}_{0}\ket{h,\overline{h}}=\overline{h}\ket{h,\overline{h}}\,.

(3.116)

Likewise, we have for
n>0

L_{n}\ket{h,\overline{h}}=0\,,\qquad\overline{L}_{n}\ket{h, \overline{h}}=0\,.\qquad(n>0)

(3.117)

The generators
L_{-m}(m>0)
also increase the conformal dimension, by virtue of the Virasoro algebra (
3.112
):

\commutator{L_0}{L_{-m}}=mL_{-m}\,.

(3.118)

This means the excited state with higher conformal dimensions can be obtained by successively applying of
L_{-m}
on
\ket{h}

L_{-\lambda}\ket{h}\equiv L_{-\lambda_{1}}L_{-\lambda_{2}}\cdots L_{-\lambda_{n}}\ket{h}\,,\qquad(\lambda=\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\geq 1)

(3.119)

where
L_{-\lambda_{i}}
appear in decresing order by convention. The states defined in (
3.119
) are called descendants of
\ket{h}
, with eigenvalue of
L_{0}

h^{\prime}=h+|\lambda|\equiv h+N\,,

(3.120)

where
N=|\lambda|=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}
is called the level of the descendant. The subset of the full Hilbert space, generated by the asymptotic state
|h\rangle
and its descendants, is closed under the action of the Virasoro algebra. This subspace forms a representation of the Virasoro algebra and is referred to as a Verma module.

l

p(l)

0

1

\ket{h}

1

1

L_{-1}\ket{h}

2

2

L^{2}_{-1}\ket{h},L_{-2}\ket{h}

3

3

L^{3}_{-1}\ket{h},L_{-2}L_{-1}\ket{h},L_{-3}\ket{h}

4

5

L^{4}_{-1}\ket{h},L_{-2}L^{2}_{-1}\ket{h},L_{-3}L_{-1}\ket{h}.L^{2}_{-2}\ket{h},L_{-4}\ket{h}

Table 2:
States of a Verma module

Table
2
displays the states within a Verma module up to level four, where
p(l)
represents the number of linearly independent states at level
l
. This number corresponds to the partitions of the integer
l
. We can also define the generating function of the Verma module as

\textrm{Tr}(q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}})=\frac{1}{\eta(q)}\equiv q^{-\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1-q^{n}}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}p(l)q^{l-\frac{1}{24}}\,,

(3.121)

which can be also interpreted as the generating function of the number
p(l)
of the partition of
l
. The function

\eta(q):=q^{1/24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})

is called the
Dedekind
\eta
-function
.

The inner product is defined following the previous definition of the Hermitian conjugate (
3.98
):
L_{m}^{\dagger}=L_{-m}
. Therefore the dual state
\ket{h}
satisfies

\bra{h}L_{j}=0\,.\qquad(j<0)

(3.122)

Then the inner product of two states
L_{-\mu}\ket{h}
and
L_{-\lambda}\ket{h}
is

\bra{h}L_{\mu_{m}}\cdots L_{\mu_{1}}L_{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots L_{-\lambda_{n}}\ket{h}\,.

(3.123)

This product can be evaluated using the Virasoro algebra. The inner product between two states vanishes unless they belong to the same level, and it depends solely on the highest weight
h
and the central charge
c
. For the representation theory to be unitary, meaning it contains no negative-norm states, there must be strict constraints on
(h,c)
. For example, consider the norm of the state
L_{-n}|h\rangle

\displaystyle\bra{h}L_{n}L_{-n}\ket{h}=\bigg[2nh+\frac{1}{12}cn(n^{2}-1)\bigg]\bra{h}\ket{h}.

(3.124)

It is easy to see that the norm becomes negative for
n
sufficiently large if
c<0
. Moreover, for
n=1
, we find that the theory with negative conformal dimensions is not unitary.

4
Free boson and free fermion

In this section, we are going to demonstrate more simple examples in CFT.

4.1
Free boson

In the previous sections, we have learned the calculation of some basic OPEs in the massless free boson theory. We will further study this theory. We also refer the reader to
[
FMS97
, §6]
,
[
Gin88a
, §6,§7]
and
[
BP09
, §2]
.

Mode Expansion

First, let us recall that the action of free boson (
3.68
)

\mathcal{S}=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial^{\mu}\varphi\,.

(4.1)

First, we consider the free boson on the cylinder, in Minkowski space-time. The fact that the fields
\varphi
live on the cylinder implies that they may satisfy periodic boundary conditions:

\varphi(x+\ell,t)=\varphi(x,t)\,

(4.2)

where
L
is the circumference of the cylinder. The classical equation of motion for
\varphi
is

[\partial_{t}^{2}-\partial_{x}^{2}]\varphi(x,t)=0\,.

(4.3)

It is a good exercise to verify that
\varphi
of the following form satisfies the above two conditions:

\varphi_{\text{cyl}}=\varphi_{0}+\frac{4\pi}{\ell}\pi_{0}t+i\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\bigg(a_{n}e^{-2\pi in(t-x)/\ell}+\overline{a}_{n}e^{-2\pi in(t+x)/\ell}\bigg)\,

(4.4)

where ’cyl’ means the field defined on the cylinder. The canonical momentum for the theory is

\Pi=\frac{\delta\mathcal{S}}{\delta(\partial_{t}\varphi)}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\partial_{t}\varphi\,,

(4.5)

For quantum fields, we impose the following canonical commutation relations

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\commutator{\varphi(x,t)}{\Pi(y,t)}=i\delta(x-y)\,,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\commutator{\varphi(x,t)}{\varphi(y,t)}=0\,,

\displaystyle\commutator{\Pi(x,t)}{\Pi(y,t)}=0\,,

In terms of the modes in (
4.4
), these are equivalent to

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\commutator{a_k}{a_l}=\commutator{\overline{a}_k}{\overline{a}_l}=k\delta_{k+l,0}\,,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\commutator{a_k}{\overline{a}_l}=0\,,

for
k,l\neq 0
, and

\commutator{\varphi_0}{\pi_0}=i\,.

(4.8)

The Hamiltonian for free boson theory is

H=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int\differential x[:(\partial_{t}\varphi)^{2}:+:(\partial_{x}\varphi)^{2}:]\,,

(4.9)

Plugging the mode expansion (
4.4
), the Hamiltonian is then expressed as

H=\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\pi^{2}_{0}+\frac{\pi}{\ell}\sum_{n\neq 0}(:a_{-n}a_{n}:+:\overline{a}_{-n}\overline{a}_{n}:)\,,

(4.10)

The commutation relations (
4.7
) lead to the relation

\commutator{H}{a_{-m}}=\frac{2\pi}{\ell}ma_{-m}\,,

(4.11)

which means
a_{-m}(m>0)
act as creation operators. When applied to an eigenstate
|E\rangle
of
H
of energy
E|E\rangle
, the state
a_{-m}|E\rangle
is another eigenstate with energy
(E+2m\pi/\ell)a_{-m}|E\rangle
. For the same reason,
a_{m}(m>0)
is an annihilation operator.

Relation between Cylinder and Plane

Now, we move to Euclidean space-time (
t\rightarrow-it
) by taking
w=t-ix
and
\overline{w}=t+ix
. The mode expansion of boson field on the cylinder now is then

\varphi_{\text{cyl}}(w,\overline{w})=\varphi_{0}-i\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\pi_{0}(w+\overline{w})+i\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\bigg(a_{n}e^{-2\pi nw/\ell}+\overline{a}_{n}e^{-2\pi n\overline{w})/\ell}\bigg)\,,

(4.12)

with now
w\sim w+i\ell
. Using a conformal transformation as in Figure
7
, we map all the operators from the cylinder to the complex plane:

z=e^{2\pi w/\ell}\,,\qquad\overline{z}=e^{2\pi\overline{w}/\ell}\,.

(4.13)

Since
\pi
is invariant under conformal transformations, we finally obtain the expansion by simply replacing
w
by
z
.

\varphi_{\text{pl}}(z,\overline{z})=\varphi_{0}-i\pi_{0}\ln(z\overline{z})+i\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}(a_{n}z^{-n}+\overline{a}_{n}\overline{z}^{-n})\,.

(4.14)

Now we concentrate on the holomorphic field
\partial\varphi

i\partial\varphi(z)=\frac{\pi_{0}}{z}+\sum_{n\neq 0}a_{n}z^{-n-1}\,.

(4.15)

We may write the zero modes by
a_{0}
and
\overline{a}_{0}
:

a_{0}\equiv\overline{a}_{0}\equiv\pi_{0}

(4.16)

Now the commutation relations of (
4.7
) can be extended to include the zero mode operator without changing the form of the algebra. The mode expansion of
\partial\varphi
is consistent with the expansion of a primary field with
h=1
(
3.101
):

i\partial\varphi(z)=\sum_{n}a_{n}z^{-n-1}\,.

(4.17)

We can calculate a two-point function by using mode expansion. For
(\absolutevalue{z}>\absolutevalue{w})
:

\expectationvalue{\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(w)}=\sum_{m,n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\expectationvalue{a_n a_m}z^{-n}w^{-m-1}\,.

(4.18)

According to the commutation relation (
4.7
) and the fact that
a_{m}
and
a_{-m}
are annihilation and creation operators respectively, it follows that

\expectationvalue{\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(w)}=\frac{1}{w}\frac{w/z}{1-w/z}=\frac{1}{z-w}\,.

(4.19)

Its differentiation with respect to
z
provides the two-point function.

\expectationvalue{\partial\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(w)}=-\frac{1}{(z-w)^{2}}\,.

(4.20)

The holomorphic energy-momentum tensor is given by

T(z)=-\frac{1}{2}:\partial\varphi(z)\partial\varphi(z):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-m-2}:a_{n}a_{m}:\,,

(4.21)

which implies

\displaystyle L_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}:a_{n-m}a_{m}:\,\qquad(n\neq 0)

(4.22)

\displaystyle L_{0}=\sum_{n>0}a_{-n}a_{n}+\frac{1}{2}a_{0}^{2}~.

The Hamiltonian (
4.10
) now can be written as,

H=\frac{2\pi}{\ell}(L_{0}+\overline{L}_{0})\,.

(4.23)

This confirms the role of
L_{0}
and
\overline{L}_{0}
as a Hamiltonian. Since we place the free boson on a cylinder of size
\ell
, energy is proportional to
\frac{2\pi}{\ell}
, which is called the
finite-size scaling

[
Car84b
,
Car84c
,
Car86a
, ISZ88-No.18,19,25]
. The mode operator
a_{m}
plays a similar role to
L_{m}
with respect to
L_{0}
, because of the commutation relation
\commutator{L_0}{a_{-m}}=ma_{-m}
. Therefore its effect on the conformal dimension is the same as that of
L_{m}
.

By definition, the normal ordering prescription implies that
\expectationvalue{T(z)}=0
.
4
4

4

However, as we will see in the next section, this is not the case for the fermionic theory with anti-periodic boundary condition.
We can always define the normal ordering product by subtracting all the singular terms from the OPE. In fact the general expectation value for energy-momentum tensor can be written as

\expectationvalue{T(z)}=-\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\bigg(\expectationvalue{\partial\varphi(z+\e) \partial\varphi(z)}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg)\,.

(4.24)

We will see that this relation is useful when we consider the anti-periodic boundary condition later. By plugging in to the two-point function, we see that
\expectationvalue{T(z)}=0
, which implies that
(L_{0})_{\text{pl}}
vanishes on the vacuum. We now map the theory back to a cylinder
z\rightarrow w=\frac{\ell}{2\pi}\ln z
, by using (
3.89
):

T_{\text{cyl}}(w)=\bigg(\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\bigg)^{2}\bigg[T_{\text{pl}}(z)z^{2}-\frac{1}{24}\bigg]\,.

(4.25)

where we use the central charge
c=1
for the free boson. Taking the expectation value on both sides, we have

\expectationvalue{T_{\text{cyl}}}=-\frac{1}{24}\bigg(\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\bigg)^{2}\,.

(4.26)

This implies

L_{0,\text{cyl}}=\frac{1}{2}a_{0}^{2}+\sum_{n>0}a_{-n}a_{n}-\frac{1}{24}\,.

(4.27)

The Hamiltonian is now written as

H=\frac{2\pi}{\ell}(L_{0,\text{cyl}}+\overline{L}_{0,\text{cyl}})\,.

(4.28)

Actually, in the general case, Hamiltonian of a theory defined on a cylinder with central charge
c
can be written as

H=\frac{2\pi}{\ell}(L_{0,\text{pl}}+\overline{L}_{0,\text{pl}}-\frac{c}{12})\,.

(4.29)

Thus, we can infer that the central charge
c
shows up as the vacuum energy of a theory on a cylinder, and this is one instance of finite-size effects
[
BCN86
, ISZ88-No.20]
.

4.2
Free fermion

OPEs in Free fermion

Now we consider another simple model: free fermion. In two dimensions, the action of a free Majorana fermion is

\mathcal{S}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\Psi^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Psi\,,

(4.30)

where the gamma matrices
\gamma^{\mu}
satisfy the so-called
Clifford algebra
:

\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=2\eta^{\eta\nu}\,,

(4.31)

and we impose the
Majorana condition

(\Psi^{*}=\Psi)
to the fermionic field to remove a half of the degrees of freedom. In the Euclidean space
\eta^{\mu\nu}=\text{diag}(1,1)
, we take a basis of Dirac matrices as

\gamma^{0}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & 1\\ 1 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)\,,\qquad\gamma^{1}=i\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & -1 \\ 1 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)\,.

(4.32)

Using this basis, we can express the action as

\mathcal{S}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\differential^{2}x(\overline{\psi}\partial\overline{\psi}+\psi\overline{\partial}\psi)\,,

(4.33)

where we write the two-component spinor
\Psi
as
(\psi,\overline{\psi})
. Since the equations of motion are
\partial\bar{\psi}=0
and
\overline{\partial}\psi=0
,
\psi(z)
and
\overline{\psi}(\overline{z})
holomorphic and antiholomorphic field, respectively.

Now let us calculate the two-point function as in the free fermion

K_{ij}=\expectationvalue{\Psi_i(x)\Psi_j(y)}\quad(i,j=1,2)\,.

(4.34)

The action can be expressed by

\mathcal{S}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\differential^{2}y\Psi_{i}(x)A_{ij}(x,y)\Psi_{j}(y)\,,

(4.35)

where the kernel is

A_{ij}(x,y)=\delta(x-y)(\gamma^{0}\gamma^{\mu})_{ij}\partial_{\mu}\,.

(4.36)

Recalling (
3.67
), the propagator
K_{ij}
is the inverse of
A_{ij}
. Therefore, we can write the equation for
K
as

\delta(x-y)(\gamma^{0}\gamma^{\mu})_{ik}\partialderivative{x^\mu}K_{kj}(x,y)=\delta(x-y)\delta_{ij}\,.

(4.37)

In terms of complex coordinates, this becomes

\frac{1}{\pi}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\overline{\partial} & 0 \\ 0 &\partial\endlx@physics@matrix)\left(\begin{array}[]{l l}{\langle\psi(z,\overline{z})\psi(w,\overline{w})\rangle}&{\langle\psi(z,\overline{z})\overline{\psi}(w,\overline{w})\rangle}\\
{\langle\overline{\psi}(z,\overline{z})\psi(w,\overline{w}))}&{\langle\overline{\psi}(z,\overline{z})\overline{\psi}(w,\overline{w})\rangle}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{\pi}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \overline{\partial}\frac{1}{z-w} & 0 \\ 0 & \partial\frac{1}{\overline{z}-\overline{w}} \endlx@physics@matrix)\,,

(4.38)

where we have used the complex form of the
\delta
-function:

\delta(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\overline{\partial}\frac{1}{z}=\frac{1}{\pi}\partial\frac{1}{\overline{z}}~.

Therefore, we obtain the two-point functions for the fermionic fields

\displaystyle\langle\psi(z,\overline{z})\psi(w,\overline{w})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z-w}\,,

(4.39)

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\psi}(z,\overline{z})\overline{\psi}(w,\overline{w})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{z}-\overline{w}}\,,

\displaystyle\langle\psi(z,\overline{z})\overline{\psi}(w,\overline{w})\rangle

\displaystyle=0\,.

Thus the OPE of two holomorphic fields can be written as:

\psi(z)\psi(w)\sim\frac{1}{z-w}

(4.40)

In order to see whether the fermion field is a primary field or not, we can calculate its OPE with the energy-momentum tensor. By using (
3.20
) in complex-coordinate form, we can calculate all the components of the energy-momentum tensor.

\displaystyle T^{\overline{z}\overline{z}}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial(\overline{\partial}\Phi)}\partial\Phi=\frac{1}{\pi}\psi\partial\psi\,,

(4.41)

\displaystyle T^{zz}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial(\partial\Phi)}\overline{\partial}\Phi=\frac{1}{\pi}\overline{\psi}\overline{\partial}\overline{\psi}\,,

\displaystyle T^{z\overline{z}}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial(\partial\Phi)}\partial\Phi-2\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\psi\overline{\partial}\psi\,.

The traceless condition
T^{z\overline{z}}
is preserved when taking into account the equation of motion, as we have discussed. The holomorphic part is defined as:

T(z)=-\frac{1}{2}:\psi(z)\partial\psi(z):\,.

(4.42)

The normal-ordering product can be written in an equivalent way for the free field as follow:

:\psi\partial\psi:(z)=\lim_{w\rightarrow z}(\psi(z)\partial\psi(w)-\langle\psi(z)\partial\psi(w)\rangle)\,,

(4.43)

which is the same expression (
4.24
) as in bosonic field theory. Then we can calculate the OPE between the fermion field and energy-momentum tensor directly.

T(z)\psi(w)\sim\frac{\frac{1}{2}\psi(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial\psi(w)}{z-w}\,.

(4.44)

This immediately tells us that in the free fermion model,
\psi
is a primary field with conformal dimension
1/2
.
TT
OPE can also be obtained directly by calculation.

T(z)T(w)\sim\frac{1/4}{(z-w)^{4}}+\frac{2T(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial T(w)}{(z-w)}\,.

(4.45)

This expression verifies the general form of
TT
OPE (
3.87
), with
1/2
the central charge in this model.

Mode Expansion

Next, we examine the free fermion field on a cylinder, following an approach similar to that used for the boson field. We consider a cylinder with circumference
L
, applying two distinct types of boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\psi(x+\ell)\equiv\psi(x)

\displaystyle\text{Ramond}(\mathrm{R})\,,

(4.46)

\displaystyle\psi(x+\ell)\equiv-\psi(x)

\displaystyle\text{Neveu-Schwarz (NS)}\,.

Analogous to the case of free bosons, the mode expansion for the free fermion is given by:

\psi(x,t)=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\ell}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}+\nu}b_{k}e^{-2\pi kw/\ell}\,.

(4.47)

In the periodic case (R),
k
takes integer values (
n=0
), while in the anti-periodic case (NS),
k
assumes half-integer values (
n=\frac{1}{2}
). As before, we introduce the complex coordinate
w=t_{E}-ix
, where
t_{E}
represents Euclidean time. For quantum fermionic fields, the canonical anti-commutation relation is introduced as follows

\left\{b_{k},b_{q}\right\}=\delta_{k+q,0}\,,

(4.48)

where
b_{-k}
for
k>0
can be considered as a creation operator and
b_{k}
for
k>0
as an annihilation one. Note that, in the R sector, there exists a zero mode
b_{0}
which leads to a degeneracy of the vacuum.

Mapping to Complex Plane

By applying the coordinate transformation
z=e^{{2\pi w}/{L}}
, the cylinder is mapped onto the plane. Unlike in the free boson theory, the field
y
possesses a conformal dimension of
1/2
, which introduces a prefactor in accordance with the conformal transformation properties of a primary field (
3.40
):

\psi_{\text{cyl}}(w)\rightarrow\psi_{\text{pl}}(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{2\pi z}}\psi_{\text{cyl}}(z)\,.

(4.49)

Therefore, on the plane, the mode expansion changes to

\psi(z)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}+\nu}b_{k}z^{-k-1/2}\,.

(4.50)

This coincides with the general expansion formula for a primary field (
3.101
). Now the prefactor
z^{-1/2}
has interchanged the role of two boundary conditions: The NS condition corresponds to a periodic field (
\nu=\frac{1}{2})
and the R condition to an anti-periodic field (
\nu=0
)

\displaystyle\psi\left(e^{2\pi i}z\right)

\displaystyle=-\psi(z)\quad\text{Ramond (R)}\,,

(4.51)

\displaystyle\psi\left(e^{2\pi i}z\right)

\displaystyle=\psi(z)\quad\text{Neveu-Schwarz (NS)}\,.

We now calculate the two-point function in the NS sector by using the mode expansion:

\langle\psi(z)\psi(w)\rangle=\sum_{k,{q}\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2}z^{-k-1/2}w^{-q-1/2}\left\langle b_{k}b_{q}\right\rangle\,.

(4.52)

Applying the commutation relations (
4.48
), this can be calculated:

\langle\psi(z)\psi(w)\rangle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2,k>0}z^{-k-1/2}w^{k-1/2}=\frac{1}{z-w}\,.

(4.53)

The final result is the same as the two-point function for fermions we have derived before. However, in the Ramond sector, the two-point function is different.

\langle\psi(z)\psi(w)\rangle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{zw}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}z^{-k-1/2}w^{k-1/2}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sqrt{z/w}+\sqrt{w/z}}{z-w}\,.

(4.54)

When
w\rightarrow z
, this result coincides with the previous one. So, the boundary condition does not affect the two-point function locally. However, globally when
z
or
w
is taken around the origin, there arises a minus sign due to the boundary condition. We find that different boundary conditions lead to different two-point functions on the plane.

Vacuum Expectation Value

In this section, we will see the boundary condition will affect the vacuum expectation of the energy-momentum tensor and finally, affect the vacuum energy. As we have mentioned before, the energy-momentum tensor can be calculated by the following normal-ordering prescription:

\langle T(z)\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left(-\langle\psi(z+\varepsilon)\partial\psi(z)\rangle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\,.

(4.55)

In the NS sector, the vacuum expectation value of the energy-momentum tensor vanishes as before.

\expectationvalue{T(z)}=0\,.

(4.56)

However, in the R sector, the calculation yields:

\langle T(z)\rangle=-\frac{1}{4}\lim_{w\rightarrow z}\partial_{w}\left(\frac{\sqrt{z/w}+\sqrt{w/z}}{z-w}\right)+\frac{1}{2(z-w)^{2}}=\frac{1}{16z^{2}}\,.

(4.57)

This means boundary conditions do affect the expectation value of the energy-momentum tensor. Now plugging the mode expansion expression (
4.50
) into the energy-momentum tensor (
4.42
):

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{pl.}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,q\in\mathbb{Z}+\nu}\left(k+\frac{1}{2}\right)z^{-q-1/2}z^{-k-3/2}:b_{q}b_{k}:

(4.58)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},k\in\mathbb{Z}+\nu}\left(k+\frac{1}{2}\right)z^{-n-2}:b_{n-k}b_{k}:\,.

We can easily extract the conformal generator from the expression above:

L_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}+\nu}\left(k+\frac{1}{2}\right):b_{n-k}b_{k}:~.

(4.59)

We can write
L_{0}
as follows by using the commutation relations (
4.48
).

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0}kb_{-k}b_{k}\quad\left(\mathrm{NS}:k\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\,,

(4.60)

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k>0}kb_{-k}b_{k}+\frac{1}{16}\quad(\mathrm{R}:k\in\mathbb{Z})\,,

where the constant term is fixed from the vacuum expectation value of energy-momentum tensor for NS and R sector, since
L_{0}
is the coefficient of
1/z^{2}
in the expansion. As mentioned before, the Hamiltonian of such a theory defined on the cylinder can be determined by the following expression with
L_{0}
on the complex plane (
4.29
) with
c
the central charge which is
1/2
, in this theory. From the relation above, we can immediately obtain the vacuum energy in the holomorphic part as follows:

\frac{\ell}{2\pi}E_{0}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}{-\frac{1}{48}}&{\text{NS sector}}\\
{+\frac{1}{24}}&{\text{R sector}}\end{array}\right.

(4.61)

The same result can be obtained by mapping the energy-momentum tensor from the complex plane to the cylinder, by using (
3.89
):

T_{\text{cyl}}(w)=\bigg(\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\bigg)^{2}\bigg[T_{\text{pl}}(z)z^{2}-\frac{c}{24}\bigg]\,,

(4.62)

with
c=1/2
. Taking expectation value from both sides of above, and then treating NS sector and R sector respectively, we find

\left\langle T_{\mathrm{cyl.}}\right\rangle=\left\{\begin{aligned} -\frac{1}{48}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\right)^{2}&\text{NS sector}\\
\frac{1}{24}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\right)^{2}&\text{R sector}\end{aligned}\right.

(4.63)

This implies the same vacuum energy as before.

4.3
Torus partition functions

Moduli of a two-torus

In this chapter, we are going to study CFTs defined on a torus
T^{2}
. The easiest way to obtain such a theory while using the previous result is to
cut out a finite piece of the infinite cylinder described by

w=x^{0}+ix^{1}
, where
x^{0}
is the Euclidean time, and we identify
the circumference of cylinder
l\equiv 2\pi
in this section.
Thus, the conformal mapping
z=e^{w}
defines a theory
from the cylinder to a complex plane.
We will now perform the compactification to the torus. A
torus can be defined by identifying points
w
in the complex plane

\mathbb{C}
as

w\sim w+m\alpha_{1}+n\alpha_{2}\,,\qquad m,n\in\mathbb{Z}

(4.64)

where
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is a pair of complex numbers. This pair
spans a lattice whose smallest cell illustrates the shape of a torus as in Figure
9
. Thus, we can introduce the
complex structure

\tau=\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}}=\tau_{1}+i\tau_{2}\,,

(4.65)

which parametrizes the shape of a torus.
However, there are various choices of pairs
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
giving the
same lattice and thus the same torus. If the two pairs
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
and
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
describe the same lattice, then they are related by an invertible
2\times 2
matrix with integral entries

\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\beta_2 \\
\beta_1\endlx@physics@matrix)=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\
c & d\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\alpha_2 \\
\alpha_1\endlx@physics@matrix)\,,\qquad a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(4.66)

Moreover, because the area is preserved, we have
ad-bc=1
so that the matrix is an element of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Furthermore, the torus illustrated by
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is the same as the one by
(-\alpha_{1},-\alpha_{2})
, so that the transformation by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
is trivial. All in all, the symmetry of the lattice with fixed shape is
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which is called the
modular group
or the
mapping class group
of the torus. Finally, by choosing

(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1,\tau)
, a modular group acts on the complex structure of the torus as

\tau
as

\tau\mapsto\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\quad\text{with}\quad\left(\begin{array}[]{l l}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{array}\right)\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}~.

(4.67)

Figure 9:
Lattice of a torus

Now let us look at some particular cases.
T
-transformation:

T:\tau\to\tau+1\,,

(4.68)

which makes the lattice
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1,\tau)
to
(1,\tau+1)
, thus defining the same lattice. And
U
-transformation:

U:\tau\to\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}\,,

(4.69)

which makes the lattice
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1,\tau)
to
(1+\tau,\tau)
, thus defining the same lattice. However, it seems more convenient to work with
S
- transformation instead of
U
-transformation.

S:\tau\to-\frac{1}{\tau}

(4.70)

which interchanges the lattice
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\leftrightarrow(-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1})
. One can easily show that

S=UT^{-1}U\,,\qquad S^{2}=\mathbb{1}\,\qquad(ST)^{3}=\mathbb{1}\,.

(4.71)

In fact,
S
and
T
can prove to be the generators of the modular group
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Therefore, it is sufficient to check whether a theory is modular invariant by studying its behavior under
T
and
S
transformations.

Figure 10:
T
-transformation

Figure 11:
U
-transformation

Torus partition function

Now we will define a torus partition function
\mathcal{Z}
in terms of Virasoro generators. The story is analogous to the partition function in statistical mechanics

\mathcal{Z}=\sum_{\textrm{all states}}\exp(-\beta H)~.

(4.72)

The sum over all states can be understood as identifying the Hilbert space at
t=0
and
t=\beta=1/T
. This is equivalent to compactifying the Euclidean time on a circle of radius
\beta=1/T
.

We can similarly define a partition function on a torus with complex structure
\tau=\tau_{1}+i\tau_{2}
(
4.65
). First, we make the time translation of length
\tau_{2}
and the spatial translation of length
\tau_{1}
, and then we identify the Hilbert space. This can be defined by the Hamiltonian formalism as

\mathcal{Z}=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(e^{-2\pi\tau_{2}H}e^{2\pi\tau_{1}P}\right)~.

(4.73)

Here the trace is taken over all states in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of the theory. Since we can write
the Hamiltonian and momentum are written as the zero modes of Virasoro generator (
3.92
),

H_{\textrm{cyl}}=L_{0,\textrm{cyl}}+\overline{L}_{0,\textrm{cyl}},\quad P_{\textrm{cyl}}=i\left(L_{0,\textrm{cyl}}-\overline{L}_{0,\textrm{cyl}}\right).

(4.74)

the partition function can be expressed as

\mathcal{Z}=\mbox{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(e^{2\pi i\tau L_{0,\textrm{cyl}}}e^{-2\pi i\overline{\tau}\overline{L}_{0,\textrm{cyl}}}\right)~.

(4.75)

The conformal transformation (
4.25
) tells us that the Virasoro generators on a cylinder and on the plane are related by
L_{0,\textrm{cyl}}=L_{0,\textrm{pl}}-\frac{c}{24}
. (The same applies to the anti-holomorphic part.)
By writing defining
q=\exp(2\pi i \tau)
, the torus partition function can be written as

\mathcal{Z}=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}}\right)~.

(4.76)

Here we omit the subscript
pl
.
Note that (the holomorphic part of) this expression is equivalent to the generating function (
3.121
) of the Verma module. As we will see later, the partition function is indeed expressed in terms of the characters of irreducible representations

\mathcal{Z}(\tau)=\sum_{(h,\bar{h})}\mathcal{N}_{h\bar{h}}\chi_{h}(\tau)\bar{\chi}_{\bar{h}}(\bar{\tau})~,

(4.77)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\overline{h}h}
is the multiplicity of the irreducible representation labelled by
(h,\overline{h})
in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.

Free boson

Let us compute the torus partition function in the simplest example: free boson. The computation is similar to In free boson, it follows from (
4.10
) that
L_{0}
is expressed as the modes
a_{m}

L_{0}=\frac{1}{2}a_{0}a_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{-k}a_{k}~.

(4.78)

In addition, the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of the free boson is the Fock space which can be obtained by acting with creation operators
a_{-k}
for
k>0
on the vacuum
|0\rangle
. From (
4.7
), we can derive

[L_{0},a_{-k}]=ka_{-k}~.

(4.79)

Thus, using the geometric progression
(1-x)^{-1}=1+x+x^{2}+\ldots
, the contribution from states
\bigoplus_{\ell}a_{-k}^{\ell}|0\rangle
to the partition function can be read off as

\Tr_{\bigoplus_{\ell}a_{-k}^{\ell}|0\rangle}q^{L_{0}}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}q^{\ell k}=\frac{1}{1-q^{k}}~.

Since a general state in the Fock space can be written as

|n_{1},n_{2},n_{3},\cdots\rangle=a_{-1}^{n_{1}}a_{-2}^{n_{2}}\cdots|0\rangle,\quad\mbox{with}\quad n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}~,

(4.80)

the holomorphic part of the torus partition function is

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{1}{24}}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\cdots\left\langle n_{1},n_{2},\cdots\left|q^{L_{0}}\right|n_{1},n_{2},\cdots\right\rangle

(4.81)

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1-q^{k}}.

This is exactly the Dedekind
\eta
-function (
4.99
), and including the anti-holomorphic contribution, we have therefore found the partition function

\mathcal{Z}_{\textrm{B}}^{\prime}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\frac{1}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}.

(4.82)

However, as the modular transformations of the Dedekind
\eta
-function are given in (
4.100
), this is
not
invariant under the
S
-transformation. To make it modular-invariant, we need to consider the contribution from the zero mode
a_{0}
.
As in (
4.79
),
a_{0}
commutes with
L_{0}
so that this can be regarded as a free parameter
a_{0}|0\rangle:=|a_{0}\rangle
. The trace over the Hilbert space means that we need to integrate over all the zero modes:

\int da_{0}(q\bar{q})^{\frac{a_{0}^{2}}{2}}=\int da_{0}e^{2\pi i\tau_{2}a_{0}^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\tau_{2}}}

(4.83)

Multiplying this factor to (
4.82
), we finally obtain the modular invariant partition function of the free boson

\mathcal{Z}_{\textrm{B}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\tau_{2}}|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}~.

(4.84)

Free boson on a circle

Figure 12:
Left: free boson. Right: free boson on a circle.

The free boson considered above can be understood as a linear sigma model where a bosonic field
\varphi:T^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a map from a torus
T^{2}
to a line
\mathbb{R}
.
As another example, we consider the free boson compactified on a circle
S^{1}
of radius
R
. This model can be interpreted as a linear sigma model
\varphi:T^{2}\to S^{1}
from a torus
T^{2}
to a circle
S^{1}
of radius
R
. (See Figure
12
.) This is equivalent to imposing the periodicity on the bosonic field

\varphi(z,\bar{z})\sim\varphi(z,\bar{z})+2\pi Rm,\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}

(4.85)

To see how this compactification changes the partition function, let us recall the mode expansion (
4.12
) of the bosonic field
\varphi
:

\varphi(w,\overline{w})=\varphi_{0}-i\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\left[(a_{0}+\overline{a}_{0})t-i(a_{0}-\overline{a}_{0})x\right]+i\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\bigg(a_{n}e^{2\pi nw/\ell}+\overline{a}_{n}e^{2\pi n\overline{w})/\ell}\bigg)\,.

(4.86)

Since we map
T^{2}
to
S^{1}
, we can impose the following periodicity to the field
\varphi
under the rotation
x\to x+\ell
on a spatial circle of the torus
T^{2}

\varphi(t,x+\ell)=\varphi(t,x)+2\pi Rw\quad w\in\mathbb{Z}~,\

(4.87)

Since
w
counts how many times the spatial circle of the torus wraps on the target
S^{1}
, it is called the
winding number
. (Note that
w
here is no longer the coordinate of the torus, so do not mix them up.)
Substituting this condition into (
4.86
), we obtain the constraint

a_{0}-\overline{a}_{0}=Rw~\quad w\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.88)

In addition, it follows from (
4.86
) that the center of mass momentum of the string is
p=(a_{0}+\overline{a}_{0})/2
. Since a boson field is compactified on a circle, the momentum is quantized as

a_{0}+\overline{a}_{0}=\frac{2n}{R}~\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}~,

where
n
is called the
Kaluza-Klein momentum
.

Thus, a vacuum state is labeled by the winding number and Kaluza-Klein momentum as
|n,w\rangle
where

a_{0}|n,w\rangle=\left(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{Rw}{2}\right)|n,w\rangle,\;\;\;\;\;\;\overline{a}_{0}|n,w\rangle=\left(\frac{n}{R}-\frac{Rw}{2}\right)|n,w\rangle~.

The contributions from descendants are as before (
4.82
). However, we now sum over all the zero modes
a_{0}
and
\overline{a}_{0}
, which is
[
Yan87
, ISZ88-No.40]

[
DFSZ87
, ISZ88-No.43]

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\frac{1}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\sum_{n,w}q^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{Rw}{2}\right)^{2}}\overline{q}^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}-\frac{Rw}{2}\right)^{2}}~.

(4.89)

It is straightforward to check that it is invariant under the
T
-transformation. The invariance under the
S
-transformation requires the
Poisson resummation formula

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\exp\left(-\pi an^{2}+bn\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\exp\left(-\frac{\pi}{a}\left(k+\frac{b}{2\pi i}\right)^{2}\right)~.

(4.90)

The derivation of the invariance under the modular
S
-transformation is left as an exercise.

The astute student notices that the partition function (
4.89
) enjoys the identity

\mathcal{Z}_{2/R}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\tau,\overline{\tau})~,

(4.91)

which is called
T
-
duality
. T-duality plays an important role in string theory. This means that closed strings cannot distinguish circles of radii
R
and
2/R
.
This shows that closed strings see geometry very differently from the way that we see it conventionally. The self-dual radius
R=\sqrt{2}
can be interpreted as a minimal length scale of a circle in closed strings where the symmetry is enhanced from U(1) to SU(2) as we will see in §
7.2
.

Free fermions

Next, let us consider the torus partition function of the free fermion. The mode expansion for a free fermion
\psi(z)
is given in (
4.50
), and for Neveu-Schwarz boundary conditions, the modes
b_{k}
take purely half-integer values
k\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
. On a torus with the coordinate
w
, this expansion corresponds to anti-periodic boundary condition along the spatial circle.
A general state in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{NS}
of this theory is obtained by acting creation operators
b_{-s}
on the vacuum
|0\rangle

|n_{\frac{1}{2}},n_{\frac{3}{2}}\cdots\rangle=(b_{-\frac{1}{2}})^{n_{\frac{1}{2}}}(b_{-\frac{3}{2}})^{n_{\frac{3}{2}}}\cdots|0\rangle,

(4.92)

where each occupation number must be
n_{s}=0,1
due to the fermion-statistics.

As before, we need to evaluate the eigenvalues of
L_{0}=\sum_{s=\frac{1}{2}}^{\infty}sb_{-s}b_{s}
in (
4.60
). Using the anti-commutation relation (
4.48
) of the fermionic modes, we have

[L_{0},b_{k}]=-kb_{k}~.

Thus, it is straightforward to compute the character

\displaystyle\chi_{AA}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{NS}}\left(q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right)

(4.93)

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{1}{48}}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{1}\sum_{\frac{3}{2}=0}^{1}\cdots\left\langle n_{\frac{1}{2}},n_{\frac{3}{2}}\cdots\left|q^{L_{0}}\right|n_{\frac{1}{2}},n_{\frac{3}{2}}\cdots\right\rangle

\displaystyle=q^{-\frac{1}{48}}\prod_{r=0}^{\infty}\left(1+q^{r+\frac{1}{2}}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}}

For the fermionic character, we impose the anti-periodic boundary condition on the time circle of the torus. The (
AA
) notation indicates that the anti-periodic boundary condition is imposed on both the time and spatial circle of the torus.

Let us investigate the property of (
4.93
) under the modular transformation. Using the properties (
4.100
) of
\eta
and
\vartheta
under the modular transformations, we have

\displaystyle S:\chi_{AA}(-1/\tau)=\chi_{AA}(\tau)

(4.94)

\displaystyle T:\chi_{AA}(\tau+1)=e^{-\frac{i\pi}{24}}\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}}.

Although the
S
-transformation exchanges the time and spatial circle of the torus, Since we impose the same anti-periodic boundary condition on both circles, the
S
-transformation does not change the boundary conditions. On the other hand, the
T
-transformation maps the time circle to the (1,1)-cycle of the torus as illustrated in Figure
11
. The periodic boundary condition is imposed on the (1,1)-cycle because imposing the anti-periodic boundary conditions twice ends up with the periodic boundary condition,
i.e.

A\times A=P
. Hence, the
T
-transformation changes the boundary condition, which results in a different
\vartheta
-function.

In order to construct a modular invariant partition function, we need to take into account all the boundary conditions. To this end, we introduce the fermion number operator
F
which assigns
F=0
for bosons and
F=1
for fermions.
Then, we can define a new character (or it is called an index)

\chi_{PA}=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{NS}}\left((-1)^{F}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right)=q^{-\frac{1}{48}}\prod_{r=0}^{\infty}\left(1-q^{r+\frac{1}{2}}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}}~.

(4.95)

The additional term
(-1)^{F}
in the argument of this trace imposes the periodicity condition in the time direction.

Again let us check the modular properties of this new character. The modular
T
-transformation takes the boundary condition back to the (
AA
) sector because the anti-periodic boundary condition is imposed on the (1,1)-cycle due to
P\times A=A
. Subsequently, we have

T:\chi_{PA}(\tau+1)=e^{-\frac{i\pi}{24}}\chi_{AA}(\tau)~.

On the other hand, the modular
S
-transformation leads to

S:\chi_{PA}(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{2}q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{r\geq 1}(1+q^{r})=\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}}~,

(4.96)

because the different boundary conditions are imposed on the time and spatial circle. Since the
S
-transformation exchanges them, we end up with
(PA)
boundary condition after the
S
-transformation,
i.e.
the anti-periodic boundary condition on the time circle and the periodic boundary condition on the spatial circle. This is the character of the Ramond sector

\chi_{AP}:=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{R}}\left(q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}}

where the modes
b_{k}
take integer values of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that the prefactor
\sqrt{2}
in (
4.96
) stems from the fact that there are two ground states
|0\rangle
,
\psi_{0}|0\rangle
due to the fermion zero mode
\psi_{0}
. Multiplying the anti-holomorphic part, this prefactor takes into account the contribution from the two ground states.

Similarly, we can read off the modular properties of this character:

T(\chi_{AP}(\tau))=e^{\frac{i\pi}{12}}\chi_{AP}(\tau),\;\;\;\;\;\;S(\chi_{AP}(\tau))=\chi_{PA}(\tau)

(4.97)

One may wonder what
\chi_{PP}(\tau)
is. In this case, we need to impose periodic boundary conditions on both time and spatial circles. Due to the presence of
(-1)^{F}
, the contributions from the two ground states
|0\rangle
,
\psi_{0}|0\rangle
cancel each other. The same argument can be applied to the excited states so that the partition function
\chi_{PP}(\tau)
just vanishes.

We start with the partition function in NS sector, and perform modular transformations. Eventually, we obtain different boundary conditions. Hence, to obtain the modular-invariant partition function of the free fermion, we just need to sum over all of them

\mathcal{Z}_{\textrm{F}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left|\frac{\vartheta_{3}}{\eta}\right|+\left|\frac{\vartheta_{4}}{\eta}\right|+\left|\frac{\vartheta_{2}}{\eta}\right|\right).

(4.98)

We multiply an overall factor of
1/2
so that we count the NS ground state only once.

Special functions

Figure 13:
T
and
S
transformation of
\vartheta
function

The special functions we have used in the text are summarized below. You can also consult with Polchinski
[
Pol98
, §7.2]
or Blumenhagen & Plauschinn
[
BP09
, §4.2]
.

Their infinite product forms are

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\eta(\tau)=q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})\ ,

(4.99)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tau)=2q^{\frac{1}{8}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})(1+q^{n})^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(\tau)=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})(1+q^{n-\frac{1}{2}})^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(\tau)=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})(1-q^{n-\frac{1}{2}})^{2}\ ,

where
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
.
The first one is called the Dedekind eta function,
the others are called (Jacobi or elliptic or simply without these) theta functions.

Their
T
and
S
-transformations are

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\eta(\tau+1)=e^{i\pi/12}\eta(\tau)\ ,

\displaystyle\eta(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\eta(\tau)\ ,

(4.100)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tau+1)=e^{i\pi/4}\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\ ,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(\tau+1)=\vartheta_{4}(\tau)\ ,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{3}(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\vartheta_{3}(\tau)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(\tau+1)=\vartheta_{3}(\tau)\ ,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{4}(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\ .

In addition, there are two important identities. One is
called Jacobi identity:

\displaystyle(\vartheta_{3}(\tau))^{4}=(\vartheta_{2}(\tau))^{4}+(\vartheta_{4}(\tau))^{4}\ .

The other is called Jacobi triple product identity:

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\vartheta_{3}(\tau)\vartheta_{4}(\tau)=2\eta^{3}(\tau)\ ,

from which you can show the modular transformation of the Dedekind
\eta
-function.

5
Minimal models

This chapter is devoted to the most important class of conformal field theories, called
minimal models
first studied in the seminal paper
[
BPZ84
, ISZ88-No.1]
. These theories
are characterized by a Hilbert space made of finite numbers of representations of the Verma modules. We refer the reader to
[
FMS97
, §7,§8]
and
[
Gin88a
, §4,§5]
for more details.

5.1
Conformal family

From the previous study, we know that primary fields play a fundamental role in conformal field theory. The asymptotic state is given by
\ket{h}=\phi(0)\ket{0}
, which satisfies
L_{0}\ket{h}=h\ket{h}
and
L_{n}\ket{h}=0
for
n>0
. In the Verma module section, we also defined descendant states leading by primary
\ket{h}
, by acting a series of
L_{-n}
on
\ket{h}
. We first study the field operator corresponding to the descendant states. The natural definition of the descendant field associated with the state
L_{-n}\ket{h}
is

\phi^{(-n)}(w)\equiv\left(L_{-n}\phi\right)(w)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{w}dz\frac{1}{(z-w)^{n-1}}T(z)\phi(w)\,.

(5.1)

By using the OPE (
3.56
), the above expression can be calculated explicitly.
In particular, we have

\phi^{(0)}(w)=h\phi(w)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\phi^{(-1)}(w)=\partial\phi(w)\,.

(5.2)

Now consider the correlation function with one descendant field in it, i.e.

\left\langle\left(L_{-n}\phi\right)(w)X\right\rangle\,,

(5.3)

where
X=\phi_{1}\left(w_{1}\right)\cdots\phi_{N}\left(w_{N}\right)
is a product of primary fields with conformal dimensions
h_{i}
.
This correlation function can be calculated by substituting the definition (
5.1
).

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi^{(-n)}(w)X\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{w}dz(z-w)^{1-n}\langle T(z)\phi(w)X\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\left(w_{i}\right)}dz(z-w)^{1-n}\sum_{i}\{\frac{1}{z-w_{i}}\partial_{w_{i}}\langle\phi(w)X\rangle+\frac{h_{i}}{\left(z-w_{i}\right)^{2}}\langle\phi(w)X\rangle\}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{L}_{-n}\langle\phi(w)X\rangle\quad(n\geq 1)\,.

In the second step, the residue theorem is applied by
reversing the contour including
w
only and summing
the contributions from all the poles at
w_{i}
. With
the help of the relevant OPEs, the definition operator
\mathcal{L}_{-n}
is given by

\mathcal{L}_{-n}=\sum_{i}\left\{\frac{(n-1)h_{i}}{\left(w_{i}-w\right)^{n}}-\frac{1}{\left(w_{i}-w\right)^{n-1}}\partial_{w_{i}}\right\}\,.

(5.4)

This result tells us that the evaluation of a correlation function containing one descendant field

\phi^{(-n)}
is controlled by its primary field.
\mathcal{L}_{-1}
is equivalent to
\partial/\partial{w}
, since the operator

\frac{\partial}{\partial w}+\sum_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{i}}\,,

(5.5)

annihilates any correlation functions because of the translation invariance.

The descendant states corresponding to
L_{-k}L_{-n}\ket{h}
can be defined recursively.

\phi^{(-k,-n)}(w)\equiv\left(L_{-k}L_{-n}\phi\right)(w)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{w}dz(z-w)^{1-k}T(z)\left(L_{-n}\phi\right)(w)\,.

(5.6)

In particular, we have

\phi^{(0,-n)}(w)=(h+n)\phi^{(-n)}(w)\quad\text{and}\quad\phi^{(-1,-n)}(w)=\partial_{w}\phi^{(-n)}(w)\,.

(5.7)

And it can be shown directly that

\left\langle\phi^{\left(-\lambda_{1}\ldots\ldots-\lambda_{l}\right)}(w)X\right\rangle=\mathcal{L}_{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{-\lambda_{l}}\langle\phi(w)X\rangle\,.

(5.8)

That is nothing but to apply the differential operators in succession. Consequently, correlation
functions of descendant fields are determined from those of the primary field. If we obtain all the correlation functions of primary fields in a theory, then the correlation functions, including descendant fields, are thus all determined.

Moreover, all the OPEs with
T(z)
with descendant states can be calculated in a similar fashion. For example, for

\phi^{(-1)}=\partial\phi

\displaystyle T(z)\partial\phi(w)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{2h\phi(w)}{(z-w)^{3}}+\frac{(h+1)\partial\phi(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\phi(w)}{z-w}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{2\phi^{(0)}(w)}{(z-w)^{3}}+\frac{(h+1)\phi^{(-1)}(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\phi^{(-1,-1)}(w)}{z-w}\,,

where we neglect the regular terms as usual.
The set of a primary field
\phi

and all of its descendants is called the
conformal family
of
\phi
, denoted by
[\phi]
. The OPEs with
T(z)
are closed in the conformal family.

5.2
Minimal models

In a conformal field theory (or more generally quantum field theories),
we want to understand correlation functions in the
theory. For free bosons and fermions,
all correlation functions can be calculated by directly using the partition function.
However, it is not so straightforward to determine all the correlation functions for the interacting CFTs.
If there are only finitely many highest weight representations of Virasoro algebra (or finitely many primary fields), one can perform exact computations of correlation functions
[
BPZ84
, ISZ88-No.1]
. Such CFTs are called
minimal models
. Let us consider a theory with the set of finitely many highest
weights
\{h_{1},\cdots h_{p}\}
so that OPEs take the following form:

\phi_{1}(z,\overline{z})\phi_{2}(0,0)=\sum_{p}\sum_{\{\lambda,\overline{\lambda}\}}C^{\{\lambda,\overline{\lambda}\}}_{12p}z^{h_{p}-h_{1}-h_{2}+|\lambda|}\overline{z}^{\overline{h}_{p}-\overline{h}_{1}-\overline{h}_{2}+|\overline{\lambda}|}\phi^{\{\lambda,\overline{\lambda}\}}_{p}(0,0)\,,

(5.9)

where
|\lambda|=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}
and
|\overline{\lambda}|=\sum_{i}\overline{\lambda}_{i}
, and
\phi_{i}
is the primary field with
highest weight
h_{i}
. As we have seen in the last subsection, all the correlation functions can be
reduced to the correlation function constructed by
primary fields. Hence, OPEs of primary fields and conformal symmetry completely determine correlation functions if there are only finitely many
p
. In this section, we will learn the method to solve correlation functions in such a model.

Kac Determinant

A representation of the Virasoro algebra is said to be unitary if it contains no negative-norm states. In the section of the Verma module, we have mentioned that the negative of the central charge and highest weight will make the states non-unitary. As we see below, the unitarity imposes strong constraints on
(c,h)
.

In addition to unitarity, one has to pay attention to zero-norm states, indeed. If a state
|\chi\rangle
in a Verma module
V(c,h)
which is not a highest weight state satisfies the condition

L_{n}|\chi\rangle=0~,\quad\textrm{for}~n>0~,

it is called a
singular vector
. In fact, a singular vector
|\chi\rangle
is orthogonal to any state
L_{-\lambda}|h\rangle
(
3.119
) in the Verma module
V(c,h)

\langle\chi|L_{-\lambda}|h\rangle=0~.

Therefore, the norm of a singular vector is zero;
\langle\chi|\chi\rangle=0
so that it is also called a null vector or a null state.

Suppose that there exists a singular vector
|\chi\rangle
at level
N
in the Verma module, namely
L_{0}|\chi\rangle=(h+N)|\chi\rangle
. Then its descendants

L_{-\lambda}\ket{\chi}\equiv L_{-\lambda_{1}}L_{-\lambda_{2}}\cdots L_{-\lambda_{l}}\ket{\chi}\,,\qquad(\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\cdots\geq\lambda_{l}\geq 1)

are all null, and moreover they form a submodule in the Verma module
V(c,h)
. Therefore, in this situation, the Verma module is
reducible
. In fact, the Verma module
V(c,h)
is irreducible if and only if it contains no singular vector. Otherwise, an irreducible representation of the Virasoro algebra is

L(c,h)=V(c,h)/J(c,h)

where
J(c,h)
consists of all null states and their descendants

J(c,h)=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi:\textrm{null}\\
\lambda:\textrm{partitions}\end{subarray}}~\{L_{-\lambda}\ket{\chi}\}~.

(5.10)

To see if a Verma module has a null state, let us define a Gram matrix as

M_{ij}=\bra{i}\ket{j}
where
\ket{i}
are basis vectors.
For instance, at level two, we have 2 basis

L_{-2}\ket{h}
,
L_{-1}^{2}\ket{h}
.
Therefore, the Gram matrix is

\displaystyle G_{h}^{(2)}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}{\left\langle h\left|L_{2}L_{-2}\right|h\right\rangle}&{\left\langle h\left|L_{1}^{2}L_{-2}\right|h\right\rangle}\\
{\left\langle h\left|L_{2}L_{-1}^{2}\right|h\right\rangle}&{\left\langle h\left|L_{1}^{2}L_{-1}^{2}\right|h\right\rangle}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{4h+\frac{c}{2}}&{6h}\\
{6h}&{4h(1+2h)}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(5.11)

We focus on a zero eigenvector of this matrix, which gives a linear combination with zero norm. We write the determinant of this matrix as

2(16h^{3}-10h^{2}+2h^{2}c+hc)=32(h-h_{1,1}(c))(h-h_{1,2}(c))(h-h_{2,1}(c))\,,

(5.12)

and

\displaystyle h_{1,1}(c)

\displaystyle=0

(5.13)

\displaystyle h_{1,2}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}(5-c)+\frac{1}{16}\sqrt{(1-c)(25-c)}

\displaystyle h_{2,1}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}(5-c)-\frac{1}{16}\sqrt{(1-c)(25-c)}~.

The
h=0
root originates from the null state at level 1,
L_{-1}\ket{0}=0
, which directly implies the vanishing of
L_{-1}(L_{-1}\ket{0})=0
. This is a general phenomenon: if a null state (a state with zero norm)
\ket{h+n}=0
appears at level
n
, then at level
N>n
, there are
P(N-n)
null states of the form
L_{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots L_{-\lambda_{k}}\ket{h+n}=0
(with
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=N-n
). Consequently, the existence of a null state for a specific value of
h
at level
n
implies that the determinant at level
N
will have a
[P(N-n)]
-th order zero for that value of
h
.

At level
N
, the Hilbert space is spanned by all states of the form:

\sum_{|\lambda|=N}a_{\lambda}L_{-\lambda}|h\rangle\,.

(5.14)

From these, we can select
P(N)
basis states to construct the Gram matrix at level
N
, referred to as the
Kac-Shapovalov
matrix, with matrix elements defined as follows:

G_{h}^{(N)}(\mu,\lambda)\equiv\left\langle h\left|L_{\mu_{\ell}}\cdots L_{\mu_{1}}L_{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots L_{-\lambda_{k}}\right|h\right\rangle

(5.15)

where
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mu_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}=N
. We denote this matrix by
M_{N}(c,h)
. If
\det M_{N}(c,h)
vanishes, it indicates the existence of a linear combination of states with zero norm for that particular
c
and
h
. If the determinant is negative,
M_{N}(c,h)
has an odd number of negative eigenvalues, implying that the representation of the Virasoro algebra for those values of
c
and
h
includes states of negative norm, and is therefore non-unitary.

Figure 14:
Kac determinant

Kac has found a closed form expression of the determinant of
N
level Gram matrix, which is called
Kac determinant

[
Kac79
, ISZ88-No.7]

\operatorname{det}M_{N}(c,h)=\alpha_{N}\prod_{r,s\leq N}\left(h-h_{r,s}(c)\right)^{P(N-rs)}\,,

(5.16)

where
\alpha_{N}
is a positive constant independent of
c

and
h
. The Kac determinant provides complete information about the null states in a Verma module.
From this expression, one can see that a null state first appears at level
n=rs
if
h=h_{r,s}
. Thus at level
N>rs
, the
zero order for
h_{r,s}
is
P(N-rs)
. In fact, there are two useful equivalent ways to write
h_{r,s}
; the first expression is

\displaystyle h_{r,s}(c)

\displaystyle=h_{0}+\frac{1}{4}\left(r\alpha_{+}+s\alpha_{-}\right)^{2}\,,

\displaystyle h_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24}(c-1)\,,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{1-c}\pm\sqrt{25-c}}{\sqrt{24}}\,,

and the second is

\displaystyle h_{r,s}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{[(p+1)r-ps]^{2}-1}{4p(p+1)}\,,

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=1-\frac{6}{p(p+1)}\,,

where
p
has two branches

p=-\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{25-c}{1-c}}

(5.17)

From this expression, one can see that when
c=1
, a Verma module is irreducible if
h\neq n^{2}/4
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. In addition, when
c=0
, a Verma module is irreducible if
h\neq(n^{2}-1)/24
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Both of the above expressions provide the following
result for
h_{r,s}(c)
, which is illustrated in Figure
14

h_{r,s}(c)=\frac{1-c}{96}\left[\left((r+s)\pm(r-s)\sqrt{\frac{25-c}{1-c}}\right)^{2}-4\right]\,.

(5.18)

One can convince oneself that
h_{1,2}
and
h_{2,1}
are as in (
5.13
).
The Kac determinant changes sign when the values of
(c,h^{2})
cross
each curve
h=h_{r,s}(c)
.

Minimal models
\mathcal{M}_{p,p^{\prime}}

Belavin-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov have noticed that all the correlation functions can be determined by differential equations when the Kac determinant becomes zero.
For example, let us first consider the situation in which there are null states at level two. In general, a null state
\ket{\chi}
at level two takes the form

\ket{\chi}=L_{-2}|h\rangle+aL_{-1}^{2}|h\rangle\,.

(5.19)

By applying the
L_{1}
to the above equation, we have

\displaystyle\left[L_{1},L_{-2}\right]|h\rangle+a[L_{1},L_{-1}^{2}]|h\rangle

\displaystyle=3L_{-1}|h\rangle+a\left(L_{-1}2L_{0}+2L_{0}L_{-1}\right)|h\rangle

\displaystyle=(3+2a(2h+1))L_{-1}|h\rangle=0\,,

which requires that

a=-\frac{3}{2(2h+1)}\,.

(5.20)

By applying
L_{2}
, we find that

\displaystyle\left[L_{2},L_{-2}\right]|h\rangle+a[L_{2},L_{-1}^{2}]|h\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(4L_{0}+\frac{c}{2}\right)|h\rangle+3aL_{1}L_{-1}|h\rangle

\displaystyle=(4h+\frac{c}{2}+6ah)|h\rangle=0\,.

Therefore, the central charge must satisfy

c=2(-6ah-4h)=2h\frac{5-8h}{2h+1}

(5.21)

Writing
h
in terms of
c
, we have

h=\frac{1}{16}\left\{5-c\pm\sqrt{(c-1)(c-25)}\right\}\,,

(5.22)

which are equal to
h_{1,2}
and
h_{2,1}
in (
5.13
).

Since the null state
\ket{\chi}
is orthogonal to any states in the Verma module, we have

\langle\chi(z)X\rangle=0

(5.23)

where
X
is a product of arbitrary primary fields as usual. Since
\chi(z)
can be obtained by acting a linear combination of Virasoro generators (
5.4
) on a primary operator, this equation becomes a linear differential equation for a correlation function of primary fields, called a
Belavin–Polyakov–Zamolodchikov (BPZ) equation
.
For example, by using
(
5.1
), the correlation function

\expectationvalue{\phi(z)X}
should be governed by the
following differential equation

\left\{\mathcal{L}_{-2}-\frac{3}{2(2h+1)}\mathcal{L}_{-1}^{2}\right\}\langle\phi(z)X\rangle=0\,,

(5.24)

where
\phi(z)
has conformal dimension
h_{1,2}
or

h_{2,1}
.
One can write the above expression as a differential equation

\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{z-z_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}+\frac{h_{i}}{\left(z-z_{i}\right)^{2}}\right]-\frac{3}{2(2h+1)}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\right\}\langle\phi(z)X\rangle=0\,.

(5.25)

If
X
is
\phi(w)
itself, the differential
equation for a two-point function becomes

\left\{\frac{1}{z-w}\partial_{w}+\frac{h}{(z-w)^{2}}-\frac{3}{2(2h+1)}\partial_{z}^{2}\right\}\langle\phi(z)\phi(w)\rangle=0\,,

(5.26)

which is satisfied by the general form of a two-point function (
3.45
).
For a three-point function (
3.46
) with
X=\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})
, we have

\left\langle\phi(z)\phi_{1}\left(z_{1}\right)\phi_{2}\left(z_{2}\right)\right\rangle=\frac{C_{h,h_{1},h_{2}}}{\left(z-z_{1}\right)^{h+h_{1}-h_{2}}\left(z_{1}-z_{2}\right)^{h_{1}+h_{2}-h}\left(z-z_{2}\right)^{h+h_{2}-h_{1}}}\,.

(5.27)

The differential equation gives further constraints to
the highest weights of field operators in the three-point function.

h_{2}=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{3}h+h_{1}\pm\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{h^{2}+3hh_{1}-\frac{1}{2}h+\frac{3}{2}h_{1}+\frac{1}{16}}\,.

(5.28)

For
h=h_{1,2}
, and set
h_{1}=h_{r,s}
in (
5.18
), we find that
h_{2}
is equal to
h_{r,s-1}
or
h_{r,s+1}
. Similarly for
h=h_{2,1}
,
h_{2}
is equal to
h_{r-1,s}
or
h_{r+1,s}
. Recall that the two-point function vanishes unless the two operators have the same conformal dimension. We thus have the following operator algebra

\displaystyle\phi_{1,2}\times\phi_{r,s}

\displaystyle=[\phi_{r,s-1}]+[\phi_{r,s+1}]

\displaystyle\phi_{2,1}\times\phi_{r,s}

\displaystyle=[\phi_{r-1,s}]+[\phi_{r+1,s}]\,,

where
\phi_{r,s}
means the field operator with highest weight
h_{r,s}
. The expression above means that the fields
\phi_{1,2}
and
\phi_{2,1}
act as ladder operators in the operator algebra. In addition, the field operators expanded in the right side not only contain the primary fields but their descendant fields as well. The coefficient on the right side may be zero. For instance,

\displaystyle\phi_{1,2}\times\phi_{2,1}

\displaystyle=[\phi_{2,0}]+[\phi_{2,2}]\,,

\displaystyle\phi_{2,1}\times\phi_{1,2}

\displaystyle=[\phi_{0,2}]+[\phi_{2,2}]\,.

Since the two OPEs are equivalent, this shows the coefficients before
\phi_{2,0}
and

\phi_{0,2}
must be zero.
Therefore, we have

\phi_{1,2}\times\phi_{2,1}=[\phi_{2,2}]\,.

(5.29)

The above expression can be generalized to
the following fusion rules

\phi_{r_{1},s_{1}}\times\phi_{r_{2},s_{2}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{1}\hskip 8.19447ptk=1+\absolutevalue{r_1-r_2}\\
k+r_{1}+r_{2}=1\,\text{mod}\,2\end{subarray}}^{k=r_{1}+r_{2}-1}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{1}\hskip 8.19447ptl=1+\absolutevalue{s_1-s_2}\\
l+s_{1}+s_{2}=1\,\text{mod}\,2\end{subarray}}^{l=s_{1}+s_{2}-1}[\phi_{k,l}]\,.

(5.30)

The summation variables
k
and
l
are incremented
by
2
. The expression above implies that
the conformal family
[\phi_{r,s}]
forms a
closed operator algebra.

Figure 15:
”diagram of dimensions”

The operator algebra
(
5.30
), implies there are infinitely many conformal families present in the theory.
However, we want a finite set of highest weights in
a minimal model. In order to understand the situation
graphically, we consider the ”diagram of dimensions” as in Figure

15
.
The points
(r,s)
in the first quadrant label the various conformal dimensions appearing in the Kac formula.
The dotted line has a slope
\tan\theta=-\alpha_{+}/\alpha_{-}
, where
\alpha_{\pm}
are defined
in (
5.2
). If
\delta
is the distance
between a point
(r,s)
and the dotted line. It is
a good exercise to show the following relation,

h_{r,s}=h_{0}+\frac{1}{4}\delta^{2}\left(\alpha_{+}^{2}+\alpha_{-}^{2}\right)\,.

(5.31)

If the slope
\tan\theta
is irrational, it will never
go through any integer point
(r,s)
, which means

\delta
is always non-zero. There is no periodical
property for
h_{r,s}
. However, if the slope

\tan\theta
is rational. That is, if there exist
two coprime integers
p
and
p^{\prime}
such that

p^{\prime}\alpha_{-}+p\alpha_{+}=0\,.

(5.32)

The dotted line will go through the point
(p^{\prime},p)
, we
have the following periodicity property.

h_{r,s}=h_{r+p,s+p^{\prime}}\,.

(5.33)

Therefore, there are finitely many primary fields and we call it the
minimal model
denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{p,p^{\prime}}
.
Using (
5.2
), the central charge and
the Kac formula become

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=1-6\frac{\left(p-p^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{pp^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle h_{r,s}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(p^{\prime}r-ps\right)^{2}-\left(p-p^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{4pp^{\prime}}\,.

We can obtain a symmetry property from above:

h_{r,s}=h_{p-r,p^{\prime}-s}\,.

(5.34)

Now, we get a finite set of conformal families, which
closes under fusion rules. The corresponding finite
set of conformal dimensions
h_{r,s}
is given by

1\leq r<p
and
1\leq s<p^{\prime}
. The symmetry above
gives

\phi_{r,s}=\phi_{p-r,p^{\prime}-s}\,.

(5.35)

Hence, there remain
(p-1)(p^{\prime}-1)/2
distinct fields in the
theory.
And the fusion rule is modified to be

\phi_{r,s}\times\phi_{m,n}=\sum^{k_{\text{max}}}_{\begin{subarray}{1}\hskip 8.19447ptk=1+\absolutevalue{r-m}\\
k+r+m=1\,\text{mod}\,2\end{subarray}}\,\sum^{l_{\text{max}}}_{\begin{subarray}{1}\hskip 8.19447ptl=1+\absolutevalue{s-n}\\
k+s+n=1\,\text{mod}\,2\end{subarray}}\left[\phi_{k,l}\right]\,,

(5.36)

where the upper bound of the summation now is control
by the periodical and symmetric condition.

\displaystyle k_{\max}

\displaystyle=\min\left(r+m-1,2p-1-r-m\right)\,,

(5.37)

\displaystyle l_{\max}

\displaystyle=\min(s+n-1,2p^{\prime}-1-s-n)\,.

We will see several examples of minimal models in the following subsections.

Unitarity for
c>1

A representation of the Virasoro algebra is unitary if it contains no negative-norm states.
An essential tool in establishing unitarity conditions is the Kac determinant, which serves as a means to derive these conditions. In fact, the unitary condition gives us a strong constraint of the choice of
h
and
c
.

We now sketch the proof that the representations are unitary when
(c>1,h>0)
. The proof is separated into three steps as follows:

•

The first step is to prove that for each curve
C_{r,s}:h=h_{r,s}
, it will either be located below, or on the axis
h=0
if
c>1
. If
1<c<25
, from (
5.18
),
h_{r,s}
is not real unless
s=r
, where
h_{r,s}\leq 0
. If
c\geq 25
the choice (
5.17
) implies that
-1<p<0
. Then
p(p+1)<0
and
[(p+1)r-ps]\geq 1
. According to (
5.2
), this tells us
h_{r,s}(p)
is
not positive. Thus we finish our proof for the first step.

•

The second step is to prove that the Kac determinant is positive throughout the region.
For a given level, we can find a
h
larger than any
h_{r,s}
, and at such a point the Kac determinant is positive. Since none of the curves lies in the region, the determinant sign can not be changed throughout the region by crossing any curves. This proves the second point.

•

The last step is to prove that the Gram matrix at level
N
,
M_{N}
is positive definite in this region, which is equivalent to the theory
in this region is unitary. Since the Kac determinant is positive, we have even number of negative eigenvalues of
M_{N}
. Since this number can change only across one of the curves
C_{r,s}
, and is consequently fixed to be the same in the region. Therefore, we need to show that the matrix
M_{N}
for each level
N
is positive definite for at least one point
(c,h)
in the region.

To show this, we define the length
n(\alpha)
of a basis vector
\ket{\alpha}
as the number of operators
L_{k}
acting on the primary state
\ket{h}
. For example,

L^{3}_{-1}
has length
3
and
L_{-3}\ket{h}
has length 1. It is possible to show that the dominant behavior in
h
of inner products is

\displaystyle\langle\alpha|\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=c_{\alpha}h^{n(\alpha)}[1+\mathcal{O}(1/h)]\quad\left(c_{\alpha}>0\right)

(5.38)

\displaystyle\langle\alpha|\beta\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(h^{(n(\alpha)+n(\beta))/2-1})+\ldots\,.

where
\ket{\alpha}
and
\ket{\beta}
. The above expression immediately shows that
M_{N}
is dominant by its diagonal element when
h
is sufficiently large, and thus
M_{N}
is positive definite. Therefore, in this region, a Verma module
V(c,h)
is irreducible.

Unitarity for
0\leq c\leq 1

This unitarity criterion significantly constrains the admissible values of the parameters
h
and
c
, particularly within the regime where
0\leq c\leq 1
.
In this region, we merely provide a brief summary.

•

It is straightforward to show that, in Figure
14
, a point in the region
0\leq c\leq 1
,
h>0
that lies between two vanishing curves of the Kac determinant is non-unitary. The Kac determinant changes sign when crossing a vanishing curve, indicating the presence of a negative-norm state at that level. Consequently, no unitary representations of the Virasoro algebra exist in this region, except possibly on the vanishing curves themselves.

•

A more careful analysis tells us that there is an additional negative norm state everywhere on the vanishing curves except at certain points where they intersect.

Therefore, the unitary representations of the Virasoro algebra only occur at the values of the central charge
[
Car84a
, ISZ88-No.3]
:

c=1-\frac{6}{p(p+1)}\,,\quad p=3,4,\ldots

(5.39)

with the corresponding highest weight

h_{r,s}(p)=\frac{[(p+1)r-ps]^{2}-1}{4p(p+1)}\,.

(5.40)

where
r,s
satisfies
1\leq s\leq r\leq p-1
. The form is exactly the same as (
5.2
), but now
p
can only take discrete integers. This result tells us that the unitary condition is satisfied only at the point
(c,h)
where the Verma module has null states.

Unitary Minimal Models
\mathcal{M}_{p}

We now consider the choice of
p
and
p^{\prime}
to make the minimal models unitary. Recalling the admissible conformal dimensions in (
5.2
), Bezout’s lemma states that there exists a couple of integers
(r_{0},s_{0})
in the range
1\leq r_{0}<p
and
1\leq s_{0}<p^{\prime}
such that

p^{\prime}r_{0}-ps_{0}=1\,.

(5.41)

Thus, the corresponding conformal dimension

h_{r_{0},s_{0}}=\frac{1-\left(p-p^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{4pp^{\prime}}\,,

(5.42)

which is always negative, expect if
\absolutevalue{p-p'}=1
, in which case it vanishes. In fact, the minimal model is unitary only if
\absolutevalue{p-p'}=1
, and the primary field
h_{r_{0},s_{0}}=0
is indeed the identity operator. Hence, we can set
p^{\prime}=p+1
so that the central charge is expressed in (
5.39
). We denote the unitary minimal model by
\mathcal{M}_{p}
. We note that the list of unitary representations given in (
5.39
) and (
5.40
), coincides with the list of highest weight
h_{r,s}
of unitary minimal models.

5.3
Characters of minimal models

The character of an irreducible representation of the Virasoro algebra

\chi_{h}(\tau)\equiv\operatorname{Tr}_{L(c,h)}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}=q^{-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}d_{h}(N)q^{h+N}~,

where
d_{h}(N)
is the number of states at level
N
, and
d_{h}(N)\leq P(N)
. If there is no null state,
d_{h}(N)=P(N)
. As seen (
4.77
),
the torus partition function with periodic boundary conditions is

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{PP}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}}\left(q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\overline{q}^{\overline{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right)

(5.43)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h,\overline{h}}\mathcal{N}_{h\overline{h}}~\chi_{h}(\tau)\chi_{\overline{h}}(\overline{\tau})~,

where
\mathcal{N}_{h\overline{h}}
is the number of primary fields of conformal dimension
(h,\overline{h})

[
Car86a
, ISZ88-No.25]
. Since there is only one vacuum, we set
\mathcal{N}_{00}=1
. Under the
T
-transformation, a character behaves as

\chi_{h}(\tau+1)=e^{2\pi i(h-\frac{c}{24})}\chi_{h}(\tau)~.

(5.44)

The invariance of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{PP}}
under the
T
-transformation requires

\mathcal{N}_{h\overline{h}}=0,\quad h-\overline{h}\notin\mathbb{Z}~.

If we write the
S
-transformation of characters

\chi_{h}(-1/\tau)=\sum_{h^{\prime}}S_{hh^{\prime}}~\chi_{h^{\prime}}\left(\tau\right)~,

then the modular invariance of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{PP}}
requires

\sum_{h,\overline{h}}\mathcal{N}_{h\overline{h}}S_{hh^{\prime}}S_{\overline{h}\overline{h}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{N}_{h^{\prime}\overline{h}^{\prime}}~.

(5.45)

Below we will determine the partition function of diagonal type
\mathcal{N}_{hh^{\prime}}=\delta_{hh^{\prime}}
in the unitary minimal models. We will see one example of non-diagonal type in the tricritical Ising model in §
5.5
, and the classification of the modular invariant partition functions is discussed at the end of §
8.3
.

From the Kac determinant, we have learned that the Verma module
V_{h_{r,s}}
has a singular vector at level
rs
. Since
h_{r,s}+rs=h_{-r,s}
, the singular vector generates a submodule
V_{h_{-r,s}}
. Furthermore. since
h_{p-r,p^{\prime}-s}+(p-r)(p^{\prime}-s)=h_{2p-r,s}
, we have another submodule
V_{h_{2p-r,s}}
. Moreover, these submodule
V_{h_{-r,s}}
,
V_{h_{2p-r,s}}
contain other submodules
V_{h_{-2p-r,s}}
,
V_{h_{2p+r,s}}
, and one can see repeatedly this pattern
[
FF83
, ISZ88-No.9]
.

(r,s)
(-r,s)
(2p-r,s)
(-2p+r,s)
(-2p-r,s)
(-4p+r,s)

(2p+r,s)
(4p-r,s)
(4p+r,s)

Therefore, the character of the irreducible representation
L(c,h_{r,s})
of the Virasoro algebra is

\displaystyle\chi_{r,s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}_{L(c,h_{r,s})}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}

(5.46)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{-\frac{1}{24}}\eta(\tau)}q^{-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left[q^{h_{r+2pk,s}}-q^{h_{2pk-r},s}\right]

(5.47)

which is called the
Rocha-Caridi formula

[
RC85
, ISZ88-No.10]
.

Defining the theta functions by

\Theta_{m,k}(\tau)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}q^{k\left(n+\frac{m}{2k}\right)^{2}}

(5.48)

a character of the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p}
can be written by

\chi_{r,s}(\tau)=\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)}\left(\Theta_{r(p+1)-sp,p(p+1)}(\tau)-\Theta_{r(p+1)+sp,p(p+1)}(\tau)\right)

(5.49)

The behavior of
\chi_{r,s}(\tau)
under the
S
-transformation has been obtained in
[
Car86b
, ISZ88-No.29]
:

\displaystyle\chi_{r,s}(-1/\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(r^{\prime},s^{\prime}):\mathrm{primary}}S_{(r,s),(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}~\chi_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}(\tau)

(5.50)

\displaystyle S_{(r,s),(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{8}{p(p+1)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{(r+s)\left(r^{\prime}+s^{\prime}\right)}\sin\left(\frac{\pi rr^{\prime}}{p}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi ss^{\prime}}{p+1}\right)

and the
T
-transformation is given in (
5.44
).
It is shown that the partition function of diagonal type

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{M}_{p}}^{\textrm{diag}}=\sum_{(r,s):\mathrm{primary}}\left|\chi_{r,s}(\tau)\right|^{2}

(5.51)

is invariant under the modular transformations.

5.4
Ising model
(p^{\prime},p)=(4,3)

3

\frac{1}{2}

0

2

\frac{1}{16}

\frac{1}{16}

1

0

\frac{1}{2}

1

2

3

\varepsilon

\mathbf{1}

2

\sigma

\sigma

1

\mathbf{1}

\varepsilon

1

2

Let us consider the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p=3}
whose central charge
c=\frac{1}{2}
. The primary fields and their conformal dimensions are described in the table above. By comparing the conformal dimensions with (
1.5
),
\varepsilon
is the energy density operator and
\sigma
is the spin field in the Ising model. Therefore, the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p=3}
describes the Ising model.

Since the Ising model is the most important 2d CFT, let us investigate it more in detail. For the Ising model on a lattice of
(N\times M)
sites, the statistical partition function is

\mathcal{Z}=\sum_{\{\sigma\}}e^{K\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}

(5.52)

where we define
K=J/(k_{B}T)
. One can use the identity

\exp\left[x\sigma_{i}\sigma_{l}\right]=\cosh x\left(1+\sigma_{i}\sigma_{l}\tanh x\right)

so that the partition function is

\mathcal{Z}=\sum_{\{\sigma\}}\prod_{\langle i,j\rangle}\cosh(K)\left(1+\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\tanh(K)\right)~.

(5.53)

At high temperature,
\tanh K
is small and one can take expansion. In the expansion, the generic expression of these terms is

\tanh(K)^{r}\sigma_{1}^{n_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{n_{2}}\sigma_{3}^{n_{3}}\ldots

where
r
is the total number of lines connecting the adjacent sites
i
and
j
, and
n_{i}
is the number of lines where
i
is the final site. Since each spin
\sigma_{i}
assumes values
\pm 1
, we have a null sum unless all
n_{1},n_{2},\ldots
are even numbers, which implies all closed loops
P
of the lattice as in Figure
16
. Hence, the partition function is

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{high}}=[2\cosh(K)]^{NM}\sum_{P}[\tanh(K)]^{\mathrm{length}(P)}

On the other hand, at low temperatures, the spins tend to align one with another. Taking the dual lattice, the border between antiparallel spins forms loops
P_{D}
, and the partition function is given by

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{low}}=2e^{NMK}\sum_{P_{D}}e^{-2K(\mathrm{length}(P_{D}))}~.

(5.54)

where the contribution of all spins down has been factored out of the sum.

Figure 16:
(Above) In high-temperature regime, the partition function is expressed as a sum over all close loops in the lattice. (Below) In low-temperature regime, the partition function is expressed as a sum over all close loops in the dual lattice.

The two phases are indeed dual to each other

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{low}}\left(K^{\prime}\right)=2(2\sinh 2\mathrm{K})^{-\frac{NM}{2}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{high}}(K)

(5.55)

where we identify

e^{-2K^{\prime}}=\tanh K~.

(5.56)

This is called the
Kramers-Wannier duality
. This can be also written as

\sinh(2K)\sinh(2K')=1

(5.57)

so that the critical temperature is at
\sinh 2K=1=\sinh 2K^{\prime}
which is

K_{c}=\frac{1}{2}\log(1+\sqrt{2})~.

(d+1)
-dimensional classical to
d
-dimensional quantum Ising

Let us first consider the one-dimensional classical Ising model

Z(K)=\sum_{\{s_{i}=\pm 1\}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{Ks_{i}s_{i+1}}

(5.58)

with periodic boundary condition
s_{1}=s_{N+1}
