. Locally, the transfer matrix can be written as

S=e^{Ks_{i}s_{i+1}}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{K}&e^{-K}\\
e^{-K}&e^{K}\end{pmatrix}

(5.59)

Then, the partition function can be written as

Z(K)=\Tr S^{N}

(5.60)

For a quantum statistical system, the quantum partition function is given by

\tilde{Z}(\beta)=\Tr(e^{\beta H})=\Tr((e^{H\Delta\tau})^{N})

(5.61)

where
H
is the Hamiltonian of the system and
\beta=1/k_{B}T
,
N\Delta\tau=\beta
. Therefore, the 1d classical Ising model can be translated into a two-qubit system if we identify
S
with
e^{H\Delta\tau}
. Moreover, since the eigenvalues of
S
are
e^{K}\pm e^{-K}
, the partition function becomes

Z(K)=(e^{K}+e^{-K})^{N}+(e^{K}-e^{-K})^{N}=\cosh^{N}K(1+\tanh^{N}K)~.

(5.62)

so that the free energy is

F(K)=\log Z(K)=N\log\cosh K+\log(1+\tanh^N K)~.

(5.63)

Now let us move on to the two-dimensional classical Ising model. Analogous to the previous example, it is equivalent to quantum system of
N
two-qubit sites to which
N
copies
S^{(1)}\cdots S^{(N)}
of the transfer matrices (
5.59
) acts on it. Since we have

e^{K^{\prime}\sigma^{x}}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh K^{\prime}&\sinh K^{\prime}\\
\sinh K^{\prime}&\cosh K^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

(5.64)

(
5.59
) can be expressed as

S=(2\sinh 2K)^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{K^{\prime}\sigma^{x}}

(5.65)

where
K
and
K^{\prime}
are related by (
5.56
). The horizontal interaction between two adjacent qubits is diagonalized as
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{z}\sigma_{i+1}^{z}
. Consequently, the partition function can be written as

Z\left(K,K^{\prime}\right)=(2\sinh 2K)^{\frac{NM}{2}}\Tr\left(e^{K\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{z}\sigma_{i+1}^{z}}e^{K^{\prime}\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}^{x}}\right)^{M}

(5.66)

The correspondence between classical and quantum statistical systems can be understood visually by Figure
17
.

Figure 17:
Mapping from
(d+1)
-dimensional classical model to
d
-dimensional quantum model

Therefore, the Hamiltonian of quantum one-dimensional Ising model is

\mathcal{H}=-k_{B}T(K\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{z}\sigma_{i+1}^{z}+K^{\prime}\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}^{x})

(5.67)

This Hamiltonian can be understood as a quantum spin chain with the external magnetic field
K^{\prime}
is parallel to the
x
-axis. In the low-temperature
K\gg K^{\prime}
, we can ignore the interaction with the external magnetic field
K^{\prime}
. However, in the high-temperature
K\ll K^{\prime}
, this is not the case. In order to capture the interaction term, it is natural to consider the
disorder operator

\mu_{i}
on the dual lattice, which is defined as follows:

\mu_{a}^{z}\equiv\prod_{i=-\frac{N}{2}}^{i=a}\sigma_{i}^{x}~,\qquad\mu_{a}^{x}=\sigma_{a}^{z}\sigma_{a+1}^{z}

For instance, if we act
\mu^{z}_{a}
on the parallel-spin state

|\uparrow\rangle\equiv\prod_{b=-\frac{N}{2}}^{\frac{N}{2}}|\uparrow\rangle_{b}

then we have

\mu_{a}^{z}|\uparrow\rangle=\prod_{b=-\frac{N}{2}}^{a}|\downarrow\rangle_{b}\prod_{c=a+1}^{\frac{N}{2}}|\uparrow\rangle_{c}~.

(5.68)

Thus, the disorder operator
\mu_{a}^{z}
changes the ordered spin configurations at the dual-site
a
so that it can be understood as a
soliton
located at the dual-site
a
. Moreover, the Hamiltonian (
5.67
) can be written as

\mathcal{H}=-k_{B}T(K^{\prime}\sum_{\left\langle a,a^{\prime}\right\rangle}\mu_{a}^{z}\mu_{a^{\prime}}^{z}+K\sum_{a}\mu_{a}^{x})

so that
B
and
J
are exchanged. Hence, in the low-temperature

\left\langle\sigma_{a}^{z}\right\rangle\neq 0,\quad\left\langle\mu_{a}^{z}\right\rangle=0,\quad T<T_{c}

whereas in the high-temperature

\left\langle\mu_{a}^{z}\right\rangle\neq 0,\quad\left\langle\sigma_{a}^{z}\right\rangle=0,\quad T>T_{c}~.

Onsager’s exact solution

To solve a one-dimensional quantum Ising chain exactly, we diagonalize the Hamiltonian (
5.67
). This can be done by introducing a set of fermionic operators
\{\psi_{i}|i=1,2,...,2N\}
satisfying Clifford algebra

\{\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\}=2\delta_{ij}.

(5.69)

A natural choice of the operators is

\displaystyle\psi_{2k-1}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{z}_{1}\sigma^{z}_{2}\cdots\sigma^{z}_{k-1}\sigma^{y}_{k}=\sigma^{z}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma^{z}_{k-1}\otimes\sigma^{y}_{k}\otimes I\otimes\cdots\otimes I

(5.70)

\displaystyle\psi_{2k}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{z}_{1}\sigma^{z}_{2}\cdots\sigma^{z}_{k-1}\sigma^{x}_{k}=\sigma^{z}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma^{z}_{k-1}\otimes\sigma^{x}_{k}\otimes I\otimes\cdots\otimes I

for
k=1,2,\cdots,N
. It can be shown that,

\displaystyle i\psi_{2k-1}\psi_{2k}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{z}_{k}

(5.71)

\displaystyle i\psi_{2k}\psi_{2k+1}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{y}_{k}\sigma^{y}_{k+1}.

Figure 18:
Expressing Ising chain by fermionic operators

By rotating the reference frame
x\to y
,
y\to z
,
z\to x
, Hamiltonian (
5.67
) can be rewritten by fermionic operators as

\mathcal{H}=-ik_{B}T(K\sum_{k=1}^{N}\psi_{2k}\psi_{2k+1}+K^{\prime}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\psi_{2k-1}\psi_{2k})

(5.72)

where the periodic boundary condition is imposed by
\psi_{2N+1}=\psi_{1}
. Then, the degree of freedom at each site is split, which could be understood by Figure
18
. This is called the
Jordan-Wigner transformation
. Set
\Psi=(\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\cdots,\psi_{2N})^{T}
, then
\mathcal{H}=-\frac{ik_{B}T}{2}\Psi^{T}\mathbf{M}\Psi
,

\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}0&K^{\prime}&&&&-K\\
-K^{\prime}&0&K&&&\\
&-K&0&K^{\prime}&&\\
&&-K^{\prime}&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&\ddots&0&K^{\prime}\\
K&&&&-K^{\prime}&0\end{pmatrix}

(5.73)

Diagonalizing
H
is equivalent to performing Fourier transform to a vector

\mathbf{v}=(\cdots,\omega^{-1}a,\omega^{-1}b,a,b,\omega a,\omega b,\cdots)^{T}

\tilde{\mathbf{v}}=-i\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v}=(\cdots,\omega^{-1}\tilde{a},\omega^{-1}\tilde{b},\tilde{a},\tilde{b},\omega\tilde{a},\omega\tilde{b},\cdots)^{T}

(5.74)

where
\omega^{N}=1
. From (
5.74
),
a,b
and
\tilde{a},\tilde{b}
is related by

\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\Tilde{a}\\\Tilde{b}\endlx@physics@matrix)=-i\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0&K'-K\omega^{-1}\\-K'+K\omega&0\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a\\b\endlx@physics@matrix).

(5.75)

Therefore, the
2N
eigenvalues of the matrix
-i\mathbf{M}
is given by

\pm m_{s}=\pm\absolutevalue{K'-Ke^{-\frac{2 \pi i s} N}}~,\qquad s=1,2,\cdots,N.

(5.76)

Hence the partition function of the system is given by

Z(K,K^{\prime})=\sum_{\pm\pm\cdots\pm}e^{\pm m_{1}\pm m_{2}\cdots\pm m_{N}}=\prod_{s=1}^{N}\left(e^{+m_{s}}+e^{-m_{s}}\right)=2^{N}\prod_{s=1}^{N}\cosh\left|K^{\prime}-Ke^{-2\pi is/N}\right|.

(5.77)

So the free energy of the system is

F(K,K^{\prime})=\frac{1}{N}\log Z(K,K^{\prime})=\log 2+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s=1}^{N}\log\cosh\left|K^{\prime}-Ke^{-\frac{2\pi is}{N}}\right|.

(5.78)

In the large-
N
limit
N\to\infty
, the summation can be replaced by an integral

F(K,K^{\prime})=\log 2+\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log\cosh\left|K^{\prime}-Ke^{-i\ell}\right|\frac{d\ell}{2\pi}.

(5.79)

Moreover, the spectrum of Onsager’s solution is given by

m_{s}=\sqrt{(K-K^{\prime})^{2}+KK^{\prime}\left(2\sin\frac{2\pi s}{N}\right)^{2}}\geq|K-K^{\prime}|.

(5.80)

When
K\neq K^{\prime}
, the spectrum is gapped. Since a gapped system has the typical length scale, the theory is
not
conformal. On the other hand, when
K=K^{\prime}=K_{c}
, we have

2m_{s}=2K|\sin\frac{2\pi s}{N}|

(5.81)

so that the first excited energy goes to zero if we take
N\to\infty
.
The system is called gapless, signaling that it can be described by CFT. (See Figure
1
.)

Figure 19:
Correlation function
\langle\sigma(x_{1})\cdots\sigma(x_{5})\mu(x_{1}^{*})\mu(x_{2}^{*})\rangle
depends on a choice of a path connecting
x_{1}^{*}
and
x_{2}^{*}
because they are mutually non-local.

Minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p=3}

In the high-temperature regime, the partition function admits the expansion (
5.53
). Hence, the two-point function of spin operators can be obtained by

\langle\sigma(x_{1})\sigma(x_{2})\rangle_{\textrm{high}}=[2\cosh(K)]^{NM}\sum_{P}[\tanh(K)]^{\mathrm{length}(P)}

(5.82)

where we sum over all the paths
P
connecting
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. On the other hand, the same method cannot be applied in the low-temperature regime because (
5.54
) makes sense only for a closed path
P_{D}
. However, we can introduce the disordered operator and consider the two-point function as follows. Usually, the Boltzmann weight
e^{K\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}
is assigned to an edge
\langle i,j\rangle
in the partition function. Choosing a path
P_{D}
connecting
x_{1}^{*}
and
x_{2}^{*}
, if an edge
\langle i,j\rangle
intersects
P_{D}
, the effect of the disordered operators changes the sign of the exponent of the Boltzmann weight
e^{K\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}\to e^{-K\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}
. Namely, we define

\left\langle\mu(x_{1}^{*})\mu(x_{2}^{*})\right\rangle_{P_{D}}=\sum_{\{\sigma\}}\prod_{\langle i,j\rangle}e^{\pm K\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}

(5.83)

where the sign
\pm
in the exponent is determined by whether or not the edge crosses
P_{D}
.
Although there is no unique choice of
P_{D}
, the two-point function is well-defined

\left\langle\mu(x_{1}^{*})\mu(x_{2}^{*})\right\rangle_{P_{D}}=\left\langle\mu(x_{1}^{*})\mu(x_{2}^{*})\right\rangle_{P_{D}^{*}}~.

(5.84)

Indeed, the Boltzmann weight does not change if we flip all spins inside the closed path
P_{D}\cdot P_{D}^{*}
.

Likewise, we can define a general correlation function as follows:

\left\langle\sigma(x_{1})\cdots\sigma(x_{m})\mu(x_{1}^{*})\mu(x_{2}^{*})\right\rangle_{P_{D}}=\sum_{\{\sigma\}}\sigma(x_{1})\cdots\sigma(x_{m})\prod_{\langle i,j\rangle}e^{\pm K\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}

(5.85)

However, this depends on the choice of
P_{D}

\left\langle\sigma(x_{1})\cdots\sigma(x_{m})\mu(x_{1}^{*})\mu(x_{2}^{*})\right\rangle_{P_{D}}=\pm\left\langle\sigma(x_{1})\cdots\sigma(x_{m})\mu(x_{1}^{*})\mu(x_{2}^{*})\right\rangle_{P_{D}^{*}}

(5.86)

where the sign
\pm
is determined by the number of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
inside the closed path
P_{D}\cdot P_{D}^{*}
. (See Figure
19
.) Put differently, the correlation function with both
\sigma
and
\mu
depends on a global structure. Therefore, they are called
mutually non-local
.
In fact, a disordered operator
\mu
introduces a branch cut, and a spin operator receives a sign. when it crosses the branch cut:

\sigma(z,\overline{z})\mu(0,0)\rightarrow\sigma\left(e^{2\pi i}z,e^{-2\pi i}\overline{z}\right)\mu(0,0)=-\sigma(z,\overline{z})\mu(0,0)

when
\sigma
is rotated around
\mu
. This can be understood that the fermion shows up in the OPE of
\sigma
and
\mu

\sigma(z,\overline{z})\mu(0,0)\sim\frac{1}{|z|^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(z^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi(0)+\overline{z}^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\psi}(0)\right)

(5.87)

Recalling that the central charge of the minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p=3}
is
c=\frac{1}{2}
, one can deduce that a free fermion describes the continuum limit of the Ising model.

In fact, identifying the various operators in Ising model with the primaries in
\mathcal{M}_{p=3}

\displaystyle\psi(z)

\displaystyle\sim\phi_{2,1}(z)\otimes\phi_{1,1}(\overline{z})

(5.88)

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}(\overline{z})

\displaystyle\sim\phi_{1,1}(z)\otimes\phi_{2,1}(\overline{z})

\displaystyle\psi\overline{\psi}:=\varepsilon(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle\sim\phi_{2,1}(z)\otimes\phi_{2,1}(\overline{z})

\displaystyle\sigma(z,\overline{z})\ \textrm{or}\ \mu(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle\sim\phi_{1,2}(z)\otimes\phi_{1,2}(\overline{z})

we obtain desired OPE relations. Note that
\sigma
and
\mu
have the same conformal dimension because the Kramers-Wannier duality exchanges the spin and disorder operator
\sigma\leftrightarrow\mu
. The fusion rule in
\mathcal{M}_{p=3}
can be read off as

\sigma\times\sigma=\mathbf{1}+\varepsilon~,\qquad\varepsilon\times\varepsilon=\mathbf{1}~,\qquad\sigma\times\varepsilon=\sigma~.

(5.89)

Moreover, using the associativity of OPEs, one can derive from (
5.87
)

\psi(z)\sigma(w,\overline{w})\sim\frac{1}{(z-w)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\mu(w,\overline{w})~.

We can compare this with the mode expansion of free fermion on the Ramond sector

\psi(z)\sigma(0)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-r-\frac{1}{2}}b_{r}\sigma(0)~,

we can deduce

b_{0}\sigma(0)=\mu(0)~.

(5.90)

In the presence of the spin field
\sigma(0)
at the origin, the fermion field obeys the anti-periodic boundary condition

\psi(z)\rightarrow\psi(e^{2\pi i}z)=-\psi(z)~.

The spin field
\sigma
creates a branch cut, which changes the boundary condition of the fermion field. Hence,
\sigma
can be regarded as the
twist operator
.

Finally, let us consider the partition functions of the Ising model. There are three unitary irreducible representations corresponding to the highest weights
h=0,\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{16}
, and the corresponding characters can be read off from (
5.49
) as

\displaystyle\chi_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{3}}{\eta}}+\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{4}}{\eta}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{NS}\left(\frac{1+(-1)^{F}}{2}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right),

\displaystyle\chi_{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{3}}{\eta}}-\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{4}}{\eta}}\right)=\mbox{Tr}_{NS}\left(\frac{1-(-1)^{F}}{2}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right),

(5.91)

\displaystyle\chi_{\frac{1}{16}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_{2}}{\eta}}=\mbox{Tr}_{R}\left(q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right).

Using these expressions, the partition function of the Ising model of diagonal type is equal to that of the free fermion (
4.98
)

\mathcal{Z}_{\textrm{F}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\chi_{0}\overline{\chi}_{0}+\chi_{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\chi}_{\frac{1}{2}}+\chi_{\frac{1}{16}}\overline{\chi}_{\frac{1}{16}}.

(5.92)

The structure of this partition function also appears when studying superstrings, and the operator
\frac{1}{2}(1+(-1)^{F})
is known as the
Gliozzi-Scherk-Olive (GSO) projection
.

5.5
Other examples

Yang-Lee singularity
(p^{\prime},p)=(2,5)

Among the minimal non-unitary models, a simple but particularly significant example is given by the model
\mathcal{M}_{2,5}
with central charge is
c=-22/5
. The primary fields in the theory are identity operator and a field
\phi_{1,2}
of conformal dimension
h=-1/5
.

The partition function of a statistical model defined on a lattice is an analytic function of its parameters as long as the number
N
of the fluctuating variables is finite. Let us consider the Ising model at a given temperature
T
in the presence of an external magnetic field
B

\mathcal{H}=-J\sum_{\left\langle i,\right\rangle}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}-B\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}

(5.93)

The Yang-Lee theorem states that the zeroes of the partition function
Z
are all on the imaginary axis
{\rm Re}B=0
. Fisher showed that the points
B=\pm iB_{c}
are new critical points of the theory, which are called
Yang-Lee edge singularities
. Furthermore, he has argued that the effective action is given by the Landau-Ginzburg theory

\mathcal{S}=\int d^{2}z\left[\frac{1}{2}(\partial\Sigma)^{2}+i\left(B-B_{c}\right)\Sigma+ig\Sigma^{3}\right]

where the non-unitarity of the model manifests itself in the imaginary value of the coupling constant. This model is described by the non-unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{2,5}

[
Car85
, ISZ88-No.13]
.

Tricritical Ising model
(p^{\prime},p)=(5,4)

4

\frac{3}{2}

\frac{7}{16}

3

\frac{3}{5}

\frac{3}{80}

\frac{1}{10}

2

\frac{1}{10}

\frac{3}{80}

\frac{3}{5}

1

0

\frac{7}{16}

\frac{3}{2}

0

G

\sigma^{\prime}

\mathbf{1}

3

t

\sigma

\varepsilon

2

\varepsilon

\sigma

t

1

\mathbf{1}

\sigma^{\prime}

G

1

3

Let us consider the variant of the Ising model whose Hamiltonian is given by

\mathcal{H}=-J\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}^{N}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}t_{i}t_{j}-B\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}t_{i}-\mu\sum_{i=1}^{N}t_{i}

where a spin variable
\sigma_{k}
takes values
\pm 1
, and a vacancy variable
t_{k}
, with values
0
and
1
. This variable specifies whether the site
is empty (0) or occupied (1). The chemical potential
\mu
controls the density of the vacancy.

Figure 20:
Phase diagram of the tricritical Ising model. The (broken) first-order line and the (full) line of Ising critical points meet at the tricritical point
(B_{t},T_{t})
, shown as a black dot

A typical phase diagram of this model is sketched in Figure
20
, where the lines of first-order and second-order transitions meet in the tricritical point, located at
B=B_{t}
and
T=T_{t}
. While at a normal critical point the two phases become indistinguishable, at a tricritical point three physically distinct phases merge. For that reason, this model is called the
tricritical Ising model
. The phenomenology of the tricritical Ising model is described in
[
Car96
, §4]
.

Remarkably, the tricritical model is described by
\mathcal{N}=1
superconformal field theory where
G
is the supercurrent
[
FQS85
, ISZ88-No.5]
.

3-state Potts model
(p^{\prime},p)=(6,5)

\begin{array}[]{c| c c c c }5&3&{\frac{7}{5}}&{\frac{2}{5}}&{0}\\[5.0pt]
4&\frac{13}{8}&{\frac{21}{40}}&{\frac{1}{40}}&{\frac{1}{8}}\\[5.0pt]
3&\frac{2}{3}&{\frac{1}{15}}&{\frac{1}{15}}&{\frac{2}{3}}\\[5.0pt]
2&\frac{1}{8}&{\frac{1}{40}}&{\frac{21}{40}}&{\frac{13}{8}}\\[5.0pt]
1&0&{\frac{2}{5}}&{\frac{7}{5}}&{3}\\[5.0pt]
\hline\cr&1&2&3&4\end{array}

On a square lattice, the Hamiltonian of the three-state Potts model is given by

\mathcal{H}=-\frac{J}{2}\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}\left(\sigma_{i}\overline{\sigma}_{j}+\overline{\sigma}_{i}\sigma_{j}\right)

(5.94)

where
\sigma
takes the values
\omega^{i}
(i=1,2,3) with a third root of unity
\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}
. This model undergoes a second-order phase transition at
J_{c}=\frac{2}{3}\log(\sqrt{3} + 1)
, which is endowed with the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry
[
Dot84
, ISZ88-No.12]
. The lattice theory is exactly solvable and consequently all critical exponents are known. For instance,

h_{\sigma}=h_{\overline{\sigma}}=\frac{1}{15}~,\qquad h_{\varepsilon}=\frac{2}{5}~.

It is tempting to identify the primary field of the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p=5}
.
However, the exact solution of the lattice model does not have operators with
conformal dimensions
\frac{1}{8}
,
\frac{1}{40}
,
\frac{21}{40}
, and
\frac{13}{8}

[
vGR86
, ISZ88-No.22]
.

In fact, the partition function of the 3-state Potts model is described by the non-diagonal type

\mathcal{Z}_{\text{3-Potts}}=\sum_{r=1,2}\left\{\left|\chi_{r,1}+\chi_{r,5}\right|^{2}+2\left|\chi_{r,3}\right|^{2}\right\}~,

(5.95)

so that the primary fields are

(0,0),\left(\frac{2}{5},\frac{2}{5}\right),\left(\frac{7}{5},\frac{7}{5}\right)~,2\times\left(\frac{1}{15},\frac{1}{15}\right),2\times\left(\frac{2}{3},\frac{2}{3}\right)(0,3),(3,0),\left(\frac{2}{5},\frac{7}{5}\right),\left(\frac{7}{5},\frac{2}{5}\right)

It is noteworthy that there are chiral spin-3 currents
W(z)
and
\overline{W}(\overline{z})
corresponding to (0,3), (3,0)
[
Zam85
, ISZ88-No.6]
. In fact,
W(z)
with the energy momentum tensor
T(z)
forms
W_{3}
-algebra
[
FZ87
]
.

Other models

It was shown in
[
Hus84
, ISZ88-No.32]
that a general unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p}
of diagonal type describes an RSOS model constructed in
[
ABF84
, ISZ88-No.31]
.
Furthermore, the CFT with
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
symmetry has been constructed in
[
FZ85
, ISZ88-No.14]
, which we will see in §
7.7
, where the central charge is

c=\frac{2(k-1)}{k+2}~.

(5.96)

When
k=4
, the central charge is
c=1
and the corresponding CFT is called the
Ashkin-Teller model
where the Hamiltonian is written by the two spin fields
\sigma_{i}
and
\tau_{i}
as

\mathcal{H}=-J_{2}\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}\left(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}+\tau_{i}\tau_{j}\right)-J_{4}\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\tau_{i}\tau_{j}~.

The
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry is given by

s_{j}\to e^{\frac{i\pi k}{2}}s_{j}~,\qquad\textrm{where}\qquad s_{j}\equiv\frac{\sigma_{j}+i\tau_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}~.

This Ashkin-Teller model can be described by the free boson on an orbifold space
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which will be studied in §
8.2
.
All in all, these models are the main characters in the papers listed in
[
ISZ88
]
.

5.6
Conformal blocks and bootstrap

The main object of a field theory is the calculation of correlation functions. We have seen the forms of two- and three-point functions in (
3.45
) and (
3.46
). As mentioned before, one way to construct all correlation functions is to find the corresponding operator algebra: The complete OPE (including all regular terms) of all primary fields with each other.
So far, we consider the fusion rule of primary fields in a minimal model, which does not tell anything about the coefficients of operator algebra. This section aims to spell out the coefficients of operator algebra and conformal bootstrap approach.

Let us start with a two-point function.
Since the coefficient
C_{12}
of a two-point function (
3.45
) is symmetric, we are free to choose a basis of primary fields of conformal dimension
h
so that
C_{\alpha\beta}=\delta_{\alpha\beta}
. Thus, conformal families associated with different
\phi_{\alpha}
are orthogonal in the sense of the two-point function.

As in (
5.9
), the general form of OPE of primary fields involves descendants

\phi_{p}^{\{\lambda,\bar{\lambda}\}}(z,\overline{z})=\mathcal{L}_{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{-\lambda_{l}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{-\overline{\lambda}_{1}}\cdots\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{-\overline{\lambda}_{m}}\phi_{p}(z,\overline{z})~.

(5.97)

In fact, the three-point function (
3.46
) explicitly determines the coefficient of the most singular term of the OPE (
5.9
) as
C_{12p}^{\{0,0\}}=C_{12p}
. Moreover,
since the correlations of descendants are built on the correlation of the primaries as in (
5.8
), we expect the coefficient to have the form:

C^{\{\lambda,\overline{\lambda}\}}_{12p}=C_{12p}\beta_{12(p,{\lambda})}\overline{\beta}_{12(p,\overline{\lambda})}\,.

(5.98)

As we will see below,
\beta_{ij(p,{\lambda})}
can be completely determined by the representation of Virasoro algebra as functions of central charge
c
and of conformal dimensions.
Therefore, once we find all the coefficients
C_{pnm}
of three-point functions with primary fields, we determine all OPEs. Indeed, the conformal bootstrap is one powerful way to determine
C_{pnm}
.

Conformal Blocks

To explain the conformal bootstrap, let us consider a four-point:

\left\langle\phi_{1}\left(z_{1},\overline{z}_{1}\right)\phi_{2}\left(z_{2},\overline{z}_{2}\right)\phi_{3}\left(z_{3},\overline{z}_{3}\right)\phi_{4}\left(z_{4},\overline{z}_{4}\right)\right\rangle\,,

(5.99)

By a suitable conformal transformation, we can set
z_{4}=0,z_{1}=\infty,z_{2}=1
and
z_{3}=x
where
x
is the cross-ratio of
z_{1,2,3,4}
:

G_{34}^{21}(x,\overline{x})=\lim_{z_{1},\overline{z}_{1}\rightarrow\infty}z_{1}^{2h_{1}}\overline{z}_{1}^{2\overline{h}_{1}}\left\langle\phi_{1}\left(z_{1},\overline{z}_{1}\right)\phi_{2}(1,1)\phi_{3}(x,\overline{x})\phi_{4}(0,0)\right\rangle\,,

(5.100)

Applying the operator algebra (
5.9
) to

\phi_{3}(x,\overline{x})\phi_{4}(0,0)
,
the function
G^{21}_{34}
becomes

G_{34}^{21}(x,\overline{x})=\sum_{p}C_{12p}C_{34p}\mathcal{F}_{34}^{21}(p|x)\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{34}^{21}(p|\overline{x})\,,

(5.101)

where

\mathcal{F}_{34}^{21}(p|x)=x^{h_{p}-h_{3}-h_{4}}\sum_{\{\lambda\}}\beta_{34(p,\lambda)}x^{|\lambda|}\frac{\left\langle h_{1}\left|\phi_{2}(1)L_{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots L_{-\lambda_{l}}\right|h_{p}\right\rangle}{\left\langle h_{1}\left|\phi_{2}(1)\right|h_{p}\right\rangle}\,,

(5.102)

which are called
conformal blocks
. Here, we can reformulate this expression using three-point functions. Specifically, we define the vertex function as follows:

\Gamma_{12(p,\lambda)}:=\frac{\left\langle h_{1}\left|\phi_{2}(1)L_{-\lambda_{1}}\cdots L_{-\lambda_{l}}\right|h_{p}\right\rangle}{\left\langle h_{1}\left|\phi_{2}(1)\right|h_{p}\right\rangle}

(5.103)

Additionally, we introduce another vertex function:

\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,\lambda^{\prime})34}:=\frac{\langle\phi_{p}|L_{\lambda^{\prime}_{N}}\cdots L_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}\phi_{3}(1)|\phi_{4}\rangle}{\langle\phi_{p}|\phi_{3}(1)|\phi_{4}\rangle}=\sum_{\lambda}\beta_{34(p,\lambda)}\langle\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}\rangle

(5.104)

The last term in the second line of this equation corresponds to the Kac-Shapovalov matrix
G_{h_{p}}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
in (
5.15
), which enables us to express the structure constants as:

\beta_{34(p,\lambda)}=\sum_{\lambda}(G_{h_{p}}(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda))^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,\lambda^{\prime})34}

(5.105)

While the Kac-Shapovalov matrix is infinite-dimensional yet block-diagonal, it is possible to compute its inverse at each level. Consequently, the conformal block can be described using vertex functions and the matrix:

\mathcal{F}_{34}^{21}(p|x)=x^{h_{p}-h_{3}-h_{4}}\sum_{|\lambda|=|\lambda^{\prime}|}x^{|\lambda|}\Gamma_{12(p,\lambda)}(G_{h_{p}}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}))^{-1}\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,\lambda^{\prime})34}

(5.106)

Therefore, the computation of the vertex functions, the three-point functions with descendants, is crucial for deriving the conformal block. Let us first consider the evaluation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. For simplicity, we assume that the three-point function is properly normalized and omit the denominator in our expressions. Using the operator product expansion (OPE) of the energy-momentum tensor
T(z)
with primary fields, we can rewrite the expression as follows:

\displaystyle\langle L_{-n}\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{4}(0)\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|\oint_{C_{0}+C_{1}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n+1}T(z)\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{4}(0)\rangle

(5.107)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oint_{C_{0}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n+1}\sum_{k}\frac{1}{z^{k+2}}\langle\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|\phi_{3}(1)(L_{k}\phi_{4}(0))\rangle

(5.108)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\oint_{C_{1}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n+1}\sum_{k}\frac{1}{(z-1)^{k+2}}\langle\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|(L_{k}\phi_{3}(1))\phi_{4}(0)\rangle~.

By expanding
z^{n+1}
around
z=1
, we obtain the series expansion:

z^{n+1}=\sum_{l=0}^{n+1}\binom{n+1}{l}(z-1)^{l}.

(5.109)

Substituting this series back into the previous expression, we simplify:

\langle L_{-n}\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{4}(0)\rangle=\langle\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|\phi_{3}(1)(L_{n}\phi_{4}(0))\rangle+\sum_{l=0}^{n+1}\binom{n+1}{l}\langle\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|(L_{l-1}\phi_{3}(1))\phi_{4}(0)\rangle.

(5.110)

For primary fields
\phi_{3}
and
\phi_{4}
, terms involving
L_{n}
for
n\geq 1
vanish, thereby simplifying the expression to:

\langle L_{-n}\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{4}(0)\rangle=\left((n+1)h_{3}+\delta_{n,0}h_{4}\right)\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,\lambda^{\prime})34}+\langle\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|L_{-1}\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{4}(0)\rangle.

(5.111)

For
n=0
, the second term can be expressed using
\Gamma^{\prime}
:

\langle\phi_{p}^{-\lambda^{\prime}}|L_{-1}\phi_{3}(1)\phi_{4}(0)\rangle=(h_{p}+|\lambda^{\prime}|-h_{3}-h_{4})\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,\lambda^{\prime})34}.

(5.112)

Substituting this back into our expression, we derive:

\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,[n,\lambda^{\prime}]);34}=(h_{p}+|\lambda^{\prime}|+nh_{3}+h_{4})\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,\lambda^{\prime});34}.

(5.113)

The notation
[n,\lambda^{\prime}]
refers to the process of adding a row of length
n
to the Young diagram
\lambda^{\prime}=[\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda^{\prime}_{d}\geq 1]
. By repeatedly applying this relation, we obtain

\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,\lambda^{\prime});34}=\prod_{i=1}^{l(\lambda^{\prime})}\Big(h_{p}+\lambda^{\prime}_{i}h_{3}-h_{4}+\sum_{j>i}\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\Big)~.

(5.114)

Next, let us analyze another component of the correlation function:

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{1}(\infty)\phi_{2}(1)(L_{n}\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0))\rangle=

\displaystyle-\oint_{C_{0}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n-1}\langle T(z)\phi_{1}(\infty)\phi_{2}(1)\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0)\rangle

(5.115)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\oint_{C_{1}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n-1}\sum_{k}\frac{1}{(z-1)^{k+2}}\langle\phi_{1}(\infty)(L_{k}\phi_{2}(1))\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0)\rangle

(5.116)

\displaystyle\qquad-\oint_{C_{\infty}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n-1}\sum_{k}z^{k-2}\langle(L_{k}\phi_{1}(\infty))\phi_{2}(1)\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0)\rangle

By deforming the integration contour using
C_{0}=-C_{1}-C_{\infty}
and applying the transformation property of the energy-momentum tensor under
z=1/w
, specifically
T(z)=w^{4}T(w)
, we can perform a Laurent expansion around
z=\infty
. Upon evaluating the residue, we obtain:

\langle\phi_{1}(\infty)\phi_{2}(1)(L_{n}\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0))\rangle=\langle(L_{n}\phi_{1})(\infty)\phi_{2}(1)\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0)\rangle\\
-\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k+1}\binom{k+n-1}{n-2}\langle\phi_{1}(\infty)(L_{k}\phi_{2})(1)\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0)\rangle~.

(5.117)

For primary fields
\phi_{1,2}
, the same analysis as above leads to

\langle\phi_{1}(\infty)\phi_{2}(1)(L_{n}\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0))\rangle=-\langle\phi_{1}(\infty)(L_{-1}\phi_{2})(1)\phi_{p}^{-\lambda}(0)\rangle+\left((n-1)h_{2}+\delta_{n,0}h_{1}\right)\Gamma_{12(p,\lambda)}.

(5.118)

Substituting
n=0
into this expression, we arrive at the simplified form

\Gamma_{12,(p,[n,\lambda])}=(h_{p}+|\lambda|+nh_{2}-h_{1})\Gamma_{12,(p,\lambda)}~.

(5.119)

Finally, the correlation function can be written in the form

\Gamma_{12,(p,\lambda)}=\prod_{i=1}^{l(\lambda)}\Big(h_{p}+\lambda_{i}h_{2}-h_{1}+\sum_{j>i}\lambda_{j}\Big).

(5.120)

Comparing with (
5.114
), we find

\Gamma_{12,(p,\lambda)}=\Gamma^{\prime}_{(p,\lambda)12}~.

(5.121)

In this way, we can determine the conformal block (
5.106
) by order-by-order.

As it stands, the conformal block is derived from the representation theory of the Virasoro algebra and is independent of the specific 2d CFT being considered. In other words, it is a universal construct that can be applied to any 2d CFT by substituting specific values, such as the central charge. In contrast, the structure coefficients in the three-point function (
3.46
) are intricately tied to the particular details of the theory.

The fusion of fields
\phi_{3}
and
\phi_{4}
generates intermediate operators, denoted as
[\phi_{p}]
, which subsequently interact with
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
. This interaction process can be visualized as depicted in Figure
21
.

Figure 21:
Diagram for the conformal block

Let us provide a few examples. By expanding the conformal block as a power of the cross-ratio
x

series in
x
:

\mathcal{F}_{34}^{21}(p|x)=x^{h_{p}-h_{3}-h_{4}}\sum_{K=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{K}x^{K}\,.

(5.122)

we can present several leading coefficients
(
5.102
).

\mathcal{F}_{0}=1\,,\qquad\mathcal{F}_{1}=\frac{\left(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1}\right)\left(h_{p}+h_{3}-h_{4}\right)}{2h_{p}}

(5.123)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1}\right)\left(h_{p}+h_{2}-h_{1}+1\right)\left(h+h_{3}-h_{4}\right)\left(h+h_{3}-h_{4}+1\right)}{4h_{p}\left(2h_{p}+1\right)}

\displaystyle+2\left(\frac{h_{1}+h_{2}}{2}+\frac{h_{p}\left(h_{p}-1\right)}{2\left(2h_{p}+1\right)}-\frac{3\left(h_{1}-h_{2}\right)^{2}}{2\left(2h_{p}+1\right)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\left(\frac{h_{3}+h_{4}}{2}+\frac{h_{p}\left(h_{p}-1\right)}{2\left(2h_{p}+1\right)}-\frac{3\left(h_{3}-h_{4}\right)^{2}}{2\left(2h_{p}+1\right)}\right)^{2}\left(c+\frac{2h_{p}\left(8h_{p}-5\right)}{2h_{p}+1}\right)^{-1}

Crossing Symmetry and the Conformal Bootstrap

To obtain a four-point function, we fuse
\phi_{3}
and
\phi_{4}
in (
5.101
). However, we can also take different pairs for a fusion, say
\phi_{3}
and
\phi_{2}
. For this, we can send
z_{2}
to
0
and
z_{4}
to
1
, and then
z_{3}
proves to be
1-x
. Then, the same procedure will give a different basis
\mathcal{F}_{32}^{41}(q|1-x)
of conformal blocks. Nonetheless, the final result for the four-point function should be the same

G^{21}_{34}(x,\overline{x})=G^{41}_{32}(1-x,1-\overline{x})\,,

(5.124)

which implies

\sum_{p}C_{12p}C_{34p}\mathcal{F}_{34}^{21}(p|x)\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{34}^{21}(p|\overline{x})=\sum_{q}C_{14q}C_{23q}\mathcal{F}_{32}^{41}(q|1-x)\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{32}^{41}(q|1-\overline{x})

(5.125)

This relation is represented graphically in Figure
22

Figure 22:
Crossing symmetry

If the conformal block
\mathcal{F}
is known, then (
5.124
) yields a system of equations that can determine
C_{ijk}
’s and
h,\overline{h}
’s. The program of calculating the correlation functions simply by assuming crossing symmetry is known as the
conformal bootstrap
approach. For minimal models, the bootstrap equations can be solved completely.

6
Renormalization group flow and
c
-theorem

6.1
Renormalization group flow

Idea

In the introduction, we have briefly discussed that conformal field theories arise at fixed points of renormalization group flow. The study of renormalization group flows tells us the special role of conformal field theories (See
[
Zam89
]
included in
[
JMT89
]
and
[
EY89
]
). In this section, we shall glimpse this in two-dimensional systems.

The name of renormalization group is actually misleading in the following two reasons:

•

the transformations of the renormalization “group” are irreversible; therefore, they do not form a group.

•

moreover, they do not necessarily concern the renormalization of a theory, i.e. the cure of the divergences of the perturbative series.

Instead, the concept of renormalization group flow describes how families of physical theories behave under change of the length scale. The effect of averaging over short-distance physics can be summarized in the change of finitely many parameters in the long-distance physics of interest.

There is a wonderful introduction to renormalization group flow in
[
Car96
, §3]
. Following Cardy,
we shall explain the concept of renormalization group flow by coarse-graining for the Ising model.
Let us consider a statistical system defined on a
d
-dimensional regular lattice of spacing
a
, with degrees of freedom placed on its sites. Let
H(\{\sigma_{i}^{(1)}\},g_{k}^{(1)})
be the Hamiltonian of the system, where
g_{k}^{(1)}
are the coupling constants of the various interactions among the spins
\sigma_{i}^{(1)}
. Now we divide the original lattice into blocks of length
ba
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{k}
, each of them made of
b^{d}
spins, and we assign a new spin variable
\sigma_{k}^{(2)}
by averaging over all the spins
\sigma_{i}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
. We are going to do coarse-graining of the original lattice by repeating this procedure. More precisely, we define the new spin variable by

\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)}=A^{(n)}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\sigma_{i}^{(n)}

(6.1)

where
A
is a suitable normalization constant.

To define the effective hamiltonian for the coarse lattice, we introduce an operator

T\left(\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)},\sigma_{i}^{(n)}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{1~,\quad\text{if}\ \eqref{average}}\\
{0~,\quad\text{otherwise}}\end{array}\right.

so that it satisfies

\sum_{\left\{\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)}\right\}}T\left(\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)},\sigma_{i}^{(n)}\right)=1

Then, the effective hamiltonian
H^{(n+1)}\left(\left\{\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)}\right\},g_{k}^{(n+1)}\right)
can be defined by averaging the spins
\sigma_{i}^{(n)}
with their Boltzmann factor

\exp\left[-H^{(n+1)}\left(\left\{\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)}\right\},g_{k}^{(n+1)}\right)\right]=\sum_{\left\{\sigma_{i}^{(n)}\right\}}\prod_{\textrm{blocks}}T\left(\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)},\sigma_{i}^{(n)}\right)\exp\left[-H^{(n)}\left(\left\{\sigma_{i}^{(n)}\right\},g_{i}^{(n)}\right)\right]

in this way. Then, ignoring an overall constant, we have the equivalence of the statistical partition functions

\sum_{\left\{\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)}\right\}}\exp\left[-H^{(n+1)}\left(\left\{\sigma_{k}^{(n+1)}\right\},g_{k}^{(n+1)}\right)\right]=\sum_{\left\{\sigma_{i}^{(n)}\right\}}\exp\left[-H^{(n)}\left(\left\{\sigma_{i}^{(n)}\right\},g_{i}^{(n)}\right)\right]

This equality also holds for the expectation value of any observable
\mathcal{O}
.
In this process, the new coupling constants are expressed by the previous one

\left\{g^{(n+1)}\right\}=\mathcal{R}\left(\left\{g^{(n)}\right\}\right)

where
\mathcal{R}
is, in general, a complicated nonlinear transformation.Starting from the original coupling constants
\{g_{k}^{(1)}\}
, the procedure of the coarse-graining provides the sequence
\{g_{k}^{(2)}\}
,
\{g_{k}^{(3)}\}
,
\ldots
, which is called a
renormalization group flow
.
In fact, a fixed point is a point in the space of the coupling constants that remains invariant

g^{*}=\mathcal{R}\left(g^{*}\right)

Since the coarse-graining does not affect the theory, it is scale-invariant, implying that it is conformal. As briefly explained in the introduction, critical phenomena are described by these fixed points.

One-dimensional Ising model

As a concrete example, let us examine the one-dimensional Ising model whose Hamiltonian is

H\left(s_{i};J\right)=-J\sum_{i}s_{i}s_{i+1}

Each pair of spins has the Boltzmann weight

W\left(s_{i},s_{i+1};v\right)=e^{Ks_{i}s_{i+1}}=\cosh K\left(1+vs_{i}s_{i+1}\right)

(6.2)

where
v=\tanh K=\tanh\frac{J}{k_{B}T}
.

Figure 23:
coarse-graining

As illustrated in Figure
23
, we divide the lattice of three spins. Denoting
\sigma_{1}=s_{2}
and
\sigma_{2}=s_{5}
, we sum over
s_{3}
and
s_{4}

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{3},s_{4}}e^{J\sigma_{1}s_{3}}e^{Js_{3}s_{4}}e^{Js_{4}\sigma_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{3},s_{4}}(\cosh K)^{3}\left(1+v\sigma_{1}s_{3}\right)\left(1+vs_{3}s_{4}\right)\left(1+vs_{4}\sigma_{2}\right)

(6.3)

\displaystyle=2^{2}(\cosh K)^{3}\left(1+v^{3}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\right)

In addition to a normalization constant, one can assign the new Boltzmann weight
W(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2};\widetilde{v})
to the new block spins
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
with the new coupling constant

\widetilde{v}=v^{3}~,

which is called the
renormalization group equation
. It has two fixed points:
v_{1}^{*}=0
and
v_{2}^{*}=1
corresponding to the high-temperature phase
T\to\infty
, and the low-temperature phase
T=0
.

In addition, the new Hamiltonian of the system is given by

H\left(\sigma_{i};\widetilde{J}\right)=\widetilde{N}p(J)-\widetilde{J}\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}

where
\widetilde{N}=N/3
is the number of new sites, and

\widetilde{J}=k_{B}T\tanh^{-1}\left[(\tanh K)^{3}\right]~,\quad p(J)=-\log\left[\frac{(\cosh K)^{3}}{\cosh\widetilde{K}}\right]-2\log 2~.

(6.4)

\beta
-functions and Callan-Symanzik equation

In general, the idea described above ends up with mathematical equations describing renormalization group flows in the space of parameters of a physical system. Let us derive these equations by using path integrals. Writing the action of a conformal field theory at a fixed point by
S^{*}
, the perturbed action can be written as

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{*}+\sum_{a}\int\frac{d^{2}x}{2\pi}~g_{a}\mathcal{O}_{a}(x)

(6.5)

where the coupling constants
g_{a}
are sufficiently small. If
\mathcal{O}_{a}
is of scaling dimension
\Delta_{a}
the coupling constant
g_{a}
has scaling dimension
2-\Delta_{a}
. Under an infinitesimal scaling transformation (dilatation)
x\to(1+d\lambda)x
, the perturbed action behaves as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{*}+\sum_{a}(1+d\lambda)^{2}(1+d\lambda)^{-\Delta_{a}}\int\frac{d^{2}x}{2\pi}~g_{a}\mathcal{O}_{a}(x)

(6.6)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{S}+d\lambda\sum_{a}\left(2-\Delta_{a}\right)\int\frac{d^{2}x}{2\pi}~g_{a}\mathcal{O}_{a}(x)

where we assume that
S^{*}
is scale-invariant. On the other hand, we recall the variation under the scaling transformation

\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}+d\lambda\int d^{2}x~\left(T_{11}(x)+T_{22}(x)\right)=S-d\lambda\int\frac{d^{2}x}{2\pi}~\Theta(x)

(6.7)

where

\Theta:=-2\pi{T^{\mu}}_{\mu}

(6.8)

is the trace of the energy-momentum tensor. Therefore, we can write

\Theta(x)=-\sum_{a}\beta_{a}(g)\mathcal{O}_{a}(x)

(6.9)

where
\beta_{a}
are first-order approximations of so-called
\beta
-functions

\beta_{a}(g)=\left(2-\Delta_{a}\right)g_{a}

(6.10)

The idea of renormalization group flow is that the change of a physical system on the length-scale is absorbed into the change of coupling constants, which is measured by the
\beta
-functions

\frac{dg_{a}}{d\lambda}=\beta_{a}(g)

Therefore, at a fixed point, we have

\beta_{a}\left(g^{*}\right)=0

by definition. At the first-order approximation (
6.10
), the coupling constants behave as

g_{a}\sim e^{(2-\Delta_{a})\lambda}

around a fixed point
g_{a}^{*}=0
.

At the long distant scale
\lambda\gg 1
that is called
infra
-
red
or
IR
,
if
\Delta_{a}<2
, a coupling constant increases, it is called a
relevant coupling
. If
\Delta_{a}>2
, it decreases and is called an
irrelevant coupling
. In the space of coupling constants
\{g_{a}\}
, the subspace spanned by irrelevant ones is called the
critical manifold
, which is the attractive basin for the fixed point
g^{*}
. Under the RG flow, irrelevant operators are scaled out, and it arrives at the fixed point.
Hence, all the theories belong to the same universality class.

On the other hand, the instability nature of a critical point is determined by the number of its relevant couplings. Relevant couplings get amplified as a theory flows to the low-energy region and moves away from the fixed point. When
\Delta_{a}=2
, an operator
\mathcal{O}_{a}
is called
marginal
, and its behavior depends on its
\beta
-function, which is determined by taking quantum corrections into account (beyond the first-order approximation). If
\beta_{a}>0
(resp.
\beta_{a}<0
), then it is called
marginally relevant
(resp.
marginally irrelevant
). If
\beta_{a}=0
, then it is called
exactly marginal
. Our main interest here is the two-dimensional theories, but the same argument can be applied to quantum field theories in any dimension.

Now let us consider the correlation functions of the local fields
\phi_{i}(x)
, defined as usual by the functional integral

\left\langle\phi_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\right\rangle=\int\mathcal{D}\phi~\phi_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)e^{-S[\phi]}

If the variation of the field
\phi_{i}
under the scaling transformation is

\delta\phi_{i}(x)=-\epsilon\left(x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}+\Delta_{i}\right)\phi_{i}(x)

the corresponding Ward-Takahashi identity (
3.47
) is

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle\left(x_{i}^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}^{\mu}}+\Delta_{i}\right)\phi_{1}\ldots\phi_{n}\right\rangle-\int\frac{d^{2}x}{2\pi}\left\langle\Theta(x)\phi_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\right\rangle=0~.

(6.11)

The fields
\phi_{i}(x)
can also depend on the coupling constants and their variation, varying the coupling
g_{a}
, is expressed in terms of an operator
\widehat{B}_{i}^{(a)}

\widehat{B}_{i}^{(a)}\phi_{i}(x)=\frac{\partial}{\partial g_{a}}\phi_{i}(x)

Using (
6.9
), we can write

\sum_{a}\beta_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial g_{a}}\langle\phi_{1}\ldots\phi_{n}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{a}\beta_{a}\left\langle\widehat{B}_{i}^{(a)}\phi_{1}\ldots\phi_{n}\right\rangle+\int\frac{d^{2}y}{2\pi}\langle\Theta(y)\phi_{1}\ldots\phi_{n}\rangle

(6.12)

Defining the operator

\widehat{\Gamma}_{i}=\widehat{\Delta}_{i}+\sum_{a}\beta_{a}\widehat{B}_{i}^{(a)}

(6.13)

we can erase
\Theta
from the (
6.11
), which is called
the
Callan-Symanzik equation

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle\left(x_{i}^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}^{\mu}}+\widehat{\Gamma}_{i}(g)\right)\phi_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\right\rangle-\sum_{a}\beta^{a}(g)\frac{\partial}{\partial g_{a}}\left\langle\phi_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\right\rangle=0~.

(6.14)

The operator
\widehat{\Gamma}_{i}
in (
6.13
) determines the scaling dimension of an operator
\phi_{i}
at quantum level where
\widehat{\Delta}_{i}
yields the classical scaling dimension and
\sum_{a}\beta_{a}\widehat{B}_{i}^{(a)}
measures quantum corrections. Thus, it is called the
anomalous dimension
.
Consequently, the Callan–Symanzik equation is a differential equation describing the evolution of a correlation function under variation of the energy scale.
Since the scaling dimension of
\Theta
is always two
\widehat{\Gamma}\Theta=2\Theta
at quantum level, we can derive

\widehat{\Gamma}\mathcal{O}_{a}(x)=\sum_{b}\gamma_{ab}\mathcal{O}_{b}(x)=\sum_{b}\left(2\delta_{ab}-\frac{\partial\beta_{b}}{\partial g_{a}}\right)\mathcal{O}_{b}(x)~.

(6.15)

Indeed, using (
6.9
), this form yields

\displaystyle\widehat{\Gamma}\Theta

\displaystyle=-\left(\widehat{\Delta}+\sum_{a}\beta_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial g_{a}}\right)\sum_{b}\beta_{b}\mathcal{O}_{b}

(6.16)

\displaystyle=-\left(\sum_{b}\beta_{b}\widehat{\Gamma}\mathcal{O}_{b}+\sum_{a,b}\beta_{a}\frac{\partial\beta_{b}}{\partial g_{a}}\mathcal{O}_{b}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(\sum_{a,b}\beta_{b}\left(2\delta_{ba}-\frac{\partial\beta_{a}}{\partial g_{b}}\right)\mathcal{O}_{a}+\sum_{a,b}\beta_{a}\frac{\partial\beta_{b}}{\partial g_{a}}\mathcal{O}_{b}\right)

\displaystyle=2\Theta

Also, in the first-order approximation (
6.10
), it is easy to see

\widehat{\Gamma}\mathcal{O}_{a}=\Delta_{a}\mathcal{O}_{a}~,

as expected.

6.2
Zamolodchikov
c
-theorem

In renormalization group flows in 2d, there is an important theorem due to A.B. Zamolodchikov, called the
c
-
theorem

[
Zam86b
, ISZ88-No.45]
.
The
c
-Theorem asserts that, under the assumptions of rotation
invariance, unitarity and energy-momentum conservation in a
two-dimensional theory, there exists a function
C
which is non-increasing along a renormalization group flow. Moreover, at an IR fixed point, it is equal to
the central charge
c
of the IR CFT. This theorem tells us the global structure of the space of physical theories and the role of conformal field theories.

We begin with energy-momentum conservation,

\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0\,,

(6.17)

where
T^{\mu\nu}
is symmetric. This can be written in complex coordinates
z
,
\overline{z}

\overline{\partial}T+\frac{1}{4}\partial\Theta=0\quad,\quad\partial\overline{T}+\frac{1}{4}\overline{\partial}\Theta=0\,,

(6.18)

where
\Theta:=-2\pi{T^{\mu}}_{\mu}
. (See (
3.48
) and (
3.49
).) At an IR fixed point,
\Theta
vanishes and
T=T(z)
is holomorphic. However, the energy-momentum tensor is dependent of both holomorphic and anti-holomorphic coordinate
T=T(z,\overline{z})
, and
\Theta(z,\overline{z})\neq 0
for a non-conformal theory. Since
T
,
\Theta
and
\overline{T}

be the components of spin
(2,0)
,
(1,1)
and
(0,2)
, respectively, of the
energy-momentum tensor, the rotational invariance, spins and dimensions imply for the
correlation functions of
T
and
\Theta

\displaystyle\langle T(z,\overline{z})T(0,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{F(z\overline{z})}{z^{4}}\,,

(6.19)

\displaystyle\langle\Theta(z,\overline{z})T(0,0)\rangle=\langle T(z,\overline{z})\Theta(0,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{G(z\overline{z})}{z^{3}\overline{z}}\,,

\displaystyle\langle\Theta(z,\overline{z})\Theta(0,0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{H(z\overline{z})}{z^{2}\overline{z}^{2}}\,.\,

and similarly for
\overline{T}
. Combining the
first equation of (
6.18
) with
T
, we have

\left\langle\overline{\partial}T(z,\overline{z})T(0,0)\right\rangle+\frac{1}{4}\left\langle\partial\Theta(z,\overline{z})T(0,0)\right\rangle=0\,,

(6.20)

We adopt the notation

\dot{F}:=z\overline{z}F^{\prime}(z\overline{z})
where the prime stands for the derivative of
F(x)
with respect to its argument. One finds

\dot{F}+\frac{1}{4}\dot{G}-\frac{3}{4}G=0\,,

(6.21)

and similarly by combining with
\Theta

\dot{G}-G+\frac{1}{4}\dot{H}-\frac{1}{2}H=0\,.

(6.22)

Eliminating
G
from above two equations, we define

C:=2F-G-\frac{3}{8}H\,.

(6.23)

Then, we find that it is non-increasing with respect to the scale

\dot{C}=-\frac{3}{4}H\leq 0\,.

(6.24)

due to unitarity. We see
C
is a non-increasing function of

R:=(z\overline{z})^{\frac{1}{2}}
, for fixed values of couplings
\{g\}
.
The
R
-dependence is related to the dependence on the

\{g\}
via the Callan-Symanzik equation (
6.14
)

\left(R\frac{\partial}{\partial R}-\sum_{a}\beta_{a}\left(g\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial g_{a}}\right)C(R,\{g\})=0\,.

(6.25)

Using (
6.9
) and the Callan-Symanzik equation (
6.14
) on
\langle\Theta\Theta\rangle
, we obtain

\beta^{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial g^{a}}C(g)=-\frac{3}{2}G_{ab}(g)\beta^{a}(g)\beta^{b}(g)\,,

(6.26)

where

G_{ab}(g)=G_{ab}(1,g),\quad G_{ab}(z\overline{z},g)=(z\overline{z})^{2}\left\langle\mathcal{O}_{a}(z,\overline{z})\mathcal{O}_{b}(0,0)\right\rangle\,,

(6.27)

is positive-definite, since the theory is unitary. In other words,
G_{ab}(g)
can be regarded as a metric of the space of the coupling constants. Therefore, it is called the
Zamolodchikov metric
. Moreover, we can write the
\beta
-function as

\beta_{a}(g)=-\frac{2}{3}\sum_{b}G_{ab}^{-1}\frac{\partial C}{\partial g_{b}}~,

(6.28)

so that
C
can be understood as a Morse function on the space of the coupling constants and
\beta_{a}
can be understood as the gradient vector fields of the Morse function
[
Mil63
]
.

It follows from (
6.26
) that the function
C(g)
decreases under the renormalization
group flow. Since the
\beta
-function vanishes at a fixed point,
C
is stationary with respect to
R
at the fixed point.
At the fixed point, the theory is conformal so that (
6.19
) becomes

F=\frac{c}{2}~,\qquad G=0=H~.

(6.29)

Consequently, it is equal to the central charge
C(g^{*})=c
at the fixed point
g=g^{*}
. This proves the statement of the

c
-
theorem
. Consequently, the central charge of
S
is less than or equal to that of
S^{*}
in (
6.5
)

c_{\textrm{UV}}\geq c_{\textrm{IR}}~.

Hence, one can think that the central charge
c
encodes degrees of freedom of the theory, and it decreases under a renormalization group flow since some massive particles are integrated out.
There is the corresponding theorem in four dimensions
[
KS11
]
. In six dimensions, the corresponding theorem is proven for supersymmetric theories
[
CDI16
]
.

6.3
Landau-Ginzburg effective theory and minimal models

Landau-Ginzburg effective theory

Zamolodchikov has proposed
[
Zam86a
, ISZ88-No.17]
that there exists a simple effective Lagrangian description for a special class of minimal models by the following action

\mathcal{S}=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}z\left(\frac{1}{2}(\partial\Sigma)^{2}+V(\Sigma)\right)

where the potential

V(\Sigma)=g_{1}\Sigma+g_{2}{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2}\mspace{2mu}}:}+\cdots+g_{2(p-2)}{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2(p-2)}\mspace{2mu}}:}+g{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2(p-1)}\mspace{2mu}}:}

Note that the terms
{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2p-3}\mspace{2mu}}:}
can always be removed by a shift of the field
\Sigma\to\Sigma+
const and absorbed in the linear term
g_{1}\Sigma
. Here we assume
g>0
so that there are
(p-1)
local minima, which corresponds to different phases of the model. This model is called
Landau-Ginzburg effective theory
.

6

\phi_{5,5}

\phi_{4,4}

\phi_{5,4}

\phi_{3,3}

\phi_{4,3}

\phi_{3,2}
