Table 3:
Relevant operators of conformal dimension
h<1
in the minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{6}
and the corresponding operators in the Landau-Ginzburg effective theory.

The theory consists of
2(p-2)
scalar relevant fields, associated to the operators
{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{k}\mspace{2mu}}:}
for
1\leq k\leq 2(p-1)
. On the other hand, the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p}
has
2(p-2)
primary fields with conformal dimension
h<1
. Hence, we identify the most relevant field
\phi_{2,2}
of
\mathcal{M}_{p}
(with the lowest conformal dimension) with the scalar field
\Sigma
in the Landau-Ginzburg theory. The OPE of
\Sigma
is

\Sigma(z)\Sigma(0)=\frac{1}{|z|^{2h_{2,2}}}+\frac{1}{|z|^{2h_{2,2}-h^{\prime}}}{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2}\mspace{2mu}}:}(0)+\cdots

where
h^{\prime}
is the conformal dimension of
{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2}\mspace{2mu}}:}
. On the other hand,
recalling the fusion rule of primary fields (
5.36
)

\phi_{2,2}\times\phi_{r,s}=\left[\phi_{r-1,s-1}\right]+\left[\phi_{r+1,s+1}\right]+\left[\phi_{r+1,s-1}\right]+\left[\phi_{r-1,s+1}\right]~,

so that

\phi_{2,2}\times\phi_{2,2}=[\mathbf{1}]+\left[\phi_{3,3}\right]+\left[\phi_{1,3}\right]+\left[\phi_{3,1}\right]~.

Apart from the identity operator, the conformal dimension of
\phi_{3,3}
is lower than the other two so that it is natural to identify

\phi_{3,3}={:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2}\mspace{2mu}}:}~.

The same procedure leads to

\begin{array}[]{llll}\phi_{3,3}={:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2}\mspace{2mu}}:}~,&\phi_{4,4}={:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{3}\mspace{2mu}}:}~,&\cdots&,\phi_{p-1,p-1}={:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{p-2}\mspace{2mu}}:}\\
\phi_{2,1}={:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{p-1}\mspace{2mu}}:}~,&\phi_{3,2}={:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{q}\mspace{2mu}}:}~,&\cdots&,\phi_{p-1,p-2}={:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2p-4}\mspace{2mu}}:}\end{array}

(6.30)

Moreover,
{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2p-3}\mspace{2mu}}:}
can be obtained from the OPE of
\phi_{p-1,p-2}
and
\phi_{2,2}
. The inversion formula (
5.34
) identifies
\phi_{1,3}=\phi_{p-1,p-2}
so that

\phi_{2,2}\times\phi_{1,3}=\left[\phi_{2,2}\right]+\left[\phi_{p-2,p-3}\right]

Therefore, we can identify
{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2p-3}\mspace{2mu}}:}
with the descendant
L_{-1}\overline{L}_{-1}\phi_{2,2}
of
\phi_{2,2}

{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2p-3}\mspace{2mu}}:}=\partial\overline{\partial}\Sigma

This is the equation of motion at the multicritical point (
g_{i}=0
) in Landau-Ginzburg theory.

If
g_{i}=0
, the theory is invariant under
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry
\Sigma\to-\Sigma

[
Zub86
, ISZ88-No.30]
. When
p=3
, it is the
\Sigma^{4}
-theory that describes the Ising model. When
p=4
, it is the
\Sigma^{6}
-theory that describes the tricritical Ising model. For a generic positive integer
p
, it describes the RSOS model
[
ABF84
, ISZ88-No.31]
.

The correspondence between the minimal models and the Landau-Ginzburg models can also be seen even with
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal symmetry. This is related to the ADE classification studied in §
8.3
, which admits a profound interpretation in geometry.

Minimal Models and Renormalization Group Flows

In fact, the minimal models provide a perfect platform to understand renormalization group flows
[
Zam87
]
. Let us consider the perturbation of the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p}
by the primary field
\phi_{1,3}

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{p}+g\int\frac{d^{2}x}{2\pi}\phi_{1,3}(x)~

where we assume
p\gg 1
. Then, the primary field
\phi_{1,3}
can be considered as a quasi-marginal operator since the scaling dimension is

\Delta_{1,3}=2h_{1,3}=2-\frac{4}{p+1}\equiv 2-\epsilon

with
\epsilon\ll 1
.
Following the fusion rule (
5.36
), one can read off

\phi_{1,3}\times\phi_{1,3}=1+\mathbf{C}_{1}\phi_{1,3}+\mathbf{C}_{2}\phi_{1,5}~.

Since
\phi_{1,5}
is an irrelevant operator, the operator expansion above implies the renormalization of the field
\phi_{1,3}
, which does not mix with any other fields. In fact, the structure constant is computed in
[
DF84
, ISZ88-No.15]

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\sqrt{3}}\frac{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}{(1-\epsilon/2)(1-3\epsilon/4)}\left[\frac{\Gamma(1-\epsilon/4)}{\Gamma(1+\epsilon/4)}\right]^{\frac{3}{2}}\left[\frac{\Gamma(1+3\epsilon/4)}{\Gamma(1-3\epsilon/4)}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\left[\frac{\Gamma(1+\epsilon/2)}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon/2)}\right]^{2}\frac{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}{\Gamma(1+\epsilon)}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\sqrt{3}}\left(1-\frac{3\epsilon}{4}+O\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)\right)

In Homework 8, we have seen that the
\beta
-function can be written as

\frac{dg}{d\lambda}\equiv\beta(g)=\epsilon g-\frac{\mathbf{C}_{1}}{2}g^{2}+\mathcal{O}(g^{3})~.

Using the gradient flow equation (
6.28
), one obtains

C(g)=c_{p}+\alpha\left[\frac{\epsilon}{2}g^{2}-\frac{\mathbf{C}_{1}}{6}g^{3}\right]+\mathcal{O}(g^{4})

where
c_{p}
is the central charge (
5.39
) of
\mathcal{M}_{p}
. Note that
\alpha
depends on the Zamolodchikov metric
G_{ab}
in (
6.27
), and
\alpha
can be evaluated as follows. The difference between the central charges at UV and IR can be computed by (
6.24
)

\Delta c=-\frac{3}{4}\int_{0}^{\infty}d(r^{2})r^{2}\langle\Theta(r)\Theta(0)\rangle

Furthermore, (
6.9
) tells us that

\displaystyle\Delta c

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{4}(\epsilon g)^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}d(r^{2})r^{2}\langle\phi_{1,3}(r)\phi_{1,3}(0)\rangle

(6.31)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}(\epsilon g)^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{3}dr}{r^{2\Delta_{1,3}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{4}\epsilon g^{2}+\mathcal{O}({\epsilon^{0}})~.

Therefore, we have
\alpha=-\frac{3}{2}
. At the fixed point
g^{*}=2\epsilon/\mathbf{C}_{1}
, the Zamolodchikov
C
-function is

\displaystyle C(g^{*})

\displaystyle=c_{p}-\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{\mathbf{C}_{1}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\epsilon^{4}\right)

(6.32)

\displaystyle=c_{p}-\frac{3}{4^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{4}{p}\right)^{3}+\mathcal{O}\left(p^{-4}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{p}-\frac{12}{p^{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(p^{-4}\right)\sim c_{p-1}

At this perturbative order, the new value of the central charge coincides with that of the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p-1}
. Therefore, the perturbation by
\phi_{1,3}
leads to the renormalization group flow from
\mathcal{M}_{p}
to
\mathcal{M}_{p-1}
.
Although we have assumed
p\gg 1
in this analysis, the evidence of RG flow from
\mathcal{M}_{p}
to
\mathcal{M}_{p-1}
for an arbitrary
p
has been shown by using factorized
S
-matrices
[
Zam91
]
. In fact, from (
6.15
), one can deduce the conformal dimension of the operator
\phi_{1,3}
at the fixed point

2-\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial g}\Big|_{g=g^{*}}=2+\epsilon+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})~,

which can be read off as the conformal dimension of
\phi_{3,1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{p-1}
at the fixed point. Thus, the relevant operator at UV becomes the irrelevant operator at IR. (See Figure
24
.)

Figure 24:
RG-flow between minimal models

In fact, this is consistent with the Landau-Ginzburg effective theory. As we have seen in (
6.30
),
\phi_{1,3}=\phi_{p-1,p-2}\sim{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2p-4}\mspace{2mu}}:}
. Hence, the Landau-Ginzburg action for
\mathcal{M}_{p}
with the perturbation is

\mathcal{S}=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}z\left(\frac{1}{2}(\partial\Sigma)^{2}+g_{2p-4}\Sigma^{2p-4}+g_{2p-2}\Sigma^{2p-2}\right)~.

Then, the operator
{:\mathrel{\mspace{2mu}\Sigma^{2p-2}\mspace{2mu}}:}
becomes irrelevant and the effective action is

\mathcal{S}=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}z\left(\frac{1}{2}(\partial\Sigma)^{2}+g_{2p-4}\Sigma^{2p-4}\right)~,

which is the effective action for
\mathcal{M}_{p-1}
.

7
Wess-Zumino-Novikov-Witten Models

This section is devoted to studying a very important class of exactly solvable 2d conformal field theories called
Wess-Zumino-Novikov-Witten (WZNW) models

[
WZ71
,
Nov82
,
Wit84
]
. The WZNW models have many applications related to condensed matter physics, AdS/CFT correspondence, knot theory, and mathematical physics. We also refer the reader to
[
FMS97
, §15,§16]
,
[
Gin88a
, §9]
and
[
BP09
, §3]
.

7.1
U(1) symmetry of free boson

Let us recall the free boson theory in §
4.1
whose action is given by

\mathcal{S}=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int d^{2}x\,\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial^{\mu}\varphi=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int d^{2}z\,\partial\varphi\overline{\partial}\varphi

(7.1)

This action has a symmetry

\varphi\to\varphi+a

(7.2)

whose Noether current is
j^{\mu}=-\partial^{\mu}\varphi/4\pi
.
The current conservation follows from the equation of motion (
3.78
)

\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}=0~.

As in §
4.1
, one can decompose the current into holomorphic and anti-holomorphic part

J(z)\equiv i\partial_{z}\varphi~,\qquad\overline{J}(\overline{z})\equiv-i\overline{\partial}\varphi

(7.3)

so that the (holomorphic part of) energy-momentum tensor can be written as

T(z)=-\frac{1}{2}:\partial\varphi\partial\varphi:=\frac{1}{2}:J(z)J(z):~.

As we will see below, this is called
Sugawara construction
of the energy-momentum tensor. In addition, the action (
7.1
)
can be written as

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\,\partial^{\mu}g^{-1}\partial_{\mu}g=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\,\partial g^{-1}\overline{\partial}g\,,

(7.4)

where
g(z,\overline{z})
is the map from

g:\mathbb{C}\to\mathrm{U}(1);\,z\mapsto g(z,\overline{z})=\exp(i\varphi(z,\overline z))~.

This action can be regarded as the simplest version of WZNW models.
In fact, the symmetry can be understood as the
\mathrm{U}(1)
rotation

e^{i\varphi(z,\overline{z})}\mapsto e^{i(\varphi(z,\overline{z})+a)}~,

so that (
7.3
) is called the
\mathrm{U}(1)
current.
The
\mathrm{U}(1)
group is an abelian group and it is natural to ask if we can generalize this simple construction to non-abelian Lie groups. This section answers this question.

7.2
SU(2) current algebra

Before moving to the WZNW models, we shall provide the easiest introduction to the non-abelian generalization. In (
4.3
), we have seen that the compactified boson enjoys the
T
-duality
R\leftrightarrow 2/R
(
4.91
) and
R=\sqrt{2}
is the self-dual radius. At the self-radius, one can define the vertex operators

J(z)=\frac{i\partial\varphi(z)}{\sqrt{2}}~,\qquad J^{\pm}(z)=:e^{\pm i\sqrt{2}\varphi(z)}:

where they satisfy the OPE

\displaystyle J(z)J^{\pm}(w)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\pm J^{\pm}(w)}{z-w}

(7.5)

\displaystyle J^{+}(z)J^{-}(w)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{2J(w)}{z-w}.

\displaystyle J(z)J(w)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1/2}{(z-w)^{2}}

Furthermore, if we write

J^{\pm}\equiv J^{1}\pm iJ^{2},\quad J\equiv J^{3}~,

(7.6)

then the OPE amounts to

J^{a}(z)J^{b}(w)\sim\frac{\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ab}}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{i\epsilon^{abc}J^{c}(w)}{z-w}

where
\epsilon^{abc}
is the anti-symmetric tensor. It is easy to see the connection to the theory of the angular momentum which is associated to the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
Lie algebra

[t^{a},t^{b}]=i\epsilon^{abc}t^{c}

(7.7)

where
t^{a}=\sigma^{a}/2
with the Pauli matrices
\sigma^{a}
(
a=1,2,3
). In fact,
as we will learn in the homework, the OPE can be generalized to

J^{a}(z)J^{b}(w)\sim\frac{\frac{k}{2}\delta_{ab}}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{i\epsilon^{abc}J^{c}(w)}{z-w}~.

We note that

J(z)=\sum_{a=1}^{3}J^{a}(z)t^{a}

is called the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
current and
k
is its
level
. As usual, the mode expansion

J^{a}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}J_{n}^{a}

leads to the following commutation relations, which is called is called the
\mathrm{SU}(2)

current algebra
,

\left[J_{m}^{a},J_{n}^{b}\right]=i\epsilon^{abc}J_{m+n}^{c}+\frac{k}{2}m\delta^{ab}\delta_{m+n,0}

where the zero modes satisfy the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
Lie algebra (
7.7
). In mathematics, it is called the
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}
affine Lie algebra
with level
k
. This can be further generalized to the so-called
Kac-Moody algebra
.

Let us briefly study the highest weight representation of the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
current algebra. From (
7.6
), we introduce

J_{n}^{\pm}\equiv J_{n}^{1}\pm iJ_{n}^{2}

where the Hermitian conjugates are defined as

\left(J_{n}^{\pm}\right)^{\dagger}=J_{-n}^{\mp},\quad\left(J_{n}^{3}\right)^{\dagger}=J_{-n}^{3}~.

Then, the highest weight state
|h,j\rangle
is defined as

J_{0}^{3}|h,j\rangle=j|h,j\rangle~,\quad J_{0}^{+}|h,j\rangle=0,\qquad J_{n}^{a}|h,j\rangle=0,\quad n>0,\quad a=0,\pm~.

(7.8)

Note that
h
is the conformal dimension

L_{0}|h,j\rangle=\frac{j(j+1)}{k+2}|h,j\rangle

which is left in homework.
Since the zero modes satisfy the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
Lie algebra,
j
takes an half-integer value
j=\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},2,\cdots
associated to the spin-
j
representation.
In addition, it is straightforward to check that the following generators

J_{(1)}^{+}=J_{-1}^{+},\quad J_{(1)}^{-}=J_{+1}^{-},\quad J_{(1)}^{3}=J_{0}^{3}-\frac{k}{2}

also form the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
Lie algebra, and we call it
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(1)}
. Since the eigenvalue of
2J_{(1)}^{3}=2J_{0}^{3}-k
is an integer,
k
has to be an integer. Moreover, the unitarity imposes

\displaystyle 0\leqq\left|J_{(1)}^{+}|h,j\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle h,j\left|J_{+1}^{-}J_{-1}^{+}\right|h,j\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle h,j\left|\left[J_{+1}^{-},J_{-1}^{+}\right]\right|h,j\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\langle j|(J_{0}^{3}-k/2)|h,j\rangle

\displaystyle=-2j+k

Therefore, for a given level
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the spin
j
is allowed to take the values

j=0,\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},\cdots,\frac{k}{2}~.

7.3
A crash course on Lie groups and Lie algebras

It is well-known that the theory of angular momentum in quantum mechanics is described by the representation theory of the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
Lie algebra (
7.7
). We want to generalize the current algebra to arbitrary Lie algebras. Thus, we shall briefly review Lie groups and Lie algebras. For more details, we refer the reader to
[
Kir08
]
.

Definition 7.1
(Lie group).

A
Lie group
is a manifold
G
with a group structure such that multiplication
m:G\times G\to G
and inverse
i:G\to G
are smooth maps. The
dimension
of a Lie group
G
is the dimension of the underlying manifold.

Some Lie groups will be given by subsets of the space
M_{n}(\mathbb{F})
of
n\times n
matrices where
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
specified by certain algebraic equations. For example,

•

General linear group:
\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{F})=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{F})|\det A\neq 0\}

•

Special linear group:
\mathrm{SL}(n,\mathbb{F})=\{A\in\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{F})|\det A=1\}

•

Symplectic group
\mathrm{Sp}(n,\mathbb{F})=\{A\in\mathrm{GL}(2n,\mathbb{F})|A^{\mathrm{T}}JA=J\ \textrm{where}\ J=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\}

•

Unitary group
\mathrm{U}(n)=\{A\in\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{C})|AA^{\dagger}=I\}

•

Special unitary group
\mathrm{SU}(n)=\{A\in\mathrm{U}(n)|\det A=1\}

•

Orthogonal group
\mathrm{O}(n)=\{A\in\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})|AA^{T}=I\}

•

Special orthogonal group
\mathrm{SO}(n)=\{A\in\mathrm{O}(n)|\det A=1\}

In fact, the simple Lie groups are classified by Killing and Cartan, and
\mathrm{SU}(n+1)
,
\mathrm{SO}(2n+1)
,
\mathrm{Sp}(n,\mathbb{R})
and
\mathrm{SO}(2n)
are assigned to type
A_{n}
,
B_{n}
,
C_{n}
and
D_{n}
, respectively. In addition to these four types, there are five exceptional Lie groups called
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
,
F_{4}
, and
G_{2}
. The Dynkin diagrams of type
A
,
D
and
E
are all simply laced, but the other types are not.

Roughly speaking, an element
X\in\mathfrak{g}
of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
describes an element
h\in G
near the identity element
1\in G
via

h=1+\epsilon X+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})~.

More precisely, the tangent space
{T}_{1}(G)
of a Lie group
G
at the identity element
1
naturally admits a Lie bracket

[\cdot,\cdot]:T_{1}G\times T_{1}G\to T_{1}G;(X,Y)\mapsto[X,Y]=XY-YX

such that the Lie algebra of

\mathfrak{g}=(T_{1}(G),[\cdot,\cdot])

is a Lie algebra.

Definition 7.2
(Lie algebra of a Lie group).

Let
G
be a Lie group. The
Lie algebra
of
G
, written
\mathfrak{g}
, is the tangent space
T_{1}G
under the natural Lie bracket.

Given a vector
X\in\mathfrak{g}
in the tangent space of the identity element
1
, the exponential map defines a map
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G
such that

\exp(tX)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ell!}(tX)^{\ell}~.

Without relying on a Lie group, one can define a Lie algebra as follows:

Definition 7.3
(Lie algebra).

A
Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}
is a vector space (over
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
) with a
bracket

[\cdot,\cdot]:\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}

satisfying

1.

[\alpha X+\beta Y,Z]=\alpha[X,Z]+\beta[Y,Z]
for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{F}
(bilinearity)

2.

[X,Y]=-[Y,X]
for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
(antisymmetry)

3.

[X,[Y,Z]]+[Y,[Z,X]]+[Z,[X,Y]]=0
for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{g}
. (Jacobi identity)

Note that linearity in the second argument follows from linearity in the first argument and antisymmetry.

For instance, we have the corresponding Lie algebras

•

\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbb{F})=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{F})\}

•

\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{F})=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{F})|\Tr A=0\}

•

\mathfrak{sp}(n,\mathbb{F})=\{X\in\mathfrak{gl}(2n,\mathbb{F}))|X^{T}J+JX=0\ \textrm{where}\ J=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}\}

•

\mathfrak{su}(n)=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{C})|\Tr A=0,A^{\dagger}=-A\}

•

\mathfrak{so}(n)=\{A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})|A^{T}=-A\}

Given a finite-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, we can pick a basis for
\mathfrak{g}
:

t^{a}:a=1,\cdots,\dim\mathfrak{g}~

(7.9)

with

\Tr(t^at^b)=\frac{1}{2}\delta^{ab}~,

which is called the Cartan-Killing form.
Then any
X\in\mathfrak{g}
can be written as

X=X^{a}t^{a}=i\sum_{a=1}^{n}X^{a}t^{a},

where
X^{a}\in\mathbb{F}
.

By linearity, the bracket of elements
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
can be computed via

[X,Y]=-X^{a}Y^{b}[t^{a},t^{b}].

In other words, the whole structure of the Lie algebra can be given by the bracket of basis vectors. We know that
[t^{a},t^{b}]
is again an element of
\mathfrak{g}
. So we can write

[t^{a},t^{b}]=if_{ab}{}^{c}t^{c},

where
f_{ab}{}^{c}\in\mathbb{F}
are called the
structure constants
.
By the antisymmetry of the bracket, we know

f_{ba}{}^{c}=-f_{ab}{}^{c}.

The Jacobi identity amounts to

f_{ab}{}^{c}f_{cd}{}^{e}+f_{da}{}^{c}f_{cb}{}^{e}+f_{bd}{}^{c}f_{ca}{}^{e}=0.

Affinization

Hence, one can straightforwardly generalize the current associated to a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
as follows. First, the current taking its value on a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
can be written

J(z)=\sum_{a}J^{a}(z)t^{a}~.

The OPE of the current takes the form

J^{a}(z)J^{b}(w)\sim\frac{\frac{k}{2}\delta^{ab}}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{if^{ab}{}_{c}J^{c}(w)}{z-w}

so that the modes are subject to

\left[J_{n}^{a},J_{m}^{b}\right]=if^{ab}J_{n+m}^{c}+\frac{k}{2}\delta^{ab}n\delta_{n+m,0}~.

This is called the
G
current algebra with level
k
or the
\widehat{}\mathfrak{g}_{k}
affine Lie algebra with level
k
.

7.4
WZNW models

Nonlinear Sigma Models

In searching for an explicit conformal field theory with the
G
current algebra,
it is natural to first consider the
nonlinear sigma model

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\Tr(\pd^\mu g^{-1} \pd_\mu g)\,,

(7.10)

where
a^{2}
is a positive, dimensionless coupling constant.
The bosonic field
g(x)
takes its value on the Lie group
G
, namely

g:\Sigma\to G

where
\Sigma
is generally a two-dimensional manifold, called a Riemann surface.
Under the variation of the action

\delta\mathcal{S}_{0}=-\frac{1}{a^{2}}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\Tr(g^{-1}\delta g \pd^\mu(g^{-1}\pd_\mu g))\,,

(7.11)

which results in the following equation of motion

\partial^{\mu}(g^{-1}\partial_{\mu}g)=0\,.

(7.12)

This implies the conservation of the currents

J_{\mu}=g^{-1}\partial_{\mu}g\,.

(7.13)

In the complex coordinate
z=x^{0}+ix^{1}
and

\overline{z}=x^{0}-ix^{1}
. If we write
J_{z}=g^{-1}\partial g

and
\overline{J}_{\overline{z}}=g^{-1}\overline{\partial}g
, we find

\partial\overline{J}_{\overline{z}}+\overline{\partial}J_{z}=0\,.

(7.14)

So that the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic currents can not
be separately conserved.

WZNW Models

Hence, a more complicated action must be considered to
enhance the symmetry. We should add a Wess-Zumino term
[
WZ71
]

\Gamma=\frac{-i}{12\pi}\int_{B}\differential^{3}y\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\Tr(\tg^{-1}\pd^\alpha\tg\tg^{-1} \pd^\beta\tg\tg^{-1}
\pd^\gamma\tg)\,.

(7.15)

This is defined on a three-dimensional manifold
B
, whose boundary is the Riemann surface
\partial\Sigma=B
. Here
\widetilde{g}:B\to G
is an extension of the original
field
g
. Although the extension is not unique, this is well-defined.
Given another choice
(B^{\prime},\widetilde{g}^{\prime})
, we can glue together the three-manifolds
B
and
B^{\prime}
along
\Sigma
to get a closed three-manifold
B\cup_{\Sigma}-B^{\prime}
. It is easy to check that
\omega\equiv\Tr(\tg^{-1}\pd^\alpha\tg\tg^{-1} \pd^\beta\tg\tg^{-1}
\pd^\gamma\tg)
is a closed three-form, and moreover it is pull-back of an element
\Tr(\theta\wedge\theta\wedge\theta)
of the third homology group
H^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z})
with integer coefficients
[
PS86
, Prop 4.4.5]
via
\widetilde{g}:M\to G
where
\theta
is the Maurer-Cartan form of
G
.
Hence, the integral of
\omega
over the closed three-manifold
B\cup_{\Sigma}-B^{\prime}
is an integer.
Therefore, the Euclidean functional integral, with weight
\exp(-k\Gamma)
is perfectly well-defined if the level
k
is
an integer.

We then consider the action

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{0}+k\Gamma\,,

(7.16)

where
k
is an integer. Since the variation of the action can be written as a total derivative,
the Wess-Zumino term is a
2
d functional.
The final result is

\delta\Gamma=-\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\Tr(g^{-1}\delta g \partial^\mu(g^{-1}\pd^\nu g))\,.

(7.17)

The equation of motion of the action (
7.16
)
is then

\partial^{\mu}(g^{-1}\partial_{\mu}g)+\frac{a^{2}ik}{4\pi}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\mu}(g^{-1}\partial^{\nu}g)=0\,.

(7.18)

In terms of the complex variable
z
,
\overline{z}
, the equation
of motion becomes

\bigg(1+\frac{a^{2}k}{4\pi}\bigg)\partial(g^{-1}\overline{\partial}g)+\bigg(1-\frac{a^{2}k}{4\pi}\bigg)\overline{\partial}(g^{-1}\partial g)=0\,.

(7.19)

Thus, for

a^{2}=4\pi/k\,,

(7.20)

we find the desired conservation law

\partial(g^{-1}\overline{\partial}g)=0\,,\qquad\overline{\partial}((\partial g)g^{-1})=0\,.

(7.21)

The action now becomes

\mathcal{S}=\frac{k}{8\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\Tr(\pd^\mu g^{-1}\pd_\mu g)+k\Gamma\,,

(7.22)

which is called the
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}

WZNW (WZNW) model
.
Since
a^{2}
is positive,
k
must be a positive integer.
The other solution
a^{2}=-4\pi/k
, which requires

k<0
, implies the conservation of the dual currents.

Due to the Noether theorem, the conservation of the currents

J_{z}
and
\overline{J}_{\overline{z}}
(
7.21
) should imply the invariance of the
action. In fact, (
7.21
) tells us that the field
g
is factorized as

g(z,\overline{z})=g_{L}(z)g_{R}(\overline{z})~.

We will see, under the transformation

g(z,\overline{z})\rightarrow\Omega(z)g(z,\overline{z})\overline{\Omega}^{-1}(\overline{z})\,,

(7.23)

where
\Omega
and
\overline{\Omega}
are two arbitrary matrices
valued in
G
, the action is invariant. Indeed, under the infinitesimal transformation

\Omega(z)=1+\omega(z)\,,\qquad\overline{\Omega}(\overline{z})=1+\overline{\omega}(\overline{z})\,.

(7.24)

g
transforms as follows

\delta_{\omega}g=\omega g\,,\qquad\delta_{\overline{\omega}}g=-g\overline{\omega}\,.

(7.25)

With
a^{2}=4\pi/k
, the variation of the action for

g\rightarrow g+\delta_{\omega}g+\delta_{\overline{\omega}}g
is

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\Tr(g^{-1}\delta g [\pd(g^{-1}\overline{\partial} g)])

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\Tr[\omega(z)\overline{\partial}(\partial gg^{-1})-\overline{\omega}(\overline{z})\partial(g^{-1}\overline{\partial}g)]\,,

which vanishes after an integral by parts. Thus the global

G\times G
invariance of the sigma model has thus been
extended to a local
G(z)\times G(\overline{z})
invariance.

By rescaling the conserved currents as

\displaystyle J(z)

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{k}{2}J_{z}(z)=-\frac{k}{2}\partial gg^{-1}\,,

(7.26)

\displaystyle\overline{J}(\overline{z})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{k}{2}\overline{J}_{\overline{z}}(\overline{z})=\frac{k}{2}g^{-1}\overline{\partial}g\,.

We can rewrite (
7.4
) in the form

\delta\mathcal{S}=-\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{2}x\left\{\overline{\partial}(\Tr[\omega(z)J(z)])+\partial(\Tr[\overline{\omega}({\overline{z}})\overline{J}({\overline{z}})])\right\}\,.

(7.27)

Replace
d^{2}x
by
(-i/2)\differential z\differential\overline{z}
and take the
holomorphic contour to be counterclockwise and the antiholomorphic contour to be clockwise. This lead to

\delta_{\omega,\overline{\omega}}=\frac{i}{\pi}\oint\differential z\Tr[\omega(z)J(z)]-\frac{i}{\pi}\oint\differential\overline{z}\Tr[\overline{\omega}({\overline{z}})\overline{J}({\overline{z}})]\,.

(7.28)

Expanding
J
and
\omega
as well as their dual part in terms
of the basis
t^{\alpha}
of
G

J=\sum_{a}J^{a}t^{a}\,,\qquad\omega=\sum_{a}\omega^{a}t^{a}\,,

(7.29)

The variation of the action now becomes

\delta_{\omega,\overline{\omega}}S=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\differential z\sum_{a}\omega^{a}J^{a}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\differential\overline{z}\sum_{a}\overline{\omega}^{a}\overline{J}^{a}\,.

(7.30)

Let
X
stand for a list of fields as usual, from (
3.14
) and (
3.34
), we have

\delta\expectationvalue{X}=\expectationvalue{(\delta S) X}\,.

(7.31)

Hence, we immediately obtain the
corresponding Ward-identity

\delta_{\omega,\overline{\omega}}\expectationvalue{X}=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\differential z\sum_{a}\omega^{a}\expectationvalue{J^a X}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint\differential\overline{z}\sum_{a}\overline{\omega}^{a}\expectationvalue{\overline{J}^a X}\,.

(7.32)

From (
7.26
) and (
7.25
),
the transformation law for the current is

\delta_{\omega}J=\commutator{\omega}{J}-\frac{k}{2}\partial_{z}\omega\,.

(7.33)

Writing in terms of
J^{a}
and
\omega^{a}
, we have

\delta_{\omega}J^{a}=\sum_{b,c}if_{abc}\omega^{b}J^{c}-\frac{k}{2}\partial_{z}\omega^{a}\,.

(7.34)

The substitution of this transformation into (
7.32
), leads to so-called
current algebra.

J^{a}(z)J^{b}(w)\sim\frac{\frac{k}{2}\delta_{ab}}{(z-w)^{2}}+\sum_{c}if_{abc}\frac{J^{c}(w)}{(z-w)}\,.

(7.35)

Introducing the modes
J^{a}_{n}
from the Laurent expansion

J^{a}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-1}J^{a}_{n}\,.

(7.36)

We can easily check (as we have done in the Virasoro case),
the commutation relations of
J^{a}_{m}
become

\commutator{J^a_n}{J^b_m}=\sum_{c}if_{abc}J^{c}_{n+m}+\frac{k}{2}n\delta_{ab}\delta_{n+m,0}\,,

(7.37)

which is the
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
affine Lie algebra at level
k
. The dual part
\overline{J}
is similar.

7.5
Sugawara construction

The energy-momentum tensor of the theory is given by Sugawara
construction

T(z)=\frac{1}{(k+h^{\vee})}\sum_{a}(J^{a}J^{a})(z)\,.

(7.38)

The prefactor

\frac{1}{(k+h^{\vee})}
is determined by the usual form of
TT
OPE, and
h^{\vee}
is called the
dual Coxeter number
of
\mathfrak{g}
.

By using the generalized wick theorem which we have encountered
in the homework, we can derive the
TJ
OPE

T(z)J^{a}(w)=\frac{J^{a}(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial J^{a}(w)}{(z-w)}\,.

(7.39)

Therefore,
J^{a}
is a Virasoro primary field with conformal dimension
1
.

TT
OPE can be calculated straightforwardly

T(z)T(w)=\frac{c/2}{(z-w)^{4}}+\frac{2T(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial T(w)}{(z-w)}\,,

(7.40)

with the central charge
c

c=\frac{k\,\text{dim}(\mathfrak{g})}{k+h^{\vee}}\,.

(7.41)

The Sugawara energy-momentum tensor can be written in terms
of mode expansion the same as before

T(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{-n-2}L_{n}\,.

(7.42)

With the OPEs listed above, we can compute the complete
affine Lie and Virasoro algebra

\displaystyle\commutator{L_n}{L_m}

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0}\,,

(7.43)

\displaystyle\commutator{L_n}{J^a_m}

\displaystyle=-mJ^{a}_{n+m}\,,

\displaystyle\commutator{J^a_n}{J^b_m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{c}if_{abc}J^{c}_{n+m}+\frac{k}{2}n\delta_{ab}\delta_{n+m,0}\,.

Finally, for future discussion, we need to write the relation
between
L_{n}
and
J^{a}_{n}
. From (
7.38
) and the mode expansion of

J^{a}
(
7.36
), the relation can be obtained:

L_{n}=\frac{1}{(k+h^{\vee})}\sum_{a}\sum_{m}:J^{a}_{m}J^{a}_{n-m}:\,.

(7.44)

7.6
Knizhinik-Zamolodchikov equations

WZNW primary field

A WZNW primary field is defined as a field that transforms
covariantly with respect to a
G(z)\times G(\overline{z})
transformation. From (
7.25
) and (
7.32
), we reformulate
Jg
OPE as
following

\displaystyle J^{a}(z)g(w,\overline{w})

\displaystyle\sim\frac{-t^{a}g(w,\overline{w})}{z-w}\,,

(7.45)

\displaystyle\overline{J}^{a}(z)g(w,\overline{w})

\displaystyle\sim\frac{g(w,\overline{w})t^{a}}{z-w}\,.

This can be generalized to define a WZNW primary field. Any
WZNW primary field
\phi_{\lambda,\mu}
with
\lambda
and
\mu

specified the representation in the holomorphic and antiholomorphic sector respectively should satisfy the
following OPE

J^{a}(z)\phi_{\lambda,\mu}(w,\overline{w})\sim\frac{-t^{a}_{\lambda}\phi_{\lambda,\mu}(w,\overline{w})}{z-w}\,,

(7.46)

\overline{J}^{a}(\overline{z})\phi_{\lambda,\mu}(w,\overline{w})\sim\frac{\phi_{\lambda,\mu}(w,\overline{w})t^{a}_{\mu}}{\overline{z}-\overline{w}}\,,

(7.47)

where
t^{a}_{\lambda}
is the matrix
t^{a}
in the
\lambda

representation. By analogy with (
3.54
), the above OPE can be extended

J^{a}(z)\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})\cdots\phi_{n}(z_{n})\sim-\sum_{i}\frac{t^{a}_{i}}{z-z_{i}}\phi_{1}(z_{1})\phi_{2}(z_{2})\cdots\phi_{n}(z_{n})\,,

(7.48)

where we consider only the holomorphic sector, with

t_{i}^{a}
a matrix acting on
\phi_{i}
.

By expanding the currents in terms of the modes evaluated at

w
,

J^{a}(z)=\sum_{n}(z-w)^{-n-1}J^{a}_{n}(w)\,.

(7.49)

We can write their OPE with an arbitrary field
A
as we have done in the Virasoro case

J^{a}(z)A(w)=\sum_{n}(z-w)^{-n-1}(J^{a}_{n}A)(w)\,.

(7.50)

Thus, from (
7.46
), for the WZNW holomorphic primary field

\phi_{\lambda}
, we have

\displaystyle J^{a}_{0}\phi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-t^{a}_{\lambda}\phi_{\lambda}\,,

(7.51)

\displaystyle J^{a}_{n}\phi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{for}\qquad n>0\,.

Associating the state
\ket{\phi_\lambda}
to the field

\phi_{\lambda}
as usual

\phi_{\lambda}(0)\ket{0}=\ket{\phi_\lambda}\,.

(7.52)

Then, the condition for a WZNW primary field translates into

\displaystyle J^{a}_{0}\ket{\phi_\lambda}

\displaystyle=-t^{a}_{\lambda}\ket{\phi_\lambda}\,,

(7.53)

\displaystyle J^{a}_{n}\ket{\phi_\lambda}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{for}\qquad n>0\,.

From (
7.44
), we see that in the expression for
L_{n}
,
with
n>0
, the rightmost factor
J^{a}_{m}
has
m>0
which implies that

L_{n}\ket{\phi_\lambda}=0\qquad\text{for}\qquad n>0\,.

(7.54)

Therefore, the WZNW primary fields are also Virasoro primary fields with the conformal dimension given by

L_{0}\ket{\phi_\lambda}=\frac{1}{(k+h^{\vee})}\sum_{a}J^{a}_{0}J^{a}_{0}\ket{\phi_\lambda}=\frac{\sum_{a}t^{a}_{\lambda}t^{a}_{\lambda}}{(k+h^{\vee})}\ket{\phi_\lambda}\,.

(7.55)

The opposite is indeed not true. For example,
J^{a}(z)
is a
Virasoro primary field but not WZNW primary field which can be seen directly from
TJ
OPE and
JJ
OPE we have shown before.

Finally, a WZNW descendant state is defined of the form

J^{a}_{-n_{a}}J^{b}_{-n_{2}}\cdots\ket{\phi_\lambda}\,,

(7.56)

with
n_{1},n_{2}\cdots
all positive integers.

Knizhnik-Zamolodchikov Equations

From the previous learning, we know that there are many
constraints to the correlation function, such as the
null fields. This section briefly discusses the affine
singular vector at level one, and its constraints.

For
n=-1
, from (
7.44
), we have

L_{-1}\ket{\phi_i}=\frac{2}{k+h^{\vee}}\sum_{a}(J^{a}_{-1}J^{a}_{0})\ket{\phi_i}=\frac{-2}{k+h^{\vee}}\sum_{a}(J^{a}_{-1}t_{i}^{a})\ket{\phi_i}\,.

(7.57)

We consider the insertion of the zero vector

\ket{\chi}=\bigg[L_{-1}+\frac{2}{k+h^{\vee}}\sum_{a}(J^{a}_{-1}t_{i}^{a})\bigg]\ket{\phi_i}=0\,.

(7.58)

First, we need to calculate the contribution of the second term.
By using (
7.36
)
, the insertion of the operator
J^{a}_{-1}
in the correlator
can be expressed as

\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z_1)\cdots(J^a_{-1}\phi_i)(z_i)\cdots\phi_n(z_n)}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{z_{i}}\frac{\differential z}{z-z_{i}}\expectationvalue{J^a(z)\phi_1(z_1)\cdots\phi_n(z_n)}\,.

(7.59)

Now using (
7.48
) and
performing the residue calculation, we get

\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z_1)\cdots(J^a_{-1}\phi_i)(z_i)\cdots\phi_n(z_n)}=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{t^{a}_{j}}{z_{i}-z_{j}}\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z_1)\cdots\phi_n(z_n)}\,.

(7.60)

Thus

\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z_1)\cdots\chi(z_i)\cdots\phi_n(z_n)}=\bigg[\partial_{z_{i}}+\frac{2}{k+h^{\vee}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\sum_{a}t^{a}_{i}\otimes t^{a}_{j}}{z_{i}-z_{j}}\bigg]\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z_1)\cdots\phi_n(z_n)}\,,

(7.61)

where
t^{a}_{j}
and
t^{a}_{i}
act on
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{j}

respectively. By construction, it must vanish so that
we have

\bigg[\partial_{z_{i}}+\frac{2}{k+h^{\vee}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\sum_{a}t^{a}_{i}\otimes t^{a}_{j}}{z_{i}-z_{j}}\bigg]\expectationvalue{\phi_1(z_1)\cdots\phi_n(z_n)}=0\,.

(7.62)

This is called the
Knizhnik-Zamolodchikov equation

[
KZ84
, ISZ88-No.4]
. The
solutions to this equation are the correlation functions
of WZNW primary fields.

7.7
Coset models

Using current algebras, one can construct an important class of 2d CFTs called
coset construction

[
GKO86
, ISZ88-No.11]
. This construction has many applications in physics like (supersymmetric) unitary minimal models (see the end of §
8.3
) and Kazama-Suzuki models
[
KS89
]
.

We start with the current algebra associated to a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
which contains
\mathfrak{h}
as subgroups. The Sugawara energy-momentum tensors of affine Lie algebra
\widehat{}\mathfrak{g}_{k_{\mathfrak{g}}}
and
\widehat{}\mathfrak{h}_{k_{\mathfrak{h}}}
are

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{g}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{\mathfrak{g}}+h^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\sum_{a=1}^{\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{g}}:J_{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}J_{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}:(z)

(7.63)

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{h}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{\mathfrak{h}}+h^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{h}}}\sum_{b=1}^{\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{h}}:J_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}J_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}:(z)

We define the energy-momentum tensor of the coset model
\widehat{}\mathfrak{g}_{k_{\mathfrak{g}}}/\widehat{}\mathfrak{h}_{k_{\mathfrak{h}}}
as

T_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}:=T_{\mathfrak{g}}-T_{\mathfrak{h}}~.

Then, we have

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{g}}(z)J_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{J_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial_{w}\dot{j}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}(w)}{z-w}+\cdots

(7.64)

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{h}}(z)J_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{J_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}(w)}{(z-w)^{2}}+\frac{\partial_{w}J_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}(w)}{z-w}+\cdots

which yield

\displaystyle\left(T_{\mathfrak{g}}-T_{\mathfrak{h}}\right)(z)J_{\mathfrak{h}}^{b}(w)

\displaystyle\sim 0

(7.65)

\displaystyle\left(T_{\mathfrak{g}}-T_{\mathfrak{h}}\right)(z)T_{\mathfrak{h}}(w)

\displaystyle\sim 0~.

Therefore, one can compute the
T_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}T_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}
OPE so that the central charge of the coset model is

c_{\mathrm{g}/\mathrm{h}}=c_{\mathfrak{g}}-c_{\mathrm{h}}=\frac{k_{\mathfrak{g}}\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{g}}{k_{\mathfrak{g}}+h^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{g}}}-\frac{k_{\mathfrak{h}}\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{h}}{k_{\mathfrak{h}}+h^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{h}}}~.

(7.66)

Parafermion

As the simplest model of the coset model, we consider
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}/\widehat{\mathfrak{u}(1)}_{k}
. The energy-momentum tensor
T_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}}=T_{\mathfrak{g}}-T_{\mathfrak{h}}
where

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{g}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k+2}\sum_{a=1}^{3}:J^{a}(z)J^{a}(z):

(7.67)

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{h}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}:J^{3}(z)J^{3}(z):

If we define
J^{3}
as

J^{3}(z)=i\sqrt{\frac{k}{2}}\partial\varphi(z)

then
T_{H}
is the energy-momentum tensor of the free boson. It is easy to read off central charge

c_{\mathrm{g}/\mathrm{h}}=\frac{3k}{k+2}-1=\frac{2(k-1)}{k+2}~,

which is equal to (
5.96
). Indeed, this coset model describes the 2d CFT with
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
symmetry
[
FZ85
, ISZ88-No.14]
. This model contains a current with fractional spin (conformal dimension), which does obey neither bose nor fermi statistics, and is rather subject to para-statistics. Therefore, it is called
\mathbb{Z}_{k}

parafermion
.
In terms of the free boson
\varphi
and the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
current
J^{\pm}
, the parafermions are defined by

\left(J^{+}\right)^{\ell}=\psi_{\ell}e^{i\ell\sqrt{\frac{2}{k}}X},\quad\left(J^{-}\right)^{\ell}=\psi_{\ell}^{\dagger}e^{-i\ell\sqrt{\frac{2}{k}}X},\quad(\ell=0,\cdots,k)

where
\psi_{\ell}
and
\psi_{\ell}^{\dagger}
have conformal dimension
h=\ell(k-\ell)/k
.

When
k=1
, the central charge is
c=0
so that the theory is trivial.
When
k=2
, the central charge is
c=\frac{1}{2}
so that the theory is the free fermion.
When
k=3
, the central charge is
c=\frac{4}{3}
so that the theory is the 3-state Potts model, which corresponds to the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{5}
. In this case, the primary field with conformal dimension
h=2/3
corresponds to the parafermion current
\psi_{1}
. The partition function of the parafermion is evaluated in
[
GQ87
, ISZ88-No.28]
.

In fact, the supersymmetric version of the parafermion describes
\mathcal{N}=2
unitary minimal models, which plays a crucial role to describe Calabi-Yau sigma-models
[
Gep87
,
Gep88
]
.

Rational conformal field theories

The minimal models, the WZNW models and the coset models belong to a class of rational conformal field theories (RCFTs). A theory is endowed with a chiral algebra
\mathcal{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{A}}
that contains the Virasoro algebra as a subalgebra. The definition of an RCFT is (roughly) given as follows.

•

It is unitary with a unique vacuum.

•

There are finitely many primary fields
\phi_{i}
of the chiral algebra
\mathcal{A}
. The same statement holds for the anti-holomorphic part.

•

The Hilbert space is spanned by finitely many irreducible representations of
\mathcal{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{A}}

\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{i,j}\mathcal{N}_{i,j}\mathcal{V}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{j}

where the irreducible representation
\mathcal{V}_{i}
arises from primary fields
\phi_{i}
.

•

Their OPEs of primary fields are closed among themselves

\phi_{i}(z)\phi_{j}(w)\sim\sum_{k}\frac{C_{ijk}}{(z-w)^{h_{i}+h_{j}-h_{k}}}\phi_{j}(w)+\cdots

It is called an RCFT because conformal dimensions of primary fields of the chiral algebra
\mathcal{A}
as well as the central charge are rational numbers
\in\mathbb{Q}

[
Vaf88
]
. In an RCFT, conformal blocks can be, in principle, determined from structure constants
C_{ijk}
by bootstrap equations (
5.125
), and one can evaluate a correlation function on an arbitrary Riemann surface. For more details, we refer to
[
MS89a
,
MS89b
]
.

8
Modular invariant partition functions

We have already seen some examples of modular invariant partition functions, such as
torus partition functions of the free boson/fermion §
4.3
, and minimal models §
5.3
.
We first review a free boson on a circle and look into its modular invariance in special cases.
In this section, we study modular invariant partition functions more in details. We also refer the reader to
[
FMS97
, §10,§17]
and
[
BP09
, §8]
.

When we consider the torus partition function, it should always be modular invariant because of torus geometry.
Nonetheless, it is non-trivial to see some cases. Instead, the modular invariance gives us some useful information,
which can be seen in §
8.2
.

8.1
Free boson on a circle

Let us first focus on a single free boson on a circle.
The partition function on a circle of radius
R
is, as we have seen in (
4.89
):

\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\Tr\bigg( q^{L_0 -\frac{c}{24}} \overline{q}^{\overline{L}_0-\frac{c}{24}} \bigg)=\frac{1}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\sum_{n,w}q^{\frac{1}{2}(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{Rw}{2})^{2}}\overline{q}^{\frac{1}{2}(\frac{n}{R}-\frac{Rw}{2})^{2}},

(8.1)

where we changed conventions, and here,
n
is for KK-momentum and
w
for winding number.
Note that
R
dependence of the partition function means that the theory depends on the radius
R
.
We will see peculiar features of theories (partition functions) at specific values of the radius.
Now, let us study the partition function on a special radius
R=\sqrt{2k}

with
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
Rearranging the summation, the partition function can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\sqrt{2k}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\eta(\tau)}^{2}}\sum_{n,w\in\mathbb{Z}}q^{\frac{1}{4k}(n+kw)^{2}}\overline{q}^{\frac{1}{4k}(n-kw)^{2}}=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\eta(\tau)}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z}_{2k}\\
n_{L},n_{R}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}q^{\frac{1}{4k}(m+2kn_{L})^{2}}\overline{q}^{\frac{1}{4k}(m-2kn_{R})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\eta(\tau)}^{2}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{2k}}\left|\Theta_{m,k}(\tau)\right|^{2}~,

(8.2)

where the range of
k
is
-k+1\leq m\leq k
,
\Theta
-function was defined in (
5.48
).
Conformal field theories corresponding to these
partition functions are commonly denoted as

\widehat{\mathfrak{u}(1)}_{k}
, and we use this notation for the subscript from now on. Indeed, for
k=1
, we find

\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{u}(1)}_{1}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{\eta(\tau)}^{2}}\bigg(\absolutevalue{\Theta_{0,1}}^{2}+\absolutevalue{\Theta_{1,1}}^{2}\bigg)=\chi_{0}^{(1)}\overline{\chi}_{0}^{(1)}+\chi_{1}^{(1)}\overline{\chi}_{1}^{(1)}\,,

(8.3)

where
\chi_{j}^{(1)}
is the character of
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{1}
as we will see below. As we have seen in §
7.2
,
k=1
is actually the self-dual point, and the symmetry
\widehat{\mathfrak{u}(1)}_{1}
is enhanced to
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{1}
,

From the definition of
\Theta
-function, it is easy to see
the T-transformations for
\Theta
- function, we have

\Theta_{m,k}(\tau+1)=e^{\pi im^{2}/2k}\Theta_{m,k}(\tau)\,.

(8.4)

For the modular S-transformation, we have

\Theta_{m,k}\bigg(-\frac{1}{\tau}\bigg)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\sum_{m^{\prime}=-k+1}^{k}S_{m,m^{\prime}}\Theta_{m^{\prime},k}(\tau)\,,

(8.5)

with the modular S-matrix

S_{m,m^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k}}\exp(-\pi i \frac{m m'}{k})\,.

(8.6)

For
k=1
case, the S-transformation for the characters
in (
8.3
) can be
inferred from the transformation properties of the

\Theta
- and
\eta
-functions

\chi^{(1)}_{m}\big(-\frac{1}{\tau}\big)=\sum_{m^{\prime}=0}^{1}S_{m,m^{\prime}}\chi_{m^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\tau)

(8.7)

Following what we have done when constructing modular invariant
partition function in minimal model, we can write the partition function
(
8.3
) as

\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{u}(1)}_{1}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=(\chi^{(1)}_{0},\chi_{1}^{(1)})\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1&0\\0&1\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\overline{\chi}_0^{(1)}\\ \overline{\chi_1}^{(1)}\endlx@physics@matrix)=\vec{\chi}^{T}M\vec{\overline{\chi}}\,,

(8.8)

where
M
is the identity matrix in this case.
The S-transformation of the partition function now reads

\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{u}(1)}_{1}}\bigg(-\frac{1}{\tau},-\frac{1}{\overline{\tau}}\bigg)=\vec{\chi}^{T}S^{T}MS^{*}\vec{\overline{\chi}}\,.

(8.9)

Since
S^{T}=S
from the definition of S matrix, the
condition for invariance under modular S-transformation
for the partition function now reads

SMS^{\dagger}=M\,.

(8.10)

This is obviously true in this simple case. Because
under T-transformations the characters
\chi_{m}^{(1)}

only acquire a phase, we have shown that

\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{u}(1)}_{1}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})
is modular-invariant.
This process can be generalized to construct more
complicated modular invariant partition functions.

Let us look into the
k=2
case as well. The modular invariant partition function is given as follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{u(1)}_{2}}=\sum_{-1\leq m\leq 2}\left|\frac{\Theta_{m,2}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}+\left|\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}+\left|\frac{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}\right\}

(8.11)

Notice that the expression above is precisely the same as the torus partition function of a free complex fermion on a circle (
5.92
),
though the second equality above is non-trivial.
This means that a free boson on a circle at
R=2
is equivalent to a free complex fermion, and it is called “Boson-Fermion correspondence”
(or bosonization, fermionization).
We have already seen the equivalence in terms of operator correspondence in homework 4.
This is a simple example of “duality”, which provides us a pair of theories that describe the same physics
(hence their physical quantities like multi-point function and partition function agree with each other).
It provides not only different physical pictures but also important mathematical formulae, which we will see in the homework this week.

8.2
Orbifold partition function

As we saw in a free boson on a circle case, the theory depends on a ‘‘target space’’
5
5

5

The target space is a space where fields take their values. In the case of a free boson on a circle,

\varphi
takes a value on the circle
x\sim x+2\pi R
. Hence, the target space is the circle.

on which the theory lives.
In particular, the boundary condition plays an important role. For the circle case it is

\displaystyle\varphi(ze^{2\pi i},\overline{z}e^{-2\pi i})=\varphi(z,\overline{z})+2\pi wR,\qquad w\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\textrm{: winding number}.

(8.12)

Now, we want to extend our knowledge to other kinds of CFT. Therefore, we consider different target spaces.
What we consider here is the so-called orbifold.
For a manifold
M
and a discrete group
\Gamma
, a space defined by a quotient
M/\Gamma
with an equivalence relation

\displaystyle x\sim gx\qquad g\in\Gamma,\quad x\in M,

(8.13)

could be singular, and it is, in general, called orbifold.

We consider, as the simplest example, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbifold of a circle with radius
R

[
Yan87
, ISZ88-No.40]
.
In this case
M=S^{1}
and
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
whose elements are
g=\{1,-1\}
.
The non-trivial equivalence relation is, therefore,
x\sim-x
.
The resultant orbifold is denoted by
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(see Fig.
25
).

\times
x=0
\times
x=\pi R
Identify
\times
\times

Figure 25:
From a circle (
S^{1}
) with radius
2\pi R
to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbifold (
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
) of the circle.
We identify points
x
and
-x
in the circle, which leads to a finite line. There are two singular points, which are illustrated by crosses in the figure.

Our goal is to calculate a partition function of a free boson on the orbifold.
In order to achieve this we consider a free boson on a circle
\varphi\sim\varphi+2\pi R
,
and define a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry (orbifold) operator
\mathcal{R}
:

\mathcal{R}:\varphi(z,\overline{z})\mapsto-\varphi(z,\overline{z})\,,\qquad\mathcal{R}^{2}=\mathrm{Id}\,.

(8.14)

We calculate a partition function with states
invariant under the orbifold action.
Namely, we
insert an orbifold projection operator
\frac{1}{2}(1+\mathcal{R})
into the trace in (
8.1
),
and the partition function reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\textrm{orb}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})

\displaystyle=\Tr\bigg(
\frac{1+\mathcal{R}}{2} q^{L_0 -\frac{c}{24}}
\overline{q}^{\overline{L}_0-\frac{c}{24}}
\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\tau,\overline{\tau})+\frac{1}{2}\Tr\big(
\mathcal{R} q^{L_0-\frac{c}{24}}
\overline{q}^{\overline{L}_0-\frac{c}{24}}
\big)\,.

(8.15)

The first term turns out to be the partition function of a
free boson on a circle which we have already computed in
(
8.1
).
Let us focus on the second term.

The action of
\mathcal{R}
on the Laurent modes
a_{n}

of
i\partial\varphi(z)
can be found

\mathcal{R}a_{n}\mathcal{R}=-a_{n}\,.

(8.16)

Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\ket{n_1,n_2,n_3,\dots}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{R}a_{-1}\mathcal{R})^{n_{1}}(\mathcal{R}a_{-2}\mathcal{R})^{n_{2}}\dots\mathcal{R}\ket{\Omega}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}+\dots}\ket{n_1, n_2, n_3,\dots}\,.

(8.17)

where
\ket{\Omega}
is a ground state, and we assumed that it is invariant under
\mathcal{R}
, i.e
\mathcal{R}\ket{\Omega}=\ket{\Omega}
.
Now, we consider the action
\mathcal{R}
on the ground states in detail. Remember that the ground state is
characterized by KK-momentum and winding number,
\ket{\Omega}=|n,w\rangle
.
Since the zero mode operator
a_{0}
gives eigenvalues of the states

\displaystyle a_{0}\ket{n,w}=\left(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{Rw}{2}\right)\ket{n,w},

(8.18)

we see that

\displaystyle a_{0}\mathcal{R}\ket{n,w}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{R}a_{0}\mathcal{R})\ket{n,w}=\mathcal{R}(-a_{0})\ket{n,w}=-\bigg(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{Rw}{2}\bigg)\mathcal{R}\ket{n,w}\,,

\displaystyle\therefore\quad\mathcal{R}\ket{m,n}\propto\ket{-m,-n}\,.

(8.19)

Therefore, only states with
\ket{m=0, n= 0}
will
contribute to the partition function.
Then following the same steps in (
4.81
),
we replace the result of the free boson as

q^{-\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1-q^{n}}\quad\to\quad q^{-\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1-(-q^{n})}=\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{\eta{(\tau)}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)}}\,.

(8.20)

We thus write the partition function as

\mathcal{Z}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\tau,\overline{\tau})+\absolutevalue{\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\vartheta_2(\tau)}}\,.

(8.21)

Notice that the result (
8.21
)
can not be the correct partition function because the second term is not invariant under modular transformation.
Recalling the free fermions partition function result,
\vartheta
-functions
enjoy the modular transformations illustrated in Fig.
13
.
In order for a free boson on the orbifold to have modular invariance,
we are required to add the so-called twisted sector, whose contributions should be

\mathcal{Z}_{\text{tw}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\absolutevalue{\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\vartheta_4(\tau)}}+\absolutevalue{\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\vartheta_3(\tau)}}\,.

(8.22)

Then, the modular invariant partition function of a free boson on the

\mathrm{Z}_{2}
-orbifold reads

\mathcal{Z}_{\text{orb}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Z}_{R}(\tau,\overline{\tau})+\absolutevalue{\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\vartheta_2(\tau)}}+\absolutevalue{\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\vartheta_4(\tau)}}+\absolutevalue{\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\vartheta_3(\tau)}}\,.

(8.23)

Although we derived the correct result by requiring modular invariance here,
it is instructive to see what is the physical realization of the twisted sector.

Twisted sector

Recall that when we calculate the torus partition function of a free fermion on a circle,
we considered Neveu-Schwarz (NS) sector as well as Ramond (R) sector, which are realized as follows.

\displaystyle\psi\left(e^{2\pi i}z\right)=\psi(z)\qquad\!\quad\textrm{NS},

(8.24)

\displaystyle\psi\left(e^{2\pi i}z\right)=-\psi(z)\qquad\textrm{R},

(8.25)

where only the holomorphic parts are shown.
We realize that NS sector corresponds to
the boundary condition of a free boson (
8.12
),
and wonder if there is a boundary condition like the R sector.
Indeed, there exists due to the operation
\mathcal{R}
:

\displaystyle\varphi(ze^{2\pi i},\overline{z}e^{-2\pi i})=\mathcal{R}\varphi(z,\overline{z})+2\pi wR=-\varphi(z,\overline{z})+2\pi wR.

(8.26)

To realize this boundary condition, the mode has to become “half-integers” modes:

\displaystyle\varphi(z,\overline{z})=\varphi(z)+\overline{\varphi}(\overline{z}),

\displaystyle\quad\varphi(z)=\frac{x}{2}+i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{n}\frac{a_{n}}{z^{n}}.

(8.27)

There are two possible values for the zero mode
x
; one is
x=0\ (w=0)
and the other is
x=\pi R\ (w=1)
.
Due to the shift of the modes the zero point energy is also modified as follows (recall homework 5:
\zeta
-function regularization).

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n=-\frac{1}{24}\quad\to\quad\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+\frac{1}{2})=-\frac{1}{24}+\frac{1}{16}=\frac{1}{48}.

(8.28)

Therefore, we have the following contribution:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{\mathrm{tw}}\left[q^{L_{0}-\frac{1}{24}}\overline{q}^{\overline{L_{0}}-\frac{1}{24}}\right]\quad\textrm{with}\quad L_{0}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{-n+\frac{1}{2}}a_{n-\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{1}{16}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\cdot|q|^{2\cdot\frac{1}{48}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\frac{1}{1-q^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}\right|^{2}=\left|\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)}\right|,

(8.29)

where the factor of two in the coefficient is coming from the two contributions of zero modes
x=0
and
x=\pi R
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Tr_{\mathrm{tw}}\left[\mathcal{R}q^{L_{0}-\frac{1}{24}}\overline{q}^{\overline{L_{0}}-\frac{1}{24}}\right]\quad\textrm{with}\quad L_{0}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{-n+\frac{1}{2}}a_{n-\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{1}{16}

\displaystyle=|q|^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\frac{1}{1+q^{n-\frac{1}{2}}}\right|^{2}=\left|\frac{\eta(\tau)}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)}\right|.

(8.30)

As we expected, we have correct contributions from the twisted sector.
There are several comments:

•

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{orb}}
depends on radius
R
only through
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
, and hence, it enjoys T-duality:

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{orb}}(R)=\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{orb}}(2/R)
.

•

One can see that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{orb}}(R=\sqrt{2})=\mathcal{Z}_{R}(R=2\sqrt{2})
.
Namely, a free boson on a circle with
R=\sqrt{2}
is equivalent to that on an
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
with
R=2\sqrt{2}
.
As we stressed before, theories depend on their radii, and two branches of a free boson theory
intersect at the point of moduli space of the theory (see Fig.
26
).

•

The
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal symmetry arises at
R_{\textrm{circ}}=\sqrt{3}
, and
\mathcal{N}=1
superconformal symmetry arises at
R_{\textrm{orb}}=\sqrt{3}

[
FS89
, ISZ88-No.39]

[
YZ87
]
.

•

In fact, a CFT with
c=1
can be constructed by taking a quotient of the
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{1}
current algebra by its finite subgroup. There is a one-to-one correspondence between the finite subgroups of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
and the ADE Dynkin diagrams, which is called the
McKay correspondence
. The CFTs corresponding to
T

(E_{6})
,
O

(E_{7})
, and
I

(E_{8})
are isolated (does not allow deformation)
[
Pas87
, ISZ88-No.33]

[
Gin88b
, ISZ88-No.41]
.

R_{\mathrm{orb}}/\!\sqrt{2}

Figure 26:
Moduli space of 2d CFTs with
c=1
. The horizontal arrow expresses the so-called toroidal branch, and the vertical arrow does the orbifold branch.

R_{\mathrm{circ}}
is the radius of a circle and
R_{\mathrm{orb}}
is that of the orbifold.

SU(2)^{2}
is the symmetry that the theory has at the self-dual point.
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
,
\mathbf{D}_{k}
,
T
,
O
, and
I
are
the subgroups of the
SU(2)^{2}
at the corresponding points illustrated above.. For more details see
[
Gin88b
, ISZ88-No.41]
.

So far, only some orbifold examples are illustrated.
For general orbifold
\Gamma
we can do parallel computations as follows.
Let us consider a field in a torus coordinates
\Phi(\sigma,\tau)
, and for
g,h\in\Gamma
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(\sigma,\tau+2\pi)=g\cdot\Phi(\sigma,\tau),

\displaystyle\Phi(\sigma+2\pi,\tau)=h\cdot\Phi(\sigma,\tau).

(8.31)

Note that
g
and
h
are commutative, i.e
gh=hg
.
As we have seen before, the time-like orbifold condition (the first line above) is realized by operator insertions,
and the space-like orbifold condition (the second line above) is realized by twisted sectors (trace over twisted Hilbert space):

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{(g,h)}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\Tr_{\mathcal{H}_{h}}\left[gq^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\overline{q}^{\overline{L_{0}}-\frac{c}{24}}\right],

(8.32)

and the full partition function is given by the sum over all possible values of
g
and
h
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{M/\Gamma}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,h\in\Gamma\\
gh=hg\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Z}_{(g,h)}(\tau,\overline{\tau}),

(8.33)

where
|\Gamma|
is the degree of a discrete group
\Gamma
.
One should check that this formula reproduces our result of
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

8.3
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}
characters

It has been briefly mentioned in §
8.1
that
\widehat{\mathfrak{u}(1)}_{1}
theory has actually
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{1}
symmetry,
therefore, its partition function can be expressed by so-called
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{1}
character.
Here, we derive the
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{1}
character by considering a free boson on a circle with
R=\sqrt{2}
.

Recall that the theory can be characterized by three operators (for more details see §
7.2
):

\displaystyle J(z)=\frac{i\partial\varphi(z)}{\sqrt{2}},\qquad J^{\pm}(z)=:\!e^{\pm i\sqrt{2}\varphi(z)}\!:.

(8.34)

OPE calculation leads to the following commutation relations

\displaystyle[J^{3}_{n},J^{\pm}_{m}]=\pm J^{\pm}_{n+m},

\displaystyle[J^{+}_{n},J^{-}_{m}]=2J^{3}_{n+m}+n\delta_{n+m,0},

\displaystyle[L_{0},J^{a}_{n}]=-nJ^{a}_{n}\qquad(a=3,\pm).

(8.35)

Let us consider the highest weight representation of the theory.
Notice that
L_{0}
and
J^{3}_{0}
commutes, hence, the highest weight vector(states) should be characterized by their eigenvalues
h
and
j
,
and the vector is defined as follows.

\displaystyle J^{\pm}_{n}\ket{h,j}=J^{3}_{n}\ket{h,j}=0\qquad(n\geq 1),

\displaystyle J^{+}_{0}\ket{h,j}=\left(J^{-}_{0}\right)^{2j+1}\ket{h,j}=0,

\displaystyle J^{3}_{0}\ket{h,j}=j\ket{h,j}.

(8.36)

Note that from the argument of §
7.2

j
can only be
j=0,\frac{1}{2}
for
k=1
.
For
j=0
, the corresponding conformal dimension is
h=0
, and for
j=\frac{1}{2}
, that is
h=\frac{1}{4}
.
We can construct an integrable representation by acting the following operators to the highest weight vectors:

\displaystyle J^{-}_{n}\quad(n\leq 0),\qquad J^{3}_{m},\ J^{+}_{m}\quad(m<0).

(8.37)

We organize these creation operators in the following way:

\displaystyle J^{+}_{(n)}=J^{+}_{-n},\qquad J^{-}_{(n)}=J^{-}_{+n},\qquad J^{3}_{(n)}=J^{3}_{0}-\frac{n}{2}.

(8.38)

Notice that each
J^{a}_{(n)}
forms
\mathfrak{su}(2)
algebra (call them
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(n)}
).

Let us consider one
\ket{0,0}
of the highest vectors.
Since
J^{3}_{(0)}\ket{0,0}=0
the vector is singlet under the
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(0)}
.
On the other hand,
J^{3}_{(1)}\ket{0,0}=-\frac{1}{2}\ket{0,0}
,
J^{-}_{(1)}\ket{0,0}=0
, and hence,
it should be a spin-
\frac{1}{2}
representation of
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(1)}
.
Therefore, there is exactly one other states
J^{+}_{(1)}\ket{0,0}
.
Repeating similar procedures for
J^{+}_{(1)}\ket{0,0}
, and it turns that

J^{+}_{(1)}\ket{0,0}
is singlet under
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(2)}
but doublet under
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(3)}
.
In summary, we have a tower of
J^{+}_{(n)}
for odd
n
(let us set
n=2p-1
):

\displaystyle J^{+}_{(1)}J^{+}_{(3)}J^{+}_{(5)}\cdots J^{+}_{(2p-1)}\ket{0,0}:=\ket{p; 0,0},

\displaystyle L_{0}\ket{p; 0,0}=\sum_{m=1}^{p}(2m-1)\ket{p; 0,0}=p^{2}\ket{p; 0,0},

\displaystyle J^{3}_{0}\ket{p; 0,0}=p\ket{p; 0,0}.

(8.39)

We can also consider negative
n=-2p+1
(
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
), and the resultant tower is

\displaystyle J^{-}_{(-1)}J^{-}_{(-3)}J^{-}_{(-5)}\cdots J^{-}_{(-2p+1)}\ket{0,0}:=\ket{-p; 0,0},

\displaystyle L_{0}\ket{-p; 0,0}=\sum_{m=1}^{p}(2m-1)\ket{-p; 0,0}=p^{2}\ket{-p; 0,0},

\displaystyle J^{3}_{0}\ket{-p; 0,0}=-p\ket{-p; 0,0}.

(8.40)

Notice that the tower corresponds to vertex operators:

\displaystyle\ket{p; 0,0}\quad

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\quad:\!e^{+ip\sqrt{2}\varphi(z)}\!:,

\displaystyle\ket{-p; 0,0}\quad

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\quad:\!e^{-ip\sqrt{2}\varphi(z)}\!:,

\displaystyle\ket{0; 0,0}:=\ket{0,0}\quad

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\quad 1.

(8.41)

J^{3}_{0}
L_{0}
0
J^{+}_{(1)}
J^{+}_{(3)}
J^{+}_{(5)}
J^{-}_{(-1)}
J^{3}_{(-1)}
\begin{subarray}{c}J^{3}_{(-2)},\\
\left(J^{3}_{(-1)}\right)^{2}\end{subarray}
\begin{subarray}{c}J^{3}_{(-3)},\ \left(J^{3}_{(-1)}\right)^{3},\\
J^{3}_{(-2)}J^{3}_{(-1)}\end{subarray}
For each dot
1
2
3
-1
-2
-3
j=0
J^{3}_{0}
L_{0}
J^{-}_{(0)}
J^{-}_{(-2)}
J^{+}_{(2)}
J^{+}_{(4)}
0
1
2
3
-1
-2
-3
j=\frac{1}{2}

Figure 27:
Highest weight representation of
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{1}
.
For each dot, there must be degenerate vertical arrows with integer length, which express descendant fields.

The other highest weight vector
\ket{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}
is doublet under
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(0)}
.

\ket{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}
is singlet under
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(1)}
and doublet under
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(2)}
. On the other hand,
J^{-}_{(0)}\ket{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}
is singlet under
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(-1)}
and doublet under
\mathfrak{su}(2)_{(-2)}
.
Therefore, similar tower for
\ket{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}
is (
n=2q\geq 2
)

\displaystyle J^{+}_{(2)}J^{+}_{(4)}\cdots J^{+}_{(2q)}\ket{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}:=\ket{q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle L_{0}\ket{q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}=\left(\frac{1}{4}+\sum_{m=1}^{q}2m\right)\ket{q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}=\left(q+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\ket{q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle J^{3}_{0}\ket{q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}=\left(q+\frac{1}{2}\right)\ket{p; 0,0},

(8.42)

and for
J^{-}_{(0)}\ket{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}
(
n=-2q\leq 0
)

\displaystyle J^{-}_{(0)}J^{-}_{(-2)}J^{-}_{(-4)}\cdots J^{-}_{(-2q)}\ket{\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}:=\ket{-q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle L_{0}\ket{-q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}=\left(\frac{1}{4}+\sum_{m=1}^{q}2m\right)\ket{q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}=\left(q+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\ket{q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle J^{3}_{0}\ket{q; \frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}}=-\left(q+\frac{1}{2}\right)\ket{p; 0,0}.

(8.43)

Again, we can see corresponding vertex operators

\displaystyle\ket{q; \frac 14,\frac 12}\quad

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\quad:\!e^{+i\frac{2q-1}{\sqrt{2}}\varphi(z)}\!:,

\displaystyle\ket{-q;\frac 14,\frac 12}\quad

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\quad:\!e^{-i\frac{2q+1}{\sqrt{2}}\varphi(z)}\!:.

(8.44)

Recall that we still have
J^{3}_{-n}\ (n\geq 1)\sim\varphi^{(n)}=\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}\varphi(z)
,
which can freely act on each tower.
Consequently, the character of the spin-
j
highest weight representation of
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{1}

\displaystyle\chi_{2j}^{(1)}(\tau,z)=\Tr_{j}\left[q^{L_{0}-\frac{1}{24}}y^{J^{3}_{0}}\right],\quad(y=e^{2\pi iz})

(8.45)

can be read off

\displaystyle\chi_{0}^{(1)}(\tau,z)=\frac{\Theta_{0,1}(\tau,z)}{\eta(\tau)},

\displaystyle\chi_{1}^{(1)}(\tau,z)=\frac{\Theta_{1,1}(\tau,z)}{\eta(\tau)},

where

\Theta_{l,k}(\tau,z):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{l}{2k}}q^{kn^{2}}y^{kn}

reduces to the one
introduced in (
5.48
) at
z=0
.
It is now obvious that the
\eta
-function is coming from the action of
\varphi^{(n)}
(
n\geq 1
)
and the rest is from
:\!e^{in\sqrt{2}\varphi(z)}\!:
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}+j
).
See Fig.
27
for the summary.

In general, irreducible representations of
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}
require more careful treatment. Given a highest weight state
|h,j\rangle
in (
7.8
), one can obtain the Verma module
V(h,j)
by acting
J_{0}^{-}
and
J_{n}^{a}
for
n<0
. However, like the Virasoro modules considered in §
5.2
, the Verma module is generally not irreducible because it contains null states
|\chi\rangle
which satisfy

J_{0}^{+}|\chi\rangle=0,\qquad J_{n}^{a}|\chi\rangle=0,\quad n>0,\quad a=0,\pm~.

The module
J(h,j)
generated by the null states
|\chi\rangle
becomes a (maximal proper) submodule of
V(h,j)
. Subsequently, the quotient module

L(h,j):=V(h,j)/J(h,j)

becomes irreducible, which is called the
integrable representation
of highest weight
|h,j\rangle
. For a general affine Lie algebra
\widehat{}\mathfrak{g}_{k}
, one can similarly construct an irreducible module and its character is called the
Weyl-Kac character formula
. Referring the reader to
[
FMS97
, §14.4]
for the derivation, we just write the Weyl-Kac character formula of the integrable representation of highest weight
|h,\frac{l}{2}\rangle
for
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}
for
0\leq l\leq k

\chi_{l}^{(k)}(\tau,z)=\frac{\Theta_{l+1,k+2}(\tau,z)-\Theta_{-l-1,k+2}(\tau,z)}{\Theta_{1,2}(\tau,z)-\Theta_{-1,2}(\tau,z)}\,.

(8.46)

We can calculate the modular
S
-matrix for the character
(
8.46
) as

S^{(k)}_{ll^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{k+2}}\sin\bigg(
\frac{\pi}{k+2}
(l+1)(l'+1)
\bigg)\qquad\text{with}\quad l,l^{\prime}=0,\dots,k\,.

(8.47)

We now want to construct modular invariant partition
functions out of the characters (
8.46
).
This amounts to determining all matrices

M_{ll^{\prime}}
such that

\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}}(\tau,\overline{\tau})=\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}\chi_{l}^{(k)}(\tau)M^{(k)}_{ll^{\prime}}\overline{\chi_{l^{\prime}}}^{(k)}(\overline{\tau})\,.

(8.48)

is modular-invariant. The invariance under the
S
-transformation
requires

S^{(k)T}M^{(k)}S^{(k)*}=M^{(k)}\,.

(8.49)

S^{(k)}
in (
8.47
) is symmetric
and real, thus we find the condition

\commutator{M^{(k)}}{S^{(k)}}=0\,.

(8.50)

We have already encountered this relation in the minimal
model modular invariant formula (
5.45
).
There are further constraints for entries
M^{(k)}_{ll^{\prime}}
.
Since
M^{(k)}_{ll^{\prime}}
admits the interpretation
as the number of degeneracies of states in the Hilbert space,
they must be non-negative integers.
In addition, for the vacuum to only appear once, we have to
require
M^{(k)}_{00}=1
.
Furthermore, it turns out that in order for (
8.48
) to be invariant under

T
-transformations, one has to satisfy the level-matching
condition
h_{l}-\overline{h}_{l^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

ADE classification of modular invariant partition functions

Cappelli, Itzykson and Zuber have conjectured
[
CIZ87a
, ISZ88-No.26]
that all matrices
M
with properties above for
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}
characters are in one-to-one correspondence with the Dynkin diagrams of ADE types, which has been proven by themselves
[
CIZ87b
]
and Kato
[
Kat87
]
. The corresponding partition functions are
listed in Table
4
, which is
known as the ADE classification. The ADE classification appears in the minimal
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal field theories (
c<3
), which admits a geometric interpretation as Arnold’s singularities
[
LVW89
]
. This interpretation plays an important role in the discovery of
mirror symmetry
. (See Itzykson’s review in
[
JMT89
]
, and
[
CZ09
]
and the references therein.)

In fact, the classification of modular invariant partition functions of the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p}
attributes to the ADE classification of the modular invariant combinations of the
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}
characters. This stems from the fact that the coset model can describe the unitary minimal model
\mathcal{M}_{p}

\frac{\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{p-2}\oplus\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{1}}{\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{p-1}}~.

As an easy check, the central charge of the coset model can be computed by (
7.66
), yielding

c=1-\frac{6}{p(p+1)}~,

which is equal to (
5.39
).
We refer the reader to
[
FMS97
, §18.3]
for the details, and we just state the result in the following.
As seen in §
5.3
, the modular invariant partition functions of
\mathcal{M}_{p}

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{M}_{p}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq s\leq r\leq p-1\\
1\leq\widetilde{s}\leq\widetilde{r}\leq p-1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{N}_{(r,s),(\widetilde{r},\widetilde{s})}^{(p)}\chi_{(r,s)}\overline{\chi_{(\widetilde{r},\widetilde{s})}}

are combinations of the Rocha-Caridi character
\chi_{r,s}
in (
5.49
). This partition function turns out to be written in terms of two modular invariant partition combinations of the
\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{k}
characters

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{p-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r,\widetilde{r}=1}^{p-1}M_{r,\widetilde{r}}^{(p-1)}\chi_{r-1}^{(p-1)}\overline{\chi_{\widetilde{r}-1}^{(p-1)}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{su}(2)}_{p-2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s,\widetilde{s}=1}^{p-2}M_{s,\widetilde{s}}^{(p-2)}\chi_{s-1}^{(p-2)}\overline{\chi_{\widetilde{s}-1}^{(p-2)}}~,

via

\mathcal{N}_{(r,s),(\widetilde{r},\widetilde{s})}^{(p)}=M_{r,\widetilde{r}}^{(p-1)}M_{s,\widetilde{s}}^{(p-2)}~.

Hence, the modular invariant partition functions of
\mathcal{M}_{p}
can be specified by a pair of ADE Dynkin diagrams with adjacent levels as in Table
5
. Note that an
A
-series is always present in a pair. In particular, the partition function (
5.51
) of diagonal type is of
(A_{p-1},A_{p})
type whereas the non-diagonal partition function (
5.95
) of the 3-state Potts model is of
(A_{4},D_{4})
type.
